Chapter 1 · Week 5YARN, file formats and compression

How a shared Hadoop cluster hands out CPU and RAM to many jobs (YARN), and how the way data is laid out and compressed on disk decides how much of it a query has to read. Part 2 tests the same ideas on LinkedIn's 5,000-node YARN cluster.

Exam question pool 19 questions from the slides · click a question for its answer

MCQ
Q1. Which component in YARN is responsible for global resource allocation across the entire cluster?
  • (a) NodeManager
  • (b) ApplicationMaster
  • (c) ResourceManager
  • (d) NameNode
show answer
(c) ResourceManager
One RM per cluster allocates containers globally; the AM manages only its own job.
MCQ
Q2. Which Hadoop file format stores data in columnar format and is best suited for analytical, read-heavy workloads?
  • (a) Avro
  • (b) Text/CSV
  • (c) Parquet
  • (d) JSON
show answer
(c) Parquet
Columnar, with min/max stats for predicate pushdown; Avro, CSV and JSON are row formats. ORC is also columnar: the instructor accepts it as partly correct with a justification (best for Hive), but Parquet is the broader standard.
MCQ
Q3. What does “splittable” mean for a file format in Hadoop?
  • (a) The file can be deleted in parts by HDFS
  • (b) Multiple Mappers can process different parts of the file simultaneously
  • (c) The file format supports splitting schema across multiple NameNodes
  • (d) The file can be distributed across multiple data centres
show answer
(b) Multiple Mappers can process different parts of the file simultaneously
Reading can start anywhere in the file, which also allows data-local tasks.
MCQ
Q4. YARN was introduced in which major Hadoop version, and what did it replace?
  • (a) Hadoop 1.0; replaced the NameNode
  • (b) Hadoop 2.0; replaced the monolithic JobTracker with a decoupled ResourceManager + ApplicationMaster
  • (c) Hadoop 3.0; replaced DataNodes with Containers
  • (d) Hadoop 2.0; replaced the Secondary NameNode with a Standby NameNode
show answer
(b) Hadoop 2.0; replaced the monolithic JobTracker with a decoupled ResourceManager + ApplicationMaster
(d) is the trap: the Standby NameNode is HDFS, not YARN.
True/False
1. Parquet is a row-oriented file format optimised for write-heavy workloads.
show answer
False. Parquet is column-oriented and optimised for read-heavy analytics; the row-oriented, append-friendly format is Avro.
True/False
2. YARN allows multiple computation frameworks such as Spark and Tez to share resources on the same Hadoop cluster.
show answer
True. Each framework brings its own ApplicationMaster, so YARN runs MapReduce, Spark and Tez on one cluster.
True/False
3. Gzip-compressed files are natively splittable in Hadoop, allowing multiple Mappers to process them in parallel.
show answer
False. Gzip files are not splittable, so one Mapper reads the whole file; use bzip2 or Parquet + Snappy for parallel reads.
True/False
4. ORC format is specifically optimised for Hive workloads and achieves among the highest compression ratios of any Hadoop format.
show answer
True. ORC is columnar, best for Hive, with the highest compression, Hive ACID and vectorized reads.
Descriptive
D1. Explain YARN’s three-component architecture — ResourceManager, NodeManager, and ApplicationMaster. Draw a diagram showing how they interact when a Spark job is submitted to the cluster.
show answer

ResourceManager (1 per cluster): Scheduler grants containers; ApplicationsManager accepts jobs and starts each AM.
NodeManager (1 per node): launches and monitors containers, heartbeats to the RM, enforces CPU/RAM limits.
ApplicationMaster (1 per job): requests containers, has NMs launch tasks, retries failures, releases containers.

Diagram (see the diagram in the YARN and its three components card): Client, RM, Spark AM, NM1–NM3, with numbered arrows:

  1. Client submits the job to the RM.
  2. RM launches the Spark AM in a container.
  3. AM registers with the RM and requests containers; the RM Scheduler grants them.
  4. AM tells NM1–NM3 to launch executors.
  5. Executors report status to the AM.
  6. AM unregisters from the RM when done.

Example: the Spark AM asks for 100 containers {4 cores, 8 GB}; the busy RM grants 80 and the AM runs executors in them.

Descriptive
D2. What is columnar storage? Why does it improve performance for analytics queries that select only a subset of columns? Use a concrete example with numbers to illustrate the I/O reduction.
show answer

Columnar storage keeps all values of one column together on disk (Parquet, ORC); a row store (CSV, Avro) writes whole records one after another.

Why faster: the query reads only the columns it names; Parquet's min/max stats skip whole row groups (predicate pushdown); same-type values compress better.

Example: 100 columns, 10 TB, query on 3 columns.

FormatData read
CSV10 TB
Parquet10 × 3/100 = 0.3 TB = 300 GB

I/O cut 97%, about 33× faster.

Descriptive
D3. Compare Avro and Parquet. Explain what “schema evolution” means and why Avro handles it better. In which scenarios would you use each?
show answer
AvroParquet
Layoutrowcolumnar
Strengthschema evolution, appendsanalytic reads, pushdown
CodecSnappy/DeflateSnappy/Gzip

Schema evolution: the schema changes over time (e.g. a new field) while old files and readers keep working.

Why Avro handles it: the writer schema is stored with the data, the reader supplies its own schema, fields match by name; missing fields get defaults, unknown fields are ignored. Backward and forward compatible.

Use: Avro for Kafka → HDFS ingest and data exchange; Parquet for Spark, Presto and ML analytics.

Analytical
A1. Data Lake Performance Migration: A Dhaka-based telecom company stores 10 TB of daily call-detail records (CDR) on HDFS as gzip-compressed CSV files. Their data engineers report that a Hive query selecting 4 of 80 columns takes 6 hours. – Identify two specific reasons why this configuration is slow – Propose a migration strategy: new format, compression codec, and partitioning scheme – Estimate the expected I/O reduction (show your calculation) – What YARN scheduler would you use and why, given that multiple teams share the cluster?
show answer

i) Why slow

  1. Gzip is not splittable: one Mapper per file, the cluster idles.
  2. CSV is a row store: reading 4 columns parses all 80; no stats to skip data.

ii) Migration: ORC (Hive) or Parquet (if Spark reads it too); Snappy for hot data (Zlib for ORC in Hive, Zstd for archive); partition by date and region, e.g. dt=YYYY-MM-DD/region=; convert once, then nightly.

iii) I/O estimate

Fraction read4/80 = 0.05
Data read10 TB × 0.05 = 500 GB
Reduction95% (20× less)
Time if I/O-bound360 min × 0.05 ≈ 18 min

iv) Scheduler: Capacity: guaranteed queues per team (ETL, analysts, data science), idle capacity borrowed, so no team starves another. FIFO lets one long job block all.

Analytical
A2. YARN Failure Trace: A NodeManager (NM2) hosting 5 running containers for a MapReduce job fails mid-execution. Trace the complete failure recovery sequence: which components detect the failure, in what order, what state changes occur in the ResourceManager, and how are the 5 containers’ tasks restarted.
show answer
  1. NM2 crashes; its 5 containers die; heartbeats stop.
  2. RM detects the missed heartbeats after the timeout (>10 min) and declares NM2 lost.
  3. RM state: NM2 removed from schedulable capacity; its 5 containers marked failed.
  4. RM notifies the MapReduce AM.
  5. AM requests 5 replacement containers.
  6. RM grants them on healthy nodes (e.g. NM1, NM3), preferring nodes with HDFS replicas of the input.
  7. AM has those NMs relaunch the 5 tasks from their input splits; finished maps whose output was on NM2's disk may rerun.

If the AM was on NM2, the RM restarts it (default 2 attempts); MapReduce recovers state from HDFS checkpoints.

Discussion
L1. Resource consolidation increases utilisation: merging 3 siloed clusters into one raised average utilisation from 45% to 78% — the same capacity served 73% more workload
show answer

Claim: one shared cluster uses hardware better than three silos.

For: LinkedIn's silos (MapReduce, Spark, Samza) were idle at different times; merged, utilisation rose 45% → 78%, and 78/45 = 1.73, so 73% more work on the same hardware. No cross-cluster copying, one config instead of three.

Against: workloads now compete, so queues, pre-emption and RM high availability become essential.

Discussion
L2. Capacity Scheduler enables SLAs: production queues guaranteed minimum resources; analyst adhoc queries cannot starve revenue-generating pipelines
show answer

Claim: guaranteed queues protect SLAs (promised finish times).

For: production holds 40% at LinkedIn, so the 10% adhoc queue cannot delay revenue pipelines, while idle capacity is still lent out.

Against: fixed percentages need tuning, and analysts may wait at busy times; Fair gives short jobs faster turnaround.

Discussion
L3. Pre-emption needs tuning: aggressive pre-emption killed long-running Spark jobs; LinkedIn set 30-second grace periods and added Spark checkpoint-on-preempt hooks
show answer

Claim: pre-emption is needed but must match the workload.

For: without reclaiming borrowed containers, queue guarantees are empty promises.

Against: killing a container wastes its work; long Spark jobs restarted repeatedly. LinkedIn's fix: a 30-second grace period and checkpoint-on-preempt hooks so jobs resume.

Discussion
L4. YARN is the OS; frameworks are processes: the paradigm shift — the cluster is the computer, YARN is its kernel
show answer

Claim: YARN is to a cluster what an OS kernel is to a computer.

For: the RM allocates CPU and RAM as containers, NMs isolate them with cgroups, and frameworks are just applications with their own AM; the same three-tier design lives on in Kubernetes.

Against: YARN sizes containers only by CPU and RAM, relies on HDFS for storage, and Kubernetes has replaced it for most new deployments.

Discussion
L5. Samza on YARN: stream processing is just another set of long-running containers; YARN’s fault tolerance restarts failed Samza tasks
show answer

Claim: a stream job is just long-running containers.

For: Samza shares the cluster in its 15% queue, no separate stream cluster, and YARN restarts failed tasks on other nodes.

Against: the containers hold capacity permanently, and the >10 min NodeManager timeout is slow for real-time data, so Samza must resume from the right Kafka offset itself.

Live Exercise
LE. Task (20 minutes, pairs) Model LinkedIn’s 5,000-node YARN cluster in Python. Given: • 5,000 nodes; each: 64 GB RAM, 16 vCPUs • Capacity Scheduler: production 40%, spark 25%, samza 15%, adhoc 10%, reserve 10% • Container sizes: Hive mapper: {4 GB, 2 vCPU}; Spark executor: {8 GB, 4 vCPU}; Samza task: {2 GB, 1 vCPU} Questions: 1 Max concurrent containers per queue? 2 Which resource is the bottleneck (CPU or RAM)? 3 If production needs 10,000 Hive mappers, how many nodes are required?
NODES    = 5_000
RAM_GB   = 64
VCPUS    = 16

queues = {
  "production": 0.40,
  "spark":      0.25,
  "samza":      0.15,
  "adhoc":      0.10,
  "reserve":    0.10,
}
containers = {
  "production": {"ram": 4, "cpu": 2},
  "spark":      {"ram": 8, "cpu": 4},
  "samza":      {"ram": 2, "cpu": 1},
  "adhoc":      {"ram": 4, "cpu": 2},
}

for q, frac in queues.items():
    nodes_q = NODES * frac
    ram_q    = nodes_q * RAM_GB
    cpu_q    = nodes_q * VCPUS
    if q in containers:
        c = containers[q]
        by_ram = ram_q // c["ram"]
        by_cpu = cpu_q // c["cpu"]
        max_c = int(min(by_ram, by_cpu))
        bottleneck = ("RAM" if by_ram < by_cpu
                             else "CPU")
        print(f"{q}: {max_c} containers "
              f"(bottleneck: {bottleneck})")
show answer

Task: run the skeleton; add Q3:

import math
c = containers["production"]
per_node = min(RAM_GB // c["ram"], VCPUS // c["cpu"])
print(per_node, math.ceil(10_000 / per_node))

Expected output:

production: 16000 containers (bottleneck: CPU)
spark: 5000 containers (bottleneck: CPU)
samza: 12000 containers (bottleneck: CPU)
adhoc: 4000 containers (bottleneck: CPU)
8 1250

1. production 16,000; spark 5,000; samza 12,000; adhoc 4,000; reserve has no container size, so none.

2. CPU. A node has 64/16 = 4 GB per vCPU; every container asks 2 GB per vCPU, so vCPUs run out with half the RAM idle.

3. Per node min(64/4, 16/2) = 8 mappers; 10,000 / 8 = 1,250 nodes, which fits in production's 2,000.

Topic cards 6 topics · everything the pool tests

YARN and its three components

Facts
  • YARN (Yet Another Resource Negotiator) arrived in Hadoop 2.0 (2013) and replaced the Hadoop 1.x JobTracker.
  • Hadoop 1.x JobTracker did resource management and job scheduling in one process: MapReduce only, ceiling about 4,000 nodes.
  • ResourceManager (RM): one per cluster, on a master node. Two parts: Scheduler (gives out containers) and ApplicationsManager (accepts jobs, starts each AM).
  • NodeManager (NM): one per worker node. Launches and monitors containers, sends heartbeats (regular I-am-alive reports) to the RM.
  • ApplicationMaster (AM): one per running job. Asks the RM for containers and tells NMs to start tasks in them.
  • Container: a slice of one node, {vcores: N, memory: M MB}. Replaced Hadoop 1's fixed slots. Ancestor of the Kubernetes Pod.
How it works
  1. Client submits the job to the ResourceManager.
  2. The RM's ApplicationsManager launches a container for the job's ApplicationMaster.
  3. The AM registers with the RM and requests containers (e.g. 100 of 4 cores, 8 GB).
  4. The RM's Scheduler grants what is free (e.g. 80) and picks the nodes.
  5. The AM tells those NodeManagers to launch tasks in the containers.
  6. Tasks report status to the AM; NMs heartbeat node health to the RM.
  7. When all tasks finish, the AM releases containers and unregisters from the RM.
Why · pros · limits
  • Splitting resource management (RM) from per-job logic (AM) removed the single JobTracker bottleneck: YARN scales past 10,000 nodes.
  • The AM holds the framework-specific logic, so YARN is framework-agnostic: MapReduce, Spark, Tez and Samza share one cluster.
  • Containers are flexible in size, unlike fixed slots, so CPU-heavy and RAM-heavy tasks both fit.
  • NMs isolate containers with Linux cgroups (kernel CPU limits) and memory limits; a container over its memory limit is killed.
  • Limit: containers are sized only by CPU and RAM. Kubernetes has largely replaced YARN for new deployments.
Compare
Hadoop 1.xYARN (Hadoop 2.x)
Who manages resourcesJobTracker (also schedules jobs)ResourceManager only
Per-job managerinside the JobTrackerone ApplicationMaster process per job
FrameworksMapReduce onlyany (Spark, Tez, Samza ...)
Resource unitfixed slotflexible container
Max nodesabout 4,000over 10,000
RM high availabilitynoyes (ZooKeeper)
How manyJob
ResourceManager1 per clusterglobal allocation of containers
NodeManager1 per nodelaunch and watch containers, heartbeat
ApplicationMaster1 per applicationrequest containers, run and retry tasks
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
one process decides which application gets resources across the whole clusterResourceManager (its Scheduler)
an agent on each machine starts tasks and reports health every few secondsNodeManager
a Spark job asks for 100 executors and gets 80ApplicationMaster requests, RM Scheduler grants
a company wants Spark and MapReduce on one cluster but runs Hadoop 1.xmove to YARN (JobTracker is MapReduce-only)
a bundle of 4 vcores and 8 GB on one nodecontainer
Traps
  • NameNode and DataNode are HDFS (storage). They are not YARN parts and do not allocate CPU or RAM.
  • The AM is per job, the RM is per cluster. Q1's wrong favourite is the AM.
  • Hadoop 2.0 also added the Standby NameNode. That is HDFS high availability, not YARN (Q4 distractor).
  • The AM requests containers; it does not grant them. Only the RM's Scheduler grants.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
YARN is Hadoop 2's resource manager: one ResourceManager gives out containers, a NodeManager runs them on each node, and one ApplicationMaster manages each job.
3 marks
YARN split the Hadoop 1.x JobTracker into a global ResourceManager and a per-job ApplicationMaster. NodeManagers on each worker launch containers (CPU + RAM slices). Example: a Spark AM asks for 100 containers of 4 cores, 8 GB; the busy RM grants 80.
5 marks

Definition. YARN (Hadoop 2.0, 2013) separates cluster resource management from job execution.

Structure. ResourceManager: one per cluster, Scheduler plus ApplicationsManager. NodeManager: one per node, launches containers and heartbeats to the RM. ApplicationMaster: one per job, requests containers and runs tasks in them.

Flow. Submit to RM, RM starts AM, AM requests containers, RM grants, NMs launch tasks, AM unregisters at the end.

Example. A Spark AM asks for 100 containers of 4 cores, 8 GB; the RM grants 80.

Limitation. containers are sized only by CPU and RAM; Kubernetes has taken over most new deployments.

MCQ

  • (a) ResourceManager
  • (b) NodeManager
  • (c) NameNode
  • (d) ApplicationMaster
show answer
(d) ApplicationMaster
Each job gets its own AM; the RM is one per cluster, the NM one per node.

show answer
JobTracker; 4,000
Define / list
Define a YARN container and list the three YARN components.
show answer
A container is a slice of one node's capacity: a set number of vcores plus a set amount of RAM, in which a task runs. Components: ResourceManager, NodeManager, ApplicationMaster.
Explain the steps
List the steps from submitting a Spark job on YARN to its completion.
show answer
Client submits to RM; RM launches the AM container; AM registers and requests containers; RM Scheduler grants them; AM tells NMs to launch executors; tasks report status to the AM; AM releases containers and unregisters.
Why / justify
Why can Spark, Tez and Samza share a YARN cluster but could not share a Hadoop 1.x cluster?
show answer
In YARN the framework logic sits in each job's own ApplicationMaster and the RM only hands out generic containers. In Hadoop 1.x the JobTracker's scheduling was written for MapReduce only.
Distinguish
Distinguish ResourceManager and ApplicationMaster.
show answer
RM: one per cluster, decides which job gets which containers across the cluster. AM: one per job, asks the RM for containers, starts tasks in them, retries failed tasks.

show answer
i) No, the JobTracker runs MapReduce only. ii) Hadoop 2.0 with YARN. iii) The Spark ApplicationMaster. iv) The container (vcores + RAM).

show answer
Use the 5-mark answer, then add the Hadoop 1.x vs YARN table: JobTracker vs RM + AM, MapReduce only vs any framework, slot vs container, about 4,000 vs over 10,000 nodes, no RM HA vs ZooKeeper HA.

YARN fault tolerance

Facts
  • YARN handles failure in three layers: NodeManager, ApplicationMaster, ResourceManager.
  • NM failure: RM sees missing heartbeats (timeout over 10 minutes) and marks all containers on that node failed.
  • AM failure: RM restarts the AM, up to yarn.resourcemanager.am.max-attempts (default 2).
  • A restarted AM can recover job state from HDFS checkpoints if the framework supports it: MapReduce yes, early Spark no.
  • RM failure: RM High Availability (YARN 2.4+) uses ZooKeeper with an Active and a Standby RM; failover in under 60 seconds.
How it works
  1. A NodeManager crashes; its containers die and its heartbeats stop.
  2. The RM waits out the heartbeat timeout (over 10 minutes) and declares the node lost.
  3. The RM removes the node's CPU and RAM from the schedulable pool and marks its containers failed.
  4. The RM notifies the job's ApplicationMaster.
  5. The AM requests the same number of replacement containers.
  6. The RM grants them on healthy nodes, ideally ones holding HDFS replicas of the input.
  7. The AM has the new NMs relaunch the tasks; map tasks rerun from the start of their input split.
Why · pros · limits
  • Fault handling is layered: YARN detects node loss, but the AM decides how to retry, because only it knows the framework.
  • Input survives in HDFS replicas, so a failed task can rerun on another node.
  • Finished map output stored on the dead node's local disk is lost too, so those maps may rerun.
  • Limit: a 10-minute heartbeat timeout is slow for streaming jobs; the framework must recover faster itself.
  • Samza streams on YARN are long-running containers, so YARN's restarts give them fault tolerance without a separate stream cluster.
Compare
FailureDetected by / howRecovery
NodeManagerRM, missed heartbeats (>10 min)containers marked failed; AM re-requests elsewhere
ApplicationMasterRMRM restarts AM (default max 2 attempts); state from HDFS checkpoint if supported
ResourceManagerZooKeeperStandby RM becomes Active in <60 s (YARN 2.4+)
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a worker machine stops reporting and its tasks reappear on other machinesNodeManager failure, heartbeat timeout, AM re-requests containers
the job's coordinator crashes and the RM starts a new one, within a set limit of attemptsAM failure, restarted up to am.max-attempts (default 2)
the master node dies and a backup takes over within a minuteRM High Availability via ZooKeeper
a stream processor runs in containers that never finish and is restarted on failureSamza on YARN (long-running containers)
Traps
  • The AM, not the RM, re-requests the replacement containers. The RM only detects and marks.
  • The NM timeout is over 10 minutes, not seconds. The 60 seconds figure is RM failover.
  • Recovery from checkpoint depends on the framework: MapReduce can, early Spark could not.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
When a NodeManager stops heartbeating, the RM marks its containers failed and the AM requests replacements on other nodes.
3 marks
YARN handles NM, AM and RM failure separately. A dead NM is found by missed heartbeats (over 10 minutes); its containers are marked failed and the AM re-requests them elsewhere. A dead AM is restarted (default 2 attempts); a dead RM fails over to a Standby via ZooKeeper in under 60 s.
5 marks

Definition. YARN recovers from failure at three layers: node, application, master.

Steps for a node. NM dies, heartbeats stop; RM times out (>10 min), marks the node lost and its containers failed; RM tells the AM; AM requests replacements; RM grants them on healthy nodes; tasks rerun from their HDFS input.

AM and RM. AM restarted by the RM (default max 2 attempts), recovering from HDFS checkpoints if the framework supports it; RM HA through ZooKeeper, failover <60 s.

Example. NM2 with 5 MapReduce containers fails; 5 new containers start on NM1 and NM3.

Limitation. The long timeout delays recovery.

Schedulers and capacity planning

Facts
  • The RM Scheduler is pluggable with three policies: FIFO, Capacity, Fair.
  • FIFO (first in, first out): one queue in submission order; one long job blocks the rest.
  • Capacity: queues with guaranteed shares (e.g. Engineering 60%, Finance 40%); idle capacity is borrowed. Default in HDP/CDH; used by Yahoo!, LinkedIn.
  • Fair: running jobs get equal shares, so short jobs finish fast. Used by Facebook, Twitter.
  • DRF (Dominant Resource Fairness): fairness across CPU and RAM together, for clusters mixing CPU-heavy and RAM-heavy jobs.
  • Pre-emption: taking borrowed containers back so a queue gets its guarantee. LinkedIn gave a 30-second warning.
How it works
  1. Give each queue its share of nodes: nodes × share.
  2. Turn the queue's nodes into total RAM and total vCPUs.
  3. Divide total RAM by the container's RAM: containers by RAM.
  4. Divide total vCPUs by the container's vCPUs: containers by CPU.
  5. The smaller count is the maximum, since a container needs both.
  6. The resource giving the smaller count is the bottleneck.
Why · pros · limits
  • Capacity guarantees protect SLAs (service level agreements, e.g. ETL done by 6 a.m.): analyst queries cannot starve production pipelines.
  • Consolidation: LinkedIn merged 3 single-workload clusters into one 5,000-node YARN cluster; utilisation rose from 45% to 78%, 73% more work on the same hardware.
  • Borrowing only works if capacity can be reclaimed, so Capacity needs pre-emption.
  • Aggressive pre-emption killed long Spark jobs; the fix was a 30-second grace period and checkpoint-on-preempt hooks.
  • Fixed queue percentages need regular tuning when workloads change.
Compare
PolicyGood for / weakness
FIFOone queue, submission ordersimple; one long job starves the rest
Capacityqueues with guaranteed %, borrow idle capacitymulti-team SLAs; needs pre-emption and tuning
Fairequal share to all running jobsmany short jobs; weaker guarantees for one team
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
several teams share a cluster and production must always get at least 40%Capacity Scheduler
a single huge job submitted first makes everyone else waitFIFO weakness
many short interactive queries should finish fast even while a big job runsFair Scheduler
jobs differ in their CPU and RAM needs and fairness must cover bothDRF
a queue gets its lent capacity back by killing containers after a warningpre-emption with a grace period
Calculation

Live exercise (P2 slide 23): 5,000 nodes, each 64 GB and 16 vCPU. Queues production 40%, spark 25%, samza 15%, adhoc 10%, reserve 10%. Containers: Hive mapper {4 GB, 2 vCPU}, Spark executor {8 GB, 4 vCPU}, Samza task {2 GB, 1 vCPU}; adhoc uses the Hive mapper size.

QueueNodesRAM GBvCPUBy RAMBy CPUMax
production2,000128,00032,00032,00016,00016,000
spark1,25080,00020,00010,0005,0005,000
samza75048,00012,00024,00012,00012,000
adhoc50032,0008,0008,0004,0004,000

Bottleneck: CPU everywhere. Nodes for 10,000 Hive mappers: 1,250.

Step by step

  1. Given 5,000 nodes and spark share 25% -> spark nodes = 5,000 × 0.25 = 1,250.
  2. Given 1,250 nodes of 64 GB -> RAM = 80,000 GB; of 16 vCPU -> vCPU = 20,000.
  3. Given 80,000 GB and 8 GB executors -> by RAM = 10,000.
  4. Given 20,000 vCPU and 4-vCPU executors -> by CPU = 5,000.
  5. Given by RAM 10,000 and by CPU 5,000 -> max = 5,000, bottleneck CPU.
  6. Given a node of 64 GB / 16 vCPU and a mapper of 4 GB / 2 vCPU -> per node = min(16, 8) = 8.
  7. Given 8 mappers per node and 10,000 mappers -> nodes = 10,000 / 8 = 1,250 (production has 2,000, so it fits).

Variants

  • Quick bottleneck test: node RAM per vCPU = 64/16 = 4 GB; every container asks 2 GB per vCPU, so CPU runs out first with half the RAM idle.
  • 2,000 nodes of 128 GB / 32 vCPU, spark 25%: 500 nodes, 64,000 GB, 16,000 vCPU; by RAM 8,000, by CPU 4,000; max 4,000 (CPU).
  • 3,000 Spark executors {8 GB, 4 vCPU} on 64 GB / 16 vCPU nodes: per node min(8, 4) = 4; nodes = 750.
  • Utilisation 45% to 78%: 78 / 45 = 1.73, so 73% more work on the same hardware.
Traps
  • Take the minimum of the by-RAM and by-CPU counts, never the RAM count alone.
  • The reserve queue has no container size, so it gets no count.
  • Capacity is not a hard cap: idle capacity is lent out and reclaimed by pre-emption.
  • 73% is 78/45 − 1, not 78 − 45 = 33 points.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
The Capacity Scheduler splits the cluster into queues with guaranteed shares and lets queues borrow idle capacity.
3 marks
YARN's Scheduler is pluggable: FIFO runs jobs in order, Capacity guarantees each queue a share, Fair gives running jobs equal shares. A queue's maximum containers = min(total RAM / container RAM, total vCPU / container vCPU). Example: spark queue of 1,250 nodes fits min(10,000, 5,000) = 5,000 executors.
5 marks

Definition. The RM Scheduler decides which queue or job gets containers.

Policies. FIFO: submission order. Capacity: guaranteed queue shares, idle capacity borrowed, reclaimed by pre-emption. Fair: equal shares. DRF extends fairness to CPU and RAM together.

Example. 5,000 nodes of 64 GB / 16 vCPU, production 40% = 2,000 nodes: by RAM 32,000, by CPU 16,000 Hive mappers, so 16,000 (CPU bottleneck). LinkedIn's shared cluster went from 45% to 78% utilisation.

Limitation. Aggressive pre-emption kills long jobs; LinkedIn added a 30-second grace period and checkpoints.

show answer

Row vs columnar storage and Parquet

Facts
  • Row store (CSV, JSON, Avro): one whole record after another on disk.
  • Columnar store (Parquet, ORC): all values of one column stored together.
  • Slide example: 100-column, 10 TB table, 3-column query: CSV reads 10 TB, Parquet about 300 GB, 33× faster, 97% less I/O.
  • Parquet layout: file header, row groups (about 128 MB), each split into column chunks, each into pages; footer holds metadata and stats.
  • The footer stores min/max per column chunk, which enables predicate pushdown: skipping row groups a filter cannot match.
  • Spark pushes down by default: spark.sql.parquet.filterPushdown=true. A 10 TB Parquet query may read only 50 GB.
How it works
  1. The query, e.g. WHERE score > 90, first reads only the file footer.
  2. It gets min/max of the score column for every row group.
  3. Row group 1 has score in [60, 85]: max 85 is not above 90, so skip it with no I/O.
  4. Row group 2 has score in [85, 99]: it may match, so read it.
  5. Inside row group 2, read only the score column chunk, not the other columns.
Why · pros · limits
  • Analytics queries touch few columns of many rows; a column store reads only those columns.
  • Same-type values sit together, so they compress better.
  • Min/max stats let whole row groups be skipped before any data is read.
  • Row groups are independent, so each can go to its own Mapper (splittable).
  • Limit: writing a single record is awkward, so row formats (Avro) are better for streaming appends.
Compare
Row store (CSV, Avro)Columnar (Parquet, ORC)
Disk layoutrecord by recordcolumn by column
SELECT score FROM usersreads id, name, age, scorereads score only
Filter skippingnone in CSVrow groups skipped by min/max
Compressionlowerhigher (similar values together)
Best forwrites, appends, exchangeread-heavy analytics
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a query needs 3 of 100 columns and the files are CSVswitch to a columnar format
a filter skips whole blocks using stored min and max valuespredicate pushdown
a footer lists stats for each chunk of each columnParquet footer / column chunk statistics
a 10 TB table is queried but only 50 GB is read from diskcolumnar layout plus predicate pushdown
Calculation

Slide (P1 slides 6, 16): D = 10 TB, N = 100 columns, k = 3 read.

  • Read = D × k / N = 10 TB × 3/100 = 0.3 TB = 300 GB
  • I/O reduction = 1 − 3/100 = 97%
  • Speedup ≈ N / k = 100/3 ≈ 33×

Pushdown (slide 23): 10 TB on disk, 50 GB read: 50/10,000 = 0.005, so 99.5% less, 200× faster.

Step by step

  1. Given k = 4 of N = 80 columns -> fraction read = 4/80 = 0.05.
  2. Given fraction 0.05 and D = 10 TB -> read = 0.5 TB = 500 GB.
  3. Given fraction 0.05 -> reduction = 1 − 0.05 = 95%; speedup = 80/4 = 20×.
  4. Given 6 h I/O-bound and fraction 0.05 -> new time ≈ 360 × 0.05 = 18 min.
  5. Given row group min/max [60, 85] and filter score > 90 -> max 85 < 90, skip.

Variants

  • D = 20 TB, N = 200, k = 5: read 0.5 TB, reduction 97.5%, speedup 40×.
  • 2 TB on disk, 40 GB read after pushdown: 40/2,000 = 0.02, reduction 98%, speedup 50×.
  • Filter score < 70 with RG1 [60, 85], RG2 [85, 99]: read RG1 (min 60 < 70), skip RG2 (min 85).
Traps
  • Avro is a row format even though it is binary and self-describing.
  • The 33× assumes columns of equal size and an I/O-bound query; it is an estimate.
  • Skip a row group only when its range cannot match: for > 90 look at the max, for < 70 look at the min.
  • Pushdown is per row group; the whole file is not skipped unless every row group fails.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Columnar storage keeps each column's values together on disk, so a query reads only the columns it needs.
3 marks
A row store must read every column of every row; a columnar store (Parquet, ORC) reads only the selected columns. Data read = D × k / N. Example: 3 of 100 columns of 10 TB reads 300 GB, a 97% cut and about 33× faster.
5 marks

Definition. Columnar storage writes all values of a column together; Parquet and ORC do this.

Structure. Parquet: row groups (about 128 MB), column chunks, pages; footer with min/max per chunk.

Steps. Read the footer, skip row groups whose range cannot match (predicate pushdown), read only needed column chunks.

Example. 100 columns, 10 TB, query on 3: CSV reads 10 TB, Parquet about 300 GB, 33× faster.

Limitation. Poor for record-by-record writes; use Avro for streaming ingest.

Practise this topic in 10 formats

  • (c) gzip block boundaries

show answer
(a) min/max statistics in the file footer

Fill in the blank

show answer

Define / list
Define predicate pushdown.
show answer
Applying a query's filter using stored min/max stats, so row groups that cannot match are skipped without being read.
Explain the steps

show answer

Why / justify

show answer
Each column is stored separately, so unread columns are physically skipped; I/O falls to roughly k/N of the table.
Distinguish
Distinguish row and columnar storage.
show answer
Row: whole records stored one after another; reading one column reads all. Columnar: each column stored together; a query reads only its columns. Row suits writes, columnar suits analytics.
Scenario

show answer
i) Columnar. ii) Parquet (or ORC). iii) 5 TB × 2/50 = 0.2 TB = 200 GB. iv) Predicate pushdown on row group min/max of price.

File formats: Avro, Parquet, ORC

Facts
  • Five formats: Text/CSV (row, external schema), JSON (row, not splittable raw), Avro (row), Parquet (columnar), ORC (columnar).
  • Avro, Parquet and ORC are self-describing: the schema (field names and types) travels inside the file. Avro's schema is JSON.
  • Avro: Snappy/Deflate; best for schema evolution and Kafka → HDFS ingest.
  • Parquet: Snappy/Gzip; best for Spark, Presto, ML pipelines.
  • ORC: Zlib/Snappy; best for Hive, highest compression, Hive ACID and vectorized reads.
  • Schema evolution: the schema changes (e.g. a new field) and old files and old readers keep working.
How it works
  1. Avro writes the writer schema into the file with the data.
  2. A reader brings its own reader schema.
  3. Avro matches fields by name between the two schemas.
  4. A field the reader expects but the file lacks gets its default value.
  5. A field in the file that the reader does not know is ignored.
  6. So new readers read old files (backward) and old readers read new files (forward).
Why · pros · limits
  • Avro is row-oriented, so appending one event at a time is cheap: good for streams.
  • Reader/writer schema matching means producers can add fields without breaking consumers.
  • Parquet's columns and stats make read-heavy analytics fast, but per-record appends slow.
  • ORC compresses best and is built for Hive, so it fits warehouses and cold archives.
  • CSV has no embedded schema and no stats, so every query reads and parses every column.
Compare
AvroParquetORC
Orientationrowcolumnarcolumnar
CodecSnappy/DeflateSnappy/GzipZlib/Snappy
Strengthschema evolution, appendsanalytic reads, pushdownhighest compression, Hive ACID
Best forKafka → HDFS ingestSpark, Presto, MLHive warehouse
ScenarioFormatCodec
Streaming ingest (Kafka → HDFS)AvroSnappy
Spark analytics pipelineParquetSnappy
Hive SQL warehouseORCZlib
Data exchange / APIsAvro or JSONnone
ML featuresParquetGzip
Archive / cold storageORC or ParquetZstd
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
event producers keep adding fields and old consumers must not breakAvro schema evolution
a Spark team runs aggregations over a few columns of billions of rowsParquet + Snappy
a Hive warehouse needs the smallest files and ACID updatesORC + Zlib
data older than 2 years must be stored as cheaply as possibleORC or Parquet + Zstd
an ingest layer lands Kafka events, converted nightly for analyticsAvro at ingest, Parquet for analytics
Traps
  • Avro is self-describing and binary, but it is row-oriented, not columnar.
  • ORC is also correct for analytics on Hive; Parquet is the broader standard (Q2 note).
  • Missing field = default value; extra field = ignored. Do not swap them.
  • JSON lines is not splittable in raw form.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Avro is a row format built for schema evolution; Parquet and ORC are columnar formats built for analytics.
3 marks
Avro stores rows with a JSON schema and handles schema evolution: missing fields take defaults, unknown fields are ignored. Parquet is columnar with min/max stats for fast analytics; ORC is columnar and compresses best for Hive. Example: Kafka events land as Avro, then convert nightly to Parquet + Snappy.
5 marks

Definition. A file format is how records are laid out on disk.

Options. Row: CSV, JSON, Avro. Columnar: Parquet, ORC.

Schema evolution in Avro. Writer schema stored in file, reader brings its schema, fields matched by name, missing fields get defaults, extra fields ignored.

Selection. Avro + Snappy for streaming ingest; Parquet + Snappy for Spark analytics; ORC + Zlib for Hive; ORC/Parquet + Zstd for archive.

Example. A new field in a Kafka event schema does not break old consumers.

Limitation. Avro is slow for column-selective analytics.

Compression codecs and splittability

Facts
  • Splittable: reading can start from any point in the file without decompressing from the beginning, so several Mappers read it in parallel.
  • Snappy: fast, medium ratio, not splittable alone (Parquet row groups are).
  • Gzip: slow, high ratio, not splittable. LZO: fast, medium, splittable with an index.
  • Bzip2: slowest, highest ratio, splittable. Zstd: fast, high ratio, not splittable (block-level).
  • Gzip trap: a 10 GB gzip CSV is read by exactly 1 Mapper, though HDFS stores it in many blocks.
  • Best practice: Parquet + Snappy for analytics, Avro + Snappy for streaming ingest, no raw gzip on big CSV.
How it works
  1. HDFS stores the file in blocks across nodes.
  2. MapReduce asks whether the format is splittable.
  3. If splittable, each split (block or Parquet row group) becomes its own Mapper.
  4. Each Mapper runs on a node holding its split (data-local scheduling).
  5. If not splittable (gzip), one Mapper reads the whole file from the start.
Why · pros · limits
  • Splittability decides parallelism: no splits means one Mapper and an idle cluster.
  • Splittable input lets the scheduler run tasks where the data is, avoiding network copies.
  • Codecs trade speed for size: Snappy for hot data read often, Zstd or Bzip2 for cold data.
  • A container format (Parquet, Avro) compresses inside its own blocks, so the file stays splittable even with Snappy.
Compare
CodecSpeed / ratioSplittable?
Snappyfast / mediumno (Parquet row groups are)
Gzipslow / highno
LZOfast / mediumyes, with index
Bzip2slowest / highestyes
Zstdfast / highno (block-level)
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a 50 GB compressed CSV keeps one Mapper busy for hours while the cluster idlesgzip is not splittable
three Mappers read three row groups of one file at oncesplittable Parquet
data must stay as plain text but still be splitbzip2 (or LZO with an index)
hot analytics data must decompress quicklySnappy inside Parquet
Calculation

Slide (P1 slides 20-21): a 10 GB gzip CSV gets 1 Mapper. The same 10 GB as Parquet with 128 MB row groups gets 10 × 1,024 / 128 = 80 splits, so up to 80 Mappers in parallel.

Rule: splits = ⌈F / B⌉ if splittable, 1 if not.

Step by step

  1. Given a gzip file of any size -> splits = 1.
  2. Given F = 10 GB -> F = 10 × 1,024 = 10,240 MB.
  3. Given 10,240 MB and B = 128 MB -> splits = 10,240 / 128 = 80.
  4. Given 80 splits -> up to 80 Mappers in parallel.

Variants

  • 10 GB with 256 MB row groups: 10,240 / 256 = 40 Mappers.
  • 1 GB with 128 MB row groups: 1,024 / 128 = 8 Mappers.
  • 300 MB with 128 MB blocks: ⌈300 / 128⌉ = ⌈2.34⌉ = 3 Mappers.
Traps
  • HDFS splitting a gzip file into blocks does not make it splittable for MapReduce.
  • Snappy alone is not splittable; Parquet + Snappy is, at row-group boundaries.
  • Bzip2 is splittable, gzip is not; T/F 3 hinges on this.
  • Round splits up: a partial last block still needs a Mapper.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
A splittable file can be read from any point, so multiple Mappers process different parts at the same time.
3 marks
Codecs trade speed for size, and only some are splittable: bzip2 and indexed LZO are; gzip, Snappy and Zstd alone are not. Example: a 10 GB gzip CSV gets 1 Mapper; as Parquet with 128 MB row groups it gets 80.
5 marks

Definition. Splittable means reading can start anywhere in the file without decompressing from the start.

Codecs. Snappy fast/medium, Gzip slow/high, LZO fast/medium, Bzip2 slowest/highest, Zstd fast/high. Splittable: Bzip2, LZO with index.

Steps. HDFS blocks; splittable files give one Mapper per split, run data-locally; gzip gives one Mapper.

Example. 10 GB gzip CSV: 1 Mapper; Parquet + Snappy, 128 MB row groups: 80.

Limitation. High-ratio codecs (Gzip, Bzip2) cost CPU on every read.

Chapter 2 · Week 6Apache Spark: RDDs and in-memory computing

Spark keeps working data in RAM so iterative jobs stop paying MapReduce's disk cost on every pass. The chapter covers how Spark is built, how RDDs recover from failures through lineage, lazy evaluation, the narrow/wide split that decides shuffles and stages, and DataFrames.

Exam question pool 22 questions from the slides · click a question for its answer

MCQ
Q1. What makes Spark significantly faster than MapReduce for iterative algorithms?
  • (a) Spark uses more CPU cores per node
  • (b) Spark writes intermediate results to faster SSDs
  • (c) Spark caches intermediate data as RDDs in memory
  • (d) Spark skips the shuffle phase entirely
show answer

(c) Spark caches intermediate data as RDDs in memory

Later iterations read from RAM instead of re-reading and re-writing HDFS; the hardware is the same.

MCQ
Q2. How does Spark achieve fault tolerance in RDDs without full data replication?
  • (a) Checkpointing to HDFS every 60 seconds
  • (b) Tracking lineage and recomputing lost partitions from parent RDDs
  • (c) Copying each RDD partition twice across two Executors
  • (d) Using a write-ahead log on the Driver node
show answer

(b) Tracking lineage and recomputing lost partitions from parent RDDs

The recipe is kept, not copies; (c) is replication.

MCQ
Q3. Which of the following is NOT a component of the Spark ecosystem?
  • (a) MLlib
  • (b) GraphX
  • (c) Structured Streaming
  • (d) HBase
show answer

(d) HBase

HBase is a separate NoSQL database on HDFS; Spark can only read it as a data source.

MCQ
Q4. In Spark, a “transformation” such as map() or filter() is:
  • (a) Immediately executed when called and materialised to memory
  • (b) Lazily evaluated — only executed when a subsequent action is called
  • (c) Automatically persisted to HDFS after execution
  • (d) Only available in the Scala API, not in PySpark
show answer

(b) Lazily evaluated — only executed when a subsequent action is called

rdd.filter(f) alone does nothing; rdd.filter(f).count() runs it.

True/False
1. Spark can only run on top of Hadoop YARN and has no other deployment option.
show answer

False. The Cluster Manager can be YARN, Kubernetes, Mesos or Spark Standalone.

True/False
2. RDDs are mutable — individual elements can be modified in place after creation.
show answer

False. RDDs are immutable; every transformation creates a new RDD.

True/False
3. Spark's DataFrame API provides schema-aware, named-column operations similar to SQL tables.
show answer

True. A DataFrame has named columns and a schema, like a relational table, and accepts SQL.

True/False
4. MapReduce is generally better than Spark for machine learning workloads that require many iterations over the same dataset.
show answer

False. Spark is better: it caches the data in RAM and is 10–100× faster on iterative work, while MapReduce hits disk every iteration.

Descriptive
D1. Explain the concept of a Resilient Distributed Dataset (RDD). What does “resilient” mean in this context? How does Spark recover a lost RDD partition without replication?
show answer

RDD: a read-only, partitioned collection of records, created from stable storage (HDFS, S3) or by transforming another RDD. Properties: immutable, partitioned, lazy, lineage, resilient.

Resilient: it survives a node failure by recomputing lost partitions from its lineage (the steps that built it) instead of keeping 3 copies. The computation is the backup.

Recovery:

  1. Spark detects Part 2 of mappedRDD is lost.
  2. Traces lineage: mappedRDD ← filteredRDD ← inputRDD.
  3. Re-reads input Part 2 from HDFS.
  4. Re-applies filter, then map.
  5. Part 2 restored; the job is not restarted.

Diagram: draw the lineage chain shown in the diagram in the RDD card: inputRDD → filteredRDD → mappedRDD, with Part 2 of mappedRDD crossed out and the recovery path going back to inputRDD Part 2 on HDFS.

Cheap for narrow dependencies; checkpoint long chains.

Descriptive
D2. Differentiate between Spark transformations and actions. Give two concrete examples of each. Why does Spark not execute transformations immediately?
show answer
TransformationAction
RunsLazily, only recorded in the DAGImmediately, triggers the DAG
ReturnsA new RDDA value to the Driver, or output to storage
Examplesmap(f), filter(f)count(), collect()

Why wait: with the whole DAG in view, Spark can

  • optimise the plan (DAG scheduler, or Catalyst for DataFrames);
  • pipeline filter and map in one pass, with no intermediate disk writes;
  • run only the partitions needed, e.g. first() stops at the first match.
Descriptive
D3. Describe Spark's lazy evaluation model. What is the advantage of deferring computation until an action is called? Use the example rdd.filter(x > 1000).first() to illustrate.
show answer

Model: transformations are only recorded in the DAG. When an action is called, Spark analyses the DAG, optimises it, pipelines compatible steps in one pass per partition, and runs only the partitions it needs.

Advantages: less work (early stop, skipped partitions), no intermediate disk writes, whole-plan optimisation.

Example:

  1. filter(x > 1000): recorded, no data read.
  2. first(): action; Spark starts reading the first partition.
  3. It returns the first value above 1000 and stops.
  4. Eager execution would filter the entire dataset, then keep one value.
Analytical
A1. Iterative I/O Cost Analysis: You need to train a K-Means clustering model over 50 GB of bKash transaction data with 100 iterations. Assume HDFS read speed = 500 MB/s and RAM cache read speed = 5,000 MB/s.
  • Calculate the total HDFS I/O for MapReduce (all 100 iterations)
  • Calculate the total I/O for Spark (50 GB fits in cluster RAM)
  • Estimate the wall-clock time for each approach
  • Under what conditions would Spark NOT outperform MapReduce?
show answer

Given: D = 50 GB = 50,000 MB, k = 100, HDFS 500 MB/s (write assumed = read), RAM 5,000 MB/s.

MapReduceSpark
HDFS I/O100 × (50 + 50) = 10 TB50 GB (read once); RAM reads 99 × 50 = 4,950 GB
Per pass50,000/500 = 100 s read + 100 s write = 200 s100 s first, then 50,000/5,000 = 10 s
Total time100 × 200 = 20,000 s ≈ 5.56 h100 + 99 × 10 = 1,090 s ≈ 18.2 min

Disk I/O 200× less; time speedup 20,000 / 1,090 ≈ 18.3×.

Spark does not win when: the job is single-pass (nothing to reuse); the data does not fit in RAM (spill or evict and recompute); Executor memory (~8 GB minimum) is too costly; stable MapReduce code is already fast enough.

Analytical
A2. RDD Failure Recovery Trace: A Spark job computes input → filteredRDD → mappedRDD → reducedRDD over 10 partitions. Mid-execution, the Executor holding partitions 3 and 7 of reducedRDD crashes. Trace the complete recovery sequence: what does the Driver detect, which partitions of which RDDs need to be recomputed, and what is the worst-case recovery cost if reducedRDD involves a wide dependency (groupByKey)?
show answer

Stages: filter, map narrow → Stage 1 (input → filteredRDD → mappedRDD). groupByKey wide → Stage 2 (reducedRDD).

Driver detects: the Executor is lost; tasks for reducedRDD partitions 3 and 7 failed. The other 8 partitions are fine.

Lineage: reducedRDD ← groupByKey ← mappedRDD ← map ← filteredRDD ← filter ← input.

CaseRecomputed
If all narrowPartitions 3 and 7 at each level: 2 × 4 = 8 computations (20%)
Wide, shuffle files surviveOnly reducedRDD 3 and 7, re-fetching from all 10 mappedRDD outputs
Wide, worst caseShuffle output lost too: all 10 input, filteredRDD and mappedRDD partitions (30) + reshuffle + reducedRDD 3, 7 (2) ≈ the whole job

Limit the cost: reduceByKey instead of groupByKey, cache mappedRDD, checkpoint long chains.

Discussion
1. In-memory is a multiplier, not a panacea: Spark is 10–100× faster on iterative workloads; for single-pass ETL over data larger than RAM, gains are smaller (2–5×)
show answer

Claim: memory speeds up reuse; it does not fix every workload.

For: iterative jobs reuse the working set. RDD paper: logistic regression 127 s vs 0.96 s per iteration (132×).

Against: single-pass ETL reads each byte once, and data larger than RAM spills to disk, so the gain falls to 2–5×.

Choose by workload: more than 2 iterations favours Spark.

Discussion
2. The lineage model scales elegantly: re-computing a lost partition from lineage is cheaper than maintaining 3× replicated in-memory state
show answer

Claim: lineage beats replication for in-memory data.

For: 3× replication triples RAM and network writes on every step; lineage costs nothing until a failure and then rebuilds only lost partitions.

Against: with wide dependencies or long chains, one lost partition can need many ancestors recomputed, so checkpoints are still needed.

Discussion
3. Shuffle is the bottleneck: both at GraySort and in production; minimising wide dependencies (shuffle) is the primary Spark tuning skill
show answer

Claim: shuffles dominate Spark's cost.

For: a shuffle moves data over the network, writes to disk and forces a stage barrier. Spark's GraySort win came from a cheaper shuffle: sort-based shuffle (about 120 TB less I/O), all 8 SSDs as shuffle directories, one sortByKey. Tuning: reduceByKey over groupByKey, co-partition before joins.

Against: sorts and un-partitioned joins need a shuffle; you minimise it, you cannot remove it.

Discussion
4. Benchmark marketing is real: GraySort made Spark famous; it also hid cases where Spark's JVM memory overhead hurt performance (many small files, high-cardinality groupBy)
show answer

Claim: a headline benchmark sells a system but shows one workload.

For: 100 TB in 23 min on 206 nodes vs Hadoop's 72 min on 2,100 drove Spark's adoption.

Against: it ran on tuned large nodes (244 GB RAM, 8 SSDs). Many small files (per-task overhead) and high-cardinality groupBy (too many keys for JVM memory, GC pauses) were not tested.

Test on your own workload.

Discussion
5. Open-source velocity: the Apache Spark community grew to 1,000+ contributors in 5 years; Databricks’ business model (cloud + support) aligned commercial incentives with open-source development
show answer

Claim: a free engine plus a paid service sped up Spark.

For: Databricks earns from cloud and support, not licences, so improving open Spark helps its business. 1,000+ contributors in 5 years produced Spark SQL, MLlib, Structured Streaming, GraphX.

Against: one company steering the project can shape the roadmap and push features to its paid product first (lock-in risk).

Live Exercise
Task. Task (25 minutes, pairs)
Run the following PySpark script locally (Spark local mode).
Goal: build and inspect an RDD lineage; observe lazy evaluation; compare cached vs. uncached timing.
from pyspark import SparkContext
import time
sc = SparkContext("local[4]", "RDDDemo")
sc.setLogLevel("WARN")
# Build lineage (lazy - no compute yet)
lines = sc.textFile("README.md")
words = lines.flatMap(lambda l: l.split())
pairs = words.map(lambda w: (w.lower(), 1))
counts = pairs.reduceByKey(lambda a, b: a+b)
# Inspect lineage BEFORE action
print(counts.toDebugString().decode())
# First action - triggers full DAG
t0 = time.time()
n = counts.count()
print(f"count={n}  time={time.time()-t0:.2f}s")
# Cache and re-run
counts.cache()
counts.count()          # warm up cache
t1 = time.time()
n = counts.count()      # served from cache
print(f"cached count={n} "
      f"time={time.time()-t1:.4f}s")
sc.stop()
show answer

local[4] = local mode, 4 threads. textFile, flatMap, map, reduceByKey only build lineage; the first count() runs the DAG. Key additions for the questions (run with python rdd_demo.py):

print(counts.toDebugString().decode())   # Q1 before cache
for i in (1, 2):                          # Q2 two uncached runs
    t0 = time.time(); counts.count()
    print(i, time.time() - t0)
counts.cache()
print(counts.toDebugString().decode())   # Q1 after cache
counts.count()                            # fills the cache
t1 = time.time(); counts.count()
print("cached", time.time() - t1)
# Q3: counts = pairs.groupByKey().mapValues(sum)
input("open localhost:4040, then Enter")

Expected: the lineage shows HadoopRDD → Python RDDs, with a +- indent at the reduceByKey shuffle; count = number of distinct lower-cased words.

Live Exercise
1. Call rdd.toDebugString() before and after cache() — what changes?
show answer

The parent chain does not change, because cache() is lazy and lineage is kept for recovery. After cache() the top line shows a storage level (e.g. [Memory Serialized 1x Replicated]). After an action fills the cache it also shows CachedPartitions and memory size (expected).

Live Exercise
2. Time rdd.count() twice without cache(). Then add .cache() and time again. Report the speedup.
show answer

Speedup = uncached time / cached time. Expected: run 1 slowest (start-up, read, shuffle); run 2 a bit faster (shuffle files reused, Stage 1 skipped); cached run fastest. On a small README start-up overhead dominates, so the ratio is modest; the RDD paper shows 127 s → 0.96 s (132×) at scale.

Live Exercise
3. Change reduceByKey to groupByKey. Use the Spark UI (localhost:4040) to compare shuffle read/write bytes between the two.
show answer

Use pairs.groupByKey().mapValues(sum), then read Shuffle Write/Read on the UI's Stages tab. Expected: same counts, larger shuffle bytes for groupByKey, because reduceByKey combines each partition's pairs before the shuffle and groupByKey sends every (word, 1) pair.

Topic cards 6 topics · everything the pool tests

Why Spark: the MapReduce iteration bottleneck

Facts
  • MapReduce writes every intermediate result to HDFS (the cluster's shared disk) before the next step can start.
  • Iterative algorithms (K-Means, logistic regression, PageRank) pass over the same data many times, so MapReduce pays disk I/O every iteration.
  • Spark reads the data from HDFS once, caches it in RAM, and runs every later iteration from memory.
  • Slide example: K-Means, 100 iterations over 50 GB. MapReduce does 10 TB of disk I/O, Spark 50 GB: about 200× less.
  • Spark is 10–100× faster on iterative work. For single-pass ETL over data larger than RAM the gain is only 2–5×.
  • Use Spark for ML, graphs, interactive queries, streaming, or more than 2 iterations over the same data.
How it works
  1. Iteration 1 reads the dataset from HDFS
  2. Spark keeps the partitions in Executor RAM as a cached RDD
  3. Each later iteration reads the cached partitions from memory, not from disk
  4. No intermediate result goes back to HDFS between iterations
  5. Only the final result is written out to storage
Why · pros · limits
  • RAM is much faster to read than disk, and Spark also skips the HDFS write after every step.
  • Memory multiplies the benefit of reuse. A job that reads each byte once has nothing to cache.
  • Spark runs on the same hardware as MapReduce. The speed comes from memory, not from more CPU cores.
  • Limitation: if the data does not fit in cluster RAM, partitions spill to disk or are evicted and recomputed.
  • Limitation: Spark needs about 8 GB per Executor. MapReduce stays reasonable for single-pass batch jobs on very large clusters where that memory cost is prohibitive, or when existing MapReduce code is stable and fast enough.
Compare
MapReduceSpark
Intermediate storageDisk (HDFS) after every stepMemory (spills to disk if needed)
Programming modelMap + Reduce onlymap, filter, join, groupBy, SQL, ML, streaming
Iterative algorithmsSlow: disk per iterationFast: cached RDD, 10–100×
Fault toleranceRe-execute task from HDFSRecompute from lineage
Streaming / interactiveNot native / over 30 sStructured Streaming / sub-second shell
Languages / learning curveJava; low (2 functions)Python, Scala, Java, R; moderate
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a model is trained with 50 passes over the same transaction tableiterative workload: Spark with the data cached in RAM
a nightly job reads 5 TB of logs once, converts the format and writes it outsingle-pass ETL: Spark gains only 2–5×; MapReduce is acceptable
analysts want sub-second answers while exploring data in a shellSpark interactive queries (MapReduce takes over 30 s)
the working data is larger than the cluster's total RAMSpark spills or evicts and recomputes, so it loses most of its edge
Calculation

K-Means on 50 GB (50,000 MB), 100 iterations. HDFS 500 MB/s (write assumed equal to read), RAM 5,000 MB/s.

MapReduceSpark
HDFS I/O100 × (50 + 50) = 10,000 GB = 10 TB50 GB (read once)
Time per pass100 s read + 100 s write = 200 sfirst 100 s, then 10 s from RAM
Total time100 × 200 = 20,000 s ≈ 5.56 h100 + 99 × 10 = 1,090 s ≈ 18.2 min

Disk I/O ratio 10,000 / 50 = 200×. Time speedup 20,000 / 1,090 ≈ 18.3×.

Step by step

  1. Given D = 50 GB, k = 100, MapReduce reads and writes D each iteration → MR I/O = 100 × (50 + 50) = 10,000 GB = 10 TB
  2. Given Spark reads HDFS only in iteration 1 → Spark HDFS I/O = 50 GB
  3. Given 10,000 GB and 50 GB → disk I/O ratio = 10,000 / 50 = 200×
  4. Given 50,000 MB at 500 MB/s → one HDFS pass = 100 s; one MR iteration (read + write) = 200 s
  5. Given 200 s per iteration → T_MR = 100 × 200 = 20,000 s ≈ 5.56 h
  6. Given RAM at 5,000 MB/s → one RAM pass = 10 s; T_Spark = 100 + 99 × 10 = 1,090 s ≈ 18.2 min
  7. Given 20,000 s and 1,090 s → speedup = 20,000 / 1,090 ≈ 18.3×

Variants

  • D = 20 GB, k = 30, HDFS 400 MB/s, RAM 4,000 MB/s: MR I/O = 30 × 40 = 1,200 GB (60× Spark's 20 GB); T_MR = 30 × 100 = 3,000 s; T_Spark = 50 + 29 × 5 = 195 s; speedup ≈ 15.4×
  • Part 2 slide: 10 iterations over 100 GB at 100 MB/s, reads only: 10 × 100,000 / 100 = 10,000 s ≈ 2.8 h (the slide's “≈ 3 hours”)
  • Per-iteration benchmark (RDD paper): logistic regression 127 s vs 0.96 s → 127 / 0.96 ≈ 132×; K-Means 150 s vs 5 s → 30×
Traps
  • The 200× is disk I/O, not time. Here RAM is only 10× faster than HDFS, so the time speedup is about 18×.
  • Spark iterations 2–100 still read 50 GB each, from RAM. That is 99 RAM passes, not 100.
  • If the HDFS write speed is not given, say you assume it equals the read speed.
  • Spark does not skip the shuffle. Wide operations like reduceByKey still shuffle.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
MapReduce writes every intermediate result to HDFS, so iterative algorithms pay disk I/O on every pass; Spark caches the data in RAM once.
3 marks
Iterative jobs read the same data many times. MapReduce reads and writes HDFS each iteration, while Spark reads HDFS once and serves later iterations from RAM. Example: 100 K-Means iterations on 50 GB cost 10 TB of disk I/O in MapReduce and 50 GB in Spark.
5 marks
MapReduce stores every intermediate result on HDFS. Iterative algorithms such as K-Means re-read the same data each pass, so they pay read + write per iteration. Spark reads the input once, caches the partitions in Executor RAM and runs iterations 2 to k from memory, writing only the final result. Example: 50 GB, 100 iterations, HDFS 500 MB/s, RAM 5,000 MB/s. MapReduce: 10 TB of I/O, 20,000 s. Spark: 50 GB from HDFS, 1,090 s, about 18× faster. Limitation: single-pass jobs, or data larger than RAM, gain only 2–5×, and Spark needs about 8 GB per Executor.

  • (c) It writes intermediate results to SSDs
  • (d) It uses more CPU cores per node

MapReduce re-reads and re-writes HDFS every iteration; Spark reads once and iterates in memory.
Fill in the blank

show answer
HDFS (disk)
Define / list
Define an iterative algorithm and give two examples from the slides.
show answer
An algorithm that passes over the same data repeatedly, each pass using the previous result. Examples: K-Means clustering, logistic regression (also PageRank).
Explain the steps
Describe how Spark runs a 100-iteration K-Means job on 50 GB.
show answer
Iteration 1 reads 50 GB from HDFS; the partitions are cached in Executor RAM; iterations 2–100 read from memory; nothing is written to HDFS between iterations; only the final centroids are written out.
Why / justify
Why does Spark gain only 2–5× on single-pass ETL over data larger than RAM?
show answer
Each byte is read once, so there is nothing to reuse from the cache; and data larger than RAM spills to disk, so Spark does disk I/O much like MapReduce.
Distinguish
Distinguish MapReduce and Spark on intermediate storage and fault tolerance.
show answer
Storage: MapReduce writes to HDFS after every step; Spark keeps data in memory, spilling only if needed. Fault tolerance: MapReduce re-executes the task from HDFS input; Spark recomputes lost partitions from lineage.
Scenario
A bank trains a fraud model: 30 passes over 20 GB of transactions. A second team converts 5 TB of logs once a night.

show answer
i) The 30-pass fraud model. ii) It reuses the same 20 GB every pass, so caching in RAM removes 29 HDFS reads and all intermediate writes. iii) Small, about 2–5×, because the data is read once and is larger than RAM. iv) Spark needs about 8 GB per Executor; for a single-pass batch job on a very large cluster that memory cost can be prohibitive, and stable MapReduce code that is fast enough need not be rewritten.
One-step calc

show answer
100 × 200 = 20,000 s ≈ 5.56 hours.
Full calc
D = 20 GB, 30 iterations, HDFS 400 MB/s read and write, RAM 4,000 MB/s. Find MR disk I/O, Spark HDFS I/O, both times and the speedup.
show answer
MR I/O = 30 × (20 + 20) = 1,200 GB. Spark = 20 GB (60× less). HDFS pass = 20,000 / 400 = 50 s, so T_MR = 30 × 100 = 3,000 s. RAM pass = 20,000 / 4,000 = 5 s, so T_Spark = 50 + 29 × 5 = 195 s. Speedup = 3,000 / 195 ≈ 15.4×.
Long answer
Explain why MapReduce fails for iterative algorithms and how Spark solves it. When would you still choose MapReduce?
show answer
Use the 5-mark answer: the disk-per-iteration cost, Spark's read-once cache, the 50 GB / 100-iteration numbers, and the limits (single pass, data larger than RAM, about 8 GB per Executor, stable MapReduce code).

Spark architecture

Facts
  • Three tiers: Driver Program, Cluster Manager, Executors.
  • Driver: runs the user's main(), creates SparkContext, builds the DAG (directed acyclic graph of steps) and coordinates execution.
  • Cluster Manager: allocates cluster resources. Options: YARN, Kubernetes, Mesos or Spark Standalone.
  • Executors: JVM processes on Worker Nodes. They run tasks and store cached RDD partitions in memory.
  • Spark 1.x entry point: SparkContext. Spark 2.x+: SparkSession unifies SQL, DataFrames and RDDs.
  • In Spark 2.x+ the SparkContext is still reachable as spark.sparkContext.
How it works
  1. The Driver starts and creates a SparkSession (and its SparkContext)
  2. The Driver asks the Cluster Manager for resources
  3. The Cluster Manager launches Executors on the Worker Nodes
  4. The Driver turns the program into a DAG, splits it into stages and tasks, and schedules the tasks on Executors
  5. Executors run the tasks and keep cached partitions in RAM
  6. Results go back to the Driver, or an action writes them to storage
  7. If an Executor stops responding, the Driver marks its partitions lost and reschedules the work
Why · pros · limits
  • A separate Cluster Manager lets the same Spark program run on YARN, Kubernetes, Mesos or Standalone.
  • Executors hold the cache, so data stays in RAM next to the tasks that reuse it.
  • The Driver sees the whole DAG, so it can plan stages and know which partitions to rebuild after a failure.
  • Cost: every Executor needs memory, about 8 GB minimum.
Compare
DriverExecutor
How manyOne per applicationMany, on Worker Nodes
Runsmain(), SparkContext/SparkSessionTasks sent by the Driver
JobBuilds the DAG, schedules, coordinatesComputes partitions
HoldsThe plan (DAG, lineage)Cached RDD partitions in RAM
SparkContextSparkSession
VersionSpark 1.x entry pointSpark 2.x+ entry point
CoversRDDsSQL, DataFrames and RDDs
AccessSparkContext(...)SparkSession.builder...getOrCreate(); context via spark.sparkContext
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
the process that builds the plan and hands out tasksDriver
Spark runs on the company's existing Hadoop resource managerCluster Manager = YARN
a JVM on a worker machine runs tasks and keeps partitions in RAMExecutor
Spark 3 code starts with spark.read and spark.sqlSparkSession, the unified entry point
Traps
  • Spark does not need YARN. Kubernetes, Mesos and Standalone also work.
  • Cached data lives in the Executors, not in the Driver.
  • SparkContext did not disappear in Spark 2; it sits inside SparkSession.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Spark has three tiers: a Driver that builds the DAG and schedules tasks, a Cluster Manager that allocates resources, and Executors that run tasks and cache data.
3 marks
The Driver runs main(), creates the SparkContext/SparkSession and turns the program into a DAG of tasks. The Cluster Manager (YARN, Kubernetes, Mesos or Standalone) gives it resources. Executors, JVMs on Worker Nodes, run the tasks and hold cached partitions, e.g. a cached training set reused by each K-Means iteration.
5 marks
Spark uses a three-tier architecture. The Driver runs the user's main(), creates the SparkSession (SparkContext in Spark 1.x), builds the DAG of transformations and schedules tasks. The Cluster Manager (YARN, Kubernetes, Mesos or Spark Standalone) allocates resources and launches Executors on Worker Nodes. Executors are JVM processes that run tasks and store cached RDD partitions in memory. Flow: Driver → Cluster Manager → Executors run tasks → results to the Driver or to storage. If an Executor dies, the Driver uses lineage to rebuild its partitions elsewhere. Limitation: each Executor needs about 8 GB of RAM.

Scenario

Long answer

RDDs and lineage-based fault tolerance

Facts
  • RDD (Resilient Distributed Dataset): a read-only, partitioned collection of records.
  • Created only two ways: by transforming an existing RDD (map, filter) or by loading from stable storage (HDFS, S3).
  • Five properties: immutable, partitioned, lazy, lineage, resilient.
  • Lineage: the record of transformations that derived an RDD from its parent RDDs.
  • Resilient: lost partitions are recomputed from lineage, not replicated. “The computation is the backup.”
  • Persistence levels: MEMORY_ONLY, MEMORY_AND_DISK, DISK_ONLY, MEMORY_ONLY_SER, OFF_HEAP. cache() = MEMORY_ONLY.
How it works
  1. An Executor crashes and Part 2 of mappedRDD is lost
  2. Spark detects the lost partition
  3. It traces the lineage: mappedRDD came from filteredRDD, which came from inputRDD
  4. It re-reads only Part 2 of the input from HDFS
  5. It re-applies filter, then map, to that one partition
  6. Part 2 is restored without restarting the whole job
Why · pros · limits
  • Replicating in-memory data 3 times would triple RAM use and send every write over the network. Lineage stores only the steps.
  • You pay only when a failure happens, and only for the lost partitions.
  • Immutability makes replay safe: the same steps on the same input give the same partition.
  • Limitation: cheap for narrow dependencies, expensive for wide ones. Checkpoint long lineage chains to HDFS.
  • Cache an RDD only if more than one action reuses it. Caching single-use RDDs wastes RAM and evicts other data (LRU).
Compare
HDFS replicationRDD lineage
What is kept3 copies of the dataThe list of transformations
Cost with no failure3× storage and network writesAlmost none
RecoveryRead another copyRecompute lost partitions from parents
Weak spotStorage overheadWide dependencies and long chains (fix: checkpoint)
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a worker dies and Spark rebuilds only the missing piece by re-running filter and maplineage-based recovery
an iterative job's chain of steps grows very long and recovery gets slowcheckpoint the RDD to HDFS
every iteration of a training loop reads the same lookup datacache()/persist() it (MEMORY_ONLY)
a student tries to change one element of an RDD in placenot possible: RDDs are immutable; map to a new RDD
Traps
  • Checkpointing is an optional add-on for long chains. Lineage is the main mechanism.
  • Copying each partition to two Executors is replication, which RDDs avoid.
  • cache() is lazy. Nothing is stored until an action runs.
  • Resilient means recomputable, not replicated.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
An RDD is a read-only, partitioned collection of records that Spark rebuilds from its lineage if a partition is lost.
3 marks
An RDD is an immutable, partitioned collection created from storage or from another RDD. “Resilient” means a lost partition is recomputed from lineage, not restored from a copy. Example: lose Part 2 of mappedRDD → re-read input Part 2, re-apply filter then map.
5 marks
An RDD is a read-only, partitioned collection of records, created from stable storage or by transforming another RDD. It is immutable, partitioned across Executors, lazy, and remembers its lineage. Resilient means fault tolerance without copies. Recovery: Spark detects the lost partition, traces lineage (mappedRDD ← filteredRDD ← inputRDD), re-reads only that input partition from HDFS, re-applies filter then map, and the partition is back without restarting the job. Sketch: inputRDD → filter → filteredRDD → map → mappedRDD [Part 2 lost]. Limitation: wide dependencies and long chains make recovery expensive, so checkpoint long chains to HDFS.
Practise this topic in 8 formats
MCQ
How does an RDD tolerate the loss of a partition?
  • (a) It replays a write-ahead log on the Driver
  • (b) It recomputes the partition from its lineage
  • (c) It reads a replica on another Executor
  • (d) It restores the latest 60-second checkpoint
show answer
(b) It recomputes the partition from its lineage
RDDs keep the recipe, not copies; Spark re-runs the steps for the lost partition.
Fill in the blank
Lineage-based recovery is efficient for ______ dependencies but expensive for ______ dependencies.
show answer
narrow; wide
Define / list
List the five key properties of an RDD.
show answer
Immutable, partitioned, lazy, lineage, resilient.
Explain the steps
List the steps Spark takes to recover a lost partition of mappedRDD.
show answer
Detect the lost partition → trace lineage to filteredRDD and inputRDD → re-read that input partition from HDFS → re-apply filter then map → partition restored, job continues.
Why / justify
Why does Spark use lineage instead of replicating RDDs in memory?
show answer
Replication would triple RAM use and network writes on every step. Lineage costs almost nothing until a failure, and then only the lost partitions are recomputed.
Distinguish
Distinguish HDFS-style replication from RDD lineage.
show answer
Replication stores 3 copies and reads another copy on failure. Lineage stores the transformations and recomputes the lost partition from its parents.
Scenario
A 200-iteration PageRank job caches its rank RDD. A worker fails at iteration 150.
i) What lets Spark rebuild the lost ranks? ii) Why might that be slow here? iii) What should the programmer have done? iv) Is a copy of the partition read from another node?
show answer
i) Lineage. ii) The chain is 150 steps long and PageRank joins (wide), so many ancestors must be recomputed. iii) Checkpoint the RDD to HDFS every so often. iv) No, RDDs are not replicated; the partition is recomputed.
Long answer
Explain the RDD, what “resilient” means and how a lost partition is recovered. Include a diagram.
show answer
Use the 5-mark answer and draw inputRDD → filteredRDD → mappedRDD with Part 2 crossed out and the recovery path back to HDFS.

Transformations, actions and lazy evaluation

Facts
  • Transformation: builds the DAG and returns a new RDD, without running. map, filter, flatMap, distinct, reduceByKey, join, groupByKey, cache.
  • Action: triggers DAG execution and returns a value to the Driver or writes to storage. collect, count, first, take(n), reduce, saveAsTextFile, foreach.
  • Rule: no transformation executes until an action is called.
  • Lazy evaluation: Spark only records transformations in the DAG and runs them when an action is called.
  • Slide example: rdd.filter(x > 1000).first() stops after the first matching element.
How it works
  1. Each transformation is recorded in the DAG; no data is read
  2. An action such as count() or collect() is called
  3. Spark analyses the full DAG
  4. It applies the Catalyst optimizer (DataFrames) or the DAG scheduler (RDDs)
  5. It pipelines compatible steps, e.g. filter and map in one pass over each partition, with no intermediate disk writes
  6. It executes only the partitions it needs and returns the result
Why · pros · limits
  • Spark sees the whole plan before running anything, so it can optimise it.
  • Pipelining saves extra passes and intermediate writes.
  • Early stop: first() and take(n) read only as much as they need.
  • The recorded plan is also the lineage that fault recovery replays.
Compare
TransformationAction
RunsLazily: only recordedEagerly: starts the job
ReturnsA new RDDA value to the Driver, or output to storage
Examplesmap, filter, flatMap, reduceByKey, join, cachecount, collect, first, take(n), reduce, saveAsTextFile
Effect on DAGAdds a nodeExecutes the DAG
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
the script runs instantly until count() is added, then takes minuteslazy evaluation: the action triggered the work
only one sample transaction above 50,000 BDT is neededfirst() or take(1): Spark stops early
the result must be saved to HDFSsaveAsTextFile, an action
filter and map run in one pass over each partition with no disk writespipelining within a stage
Traps
  • cache() is a transformation (lazy), not an action.
  • reduceByKey is a transformation; reduce is an action.
  • map and filter exist in PySpark, not only in Scala.
  • A transformation's result is not written to HDFS automatically.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Lazy evaluation means Spark records transformations in a DAG and runs nothing until an action is called.
3 marks
Transformations (map, filter) return a new RDD and only add to the DAG; actions (count, collect) run the DAG and return a value or write output. Waiting lets Spark optimise and pipeline the whole plan. Example: in rdd.filter(x > 1000).first(), Spark stops at the first match.
5 marks
A transformation (map, filter, flatMap, reduceByKey) returns a new RDD and only records a step in the DAG. An action (count, collect, first, saveAsTextFile) triggers execution and returns a value to the Driver or writes to storage. When an action is called, Spark analyses the DAG, optimises it (Catalyst or the DAG scheduler), pipelines steps like filter and map into one pass per partition, and runs only the partitions it needs. Example: rdd.filter(x > 1000).first() reads until it finds one value above 1000 and stops; eager execution would filter the whole dataset first. Limitation: nothing runs until the action, so errors appear late.
Practise this topic in 8 formats
MCQ

  • (a) cache()
  • (b) map()
  • (c) reduceByKey()
  • (d) count()
show answer
(d) count()
count() runs the DAG and returns a number to the Driver; the others are transformations.
Fill in the blank
No ______ executes until an ______ is called.
show answer
transformation; action
Define / list
