show answer
Transformation: returns a new RDD lazily (map, filter). Action: triggers execution, returns a value or writes output (count, collect).
Explain the steps
What does Spark do when an action is called?
show answer
Analyses the full DAG → optimises it (Catalyst/DAG scheduler) → pipelines compatible transformations into one pass per partition → executes only the partitions needed.
Why / justify
Why does Spark not execute transformations immediately?
show answer
Seeing the whole plan first lets it optimise, pipeline steps without intermediate writes, and skip work, e.g. first() stops at the first match.
Distinguish
Distinguish reduce and reduceByKey.
show answer
reduce(f) is an action that returns one aggregate value to the Driver. reduceByKey(f) is a transformation that returns a new RDD with one value per key.
Scenario
A student writes big = rdd.filter(lambda t: t[2] > 50000), sees it finish in 0.01 s, then adds big.first().
i) Why was the first line so fast? ii) Is first() a transformation or action? iii) How much data does first() read? iv) Name the property shown.
show answer
i) filter is a transformation; it was only recorded. ii) Action. iii) Only until the first matching record is found. iv) Lazy evaluation.
Long answer
Describe Spark's lazy evaluation model using rdd.filter(x > 1000).first().
show answer
Use the 5-mark answer: transformation vs action, the four steps on an action, the early-stop example, the advantage over eager execution.

Narrow vs wide dependencies, stages and shuffle

Facts
  • Narrow dependency: each output partition depends on at most one input partition. map, filter, flatMap, union, mapPartitions, coalesce.
  • Wide dependency: an output partition may depend on many input partitions. groupByKey, reduceByKey, aggregateByKey, sortByKey, join, repartition, distinct.
  • Wide dependency ⇒ shuffle (data sent across the network) ⇒ stage boundary (a new stage).
  • Narrow steps are pipelined in one stage. A lost narrow partition is rebuilt from one parent partition; a wide one needs all parent partitions.
  • Prefer reduceByKey over groupByKey: it combines locally before the shuffle, like a MapReduce Combiner.
  • Hash partitioner (default): key.hashCode() % numPartitions. Co-partitioning two RDDs on the same key removes the join shuffle.
How it works
  1. The Driver reads the DAG from the action back to the input
  2. Consecutive narrow transformations are grouped into one stage and pipelined
  3. Each wide transformation cuts a stage boundary
  4. Each stage runs as one task per partition
  5. Tasks before the boundary write shuffle output to local disk
  6. Tasks after the boundary fetch their keys from every earlier task over the network
Why · pros · limits
  • A shuffle costs network traffic, disk writes and a barrier where every task must finish. It is Spark's main cost.
  • Minimising wide dependencies is the main Spark tuning skill.
  • reduceByKey sends at most one record per key per partition; groupByKey sends every pair.
  • GraySort 2013: Spark sorted 100 TB in 23 min on 206 nodes (Hadoop: 72 min, 2,100 nodes), mainly through a cheaper sort-based shuffle.
  • Limitation: some shuffles cannot be avoided, e.g. a global sort or a join on un-partitioned data.
Compare
NarrowWide
Dependency1-to-1 (at most one parent partition)All-to-all (many parent partitions)
Examplesmap, filter, flatMap, union, coalescegroupByKey, reduceByKey, join, repartition, distinct
Data movementNoneShuffle across the network
StagesPipelined in one stageStarts a new stage
RecoveryRecompute one parent partitionRecompute from all parent partitions
reduceByKeygroupByKey
Local combineYes, before the shuffleNo
Records shuffledAt most one per key per partitionEvery (key, value) pair
ResultOne value per keyAll values per key (list)
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
word counts are slow and the Spark UI shows huge shuffle bytes from groupByKeyswitch to reduceByKey (local combine)
two RDDs are partitioned on customer_id and joined with no shuffleco-partitioning makes the join narrow
filter then map run back to back in one stagenarrow dependencies, pipelined
losing one partition after a groupByKey forces many parent partitions to rerunwide dependency recovery is expensive
Calculation

A2: input → filteredRDD → mappedRDD → reducedRDD (groupByKey), 10 partitions. The Executor with reducedRDD partitions 3 and 7 crashes.

CaseWork redone
All narrow (comparison)Partitions 3 and 7 only: 2 / 10 = 20%; 2 × 4 = 8 partition computations
Wide, shuffle files surviveOnly the 2 Stage-2 tasks, re-fetching from all 10 map outputs
Wide, worst case (shuffle files lost)Stage 1 again: 10 input + 10 filter + 10 map = 30, plus 2 reduce tasks

Shuffle size: 4 partitions × 1,000 pairs, 50 distinct words each. groupByKey 4,000 records; reduceByKey ≤ 4 × 50 = 200, 95% less.

Step by step

  1. Given filter and map are narrow and groupByKey is wide → Stage 1 = input → filter → map; Stage 2 = groupByKey
  2. Given partitions 3 and 7 lost with only narrow steps → redo 2 / 10 = 20% of the work
  3. Given groupByKey → each lost partition needs data from all 10 mappedRDD partitions
  4. Given shuffle files lost → rerun Stage 1: 10 + 10 + 10 = 30 partition computations, then 2 reduce tasks
  5. Given 4 partitions × 1,000 pairs → groupByKey shuffles 4,000 records
  6. Given 50 distinct words per partition → reduceByKey shuffles at most 4 × 50 = 200, i.e. 1 − 200/4,000 = 95% less
  7. Given numPartitions = 4 and a key with hash 17 → 17 % 4 = 1, so partition 1

Variants

  • Hashes 42, 10, 23, 31 with numPartitions = 4 → partitions 2, 2, 3, 3
  • 3 of 12 partitions lost, all narrow → 3 / 12 = 25% of the work redone
  • 8 partitions × 500 pairs, 20 distinct keys each → groupByKey 4,000; reduceByKey ≤ 160; 96% less
  • GraySort ratios: 72 / 23 ≈ 3.1× faster; 2,100 / 206 ≈ 10.2× fewer nodes; (72 × 2,100) / (23 × 206) = 151,200 / 4,738 ≈ 32× better efficiency per node
Traps
  • reduceByKey is still wide. It shuffles, only less data.
  • coalesce is narrow (no shuffle); repartition is wide.
  • join is narrow only when both RDDs are co-partitioned on the key.
  • Part 2 slide 2 says “3.5× fewer machines”, but 2,100 / 206 ≈ 10.2×, as slide 14 says.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
A wide dependency is one where an output partition needs data from many input partitions, which forces a shuffle and a new stage.
3 marks
In a narrow dependency (map, filter) each output partition needs at most one input partition, so steps pipeline in one stage. In a wide dependency (groupByKey, join) data is shuffled across the network and a new stage starts. Example: load → filter → map is Stage 1; reduceByKey starts Stage 2.
5 marks
Narrow dependency: each output partition depends on at most one input partition (map, filter, union, coalesce). No data moves, steps pipeline in one stage, and a lost partition is rebuilt from one parent. Wide dependency: an output partition depends on many input partitions (groupByKey, reduceByKey, join, distinct). Data is shuffled across the network, a stage boundary appears, and recovery may need all parent partitions. Example: load → filter(amount > 1000) → map(fee) = Stage 1; reduceByKey(sum) → saveAsTextFile = Stage 2. Tuning: prefer reduceByKey, which combines locally (4,000 vs 200 records in the slide-style example). Limitation: sorts and un-partitioned joins always need a shuffle.
Practise this topic in 10 formats
MCQ
Which transformation does NOT cause a shuffle?
  • (a) reduceByKey
  • (b) groupByKey
  • (c) filter
  • (d) distinct
show answer
(c) filter
filter is narrow: each output partition comes from one input partition.
Fill in the blank
A wide dependency requires a ______, which creates a ______ boundary.
show answer
shuffle; stage
Define / list
Define a narrow dependency and list three narrow transformations.
show answer
Each output partition depends on at most one input partition. map, filter, flatMap (also union, coalesce).
Explain the steps
How does Spark split a DAG into stages?
show answer
Walk the DAG from the action back to the input; group consecutive narrow steps into one pipelined stage; cut a new stage at each wide (shuffle) step; run each stage as one task per partition.
Why / justify
Why should you prefer reduceByKey over groupByKey?
show answer
reduceByKey combines values locally in each partition before the shuffle, so far fewer records cross the network; groupByKey ships every pair.
Distinguish
Distinguish coalesce and repartition.
show answer
coalesce reduces partitions without a shuffle (narrow). repartition reshuffles all data (wide).
Scenario
A word count over 4 partitions (1,000 pairs each, 50 distinct words per partition) uses groupByKey and the shuffle is slow.
i) Is groupByKey narrow or wide? ii) How many records does it shuffle? iii) What should replace it? iv) At most how many records would that shuffle?
show answer
i) Wide. ii) 4 × 1,000 = 4,000. iii) reduceByKey. iv) 4 × 50 = 200 (95% fewer).
One-step calc
numPartitions = 4. A key's hashCode is 23. Which partition does it go to?
show answer
23 % 4 = 3 → partition 3.
Full calc
A job input → filter → map → groupByKey runs on 10 partitions. The Executor with output partitions 3 and 7 dies and its shuffle files are lost. How many partition computations are redone, and how does this compare with an all-narrow job?
show answer
Stage 1 reruns on all 10: 10 input reads + 10 filter + 10 map = 30, then 2 reduce tasks (32). All-narrow: only partitions 3 and 7 at 4 levels = 2 × 4 = 8, i.e. 20% of the work.
Long answer
Explain narrow and wide dependencies, how they form stages, and their effect on fault recovery and performance.
show answer
Use the 5-mark answer, draw the 1-to-1 vs all-to-all partition diagram, and cite the GraySort lesson that shuffle is the bottleneck.

DataFrames, Spark SQL and the ecosystem

Facts
  • DataFrame: a distributed collection organised into named columns with a schema, like a database table or a distributed Pandas DataFrame.
  • Built on RDDs internally; every DataFrame operation compiles to an RDD plan when it runs.
  • Catalyst optimizer rewrites query plans: predicate pushdown, join reordering, constant folding.
  • DataFrames are 10–100× faster than RDDs for structured data. Use them unless you need low-level control.
  • Supports SQL and method chaining. Reads Parquet, ORC, CSV, JSON, Hive tables, JDBC.
  • On Spark Core: Spark SQL, MLlib (ML), Structured Streaming, GraphX (graphs). HBase is a storage source, not a component.
How it works
  1. Load the data: df = spark.read.parquet(path)
  2. Register a SQL view: df.createOrReplaceTempView("health_records")
  3. Run the query: spark.sql("SELECT ... GROUP BY ...")
  4. Catalyst rewrites the plan, e.g. pushes the WHERE filter down to the scan
  5. The optimised plan runs as RDD stages on the Executors
  6. Write the result: result.write.mode("overwrite").csv(path)
Why · pros · limits
  • The schema tells Catalyst what each column is, so it can optimise the query; raw RDD lambdas are opaque to it.
  • One engine covers SQL, ML, streaming and graphs, so there is no separate system per workload.
  • MLlib trains iteratively on cached data, which is where Spark is strongest.
  • Limitation: DataFrames give less low-level control than RDDs.
Compare
DataFrameRDD
StructureNamed columns + schemaRecords with no schema
OptimiserCatalystDAG scheduler only
Speed (structured data)10–100× fasterBaseline
APISQL + method chainingFunctional (map, filter, lambdas)
Use whenDefault for structured dataLow-level control needed
DoesExample
Spark SQLStructured queries on DataFramesDistrict-wise disease counts
MLlibDistributed ML in memoryk-means, logistic regression, random forest
Structured StreamingStream as an ever-growing table; watermarks, exactly-once with KafkaReal-time events
GraphXGraph algorithmsPageRank, connected components
HBaseNot Spark: a NoSQL store Spark can readData source
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
analysts run SELECT ... GROUP BY over Parquet health recordsSpark SQL on a DataFrame
a fraud model is trained with logistic regression across the clusterMLlib
live payment events, with late data handled by watermarksStructured Streaming
ranking pages by their linksGraphX (PageRank)
a NoSQL table that Spark reads fromHBase: a data source, not part of Spark
Traps
  • HBase is not a Spark ecosystem component.
  • DataFrames do not replace the engine: they compile down to RDDs.
  • Catalyst optimises DataFrames/SQL; RDD code goes through the DAG scheduler only.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
A DataFrame is a distributed table with named columns and a schema, optimised by Catalyst.
3 marks
A DataFrame organises distributed data into named columns with a schema, like a SQL table. Because Catalyst can see the schema and the whole query, it rewrites the plan (e.g. predicate pushdown), making DataFrames 10–100× faster than RDDs on structured data. Example: spark.sql("SELECT district, COUNT(*) ... GROUP BY district").
5 marks
A DataFrame is a distributed collection organised into named columns with a schema, built on RDDs. It supports SQL and method chaining and reads Parquet, ORC, CSV, JSON, Hive and JDBC. Flow: read Parquet → register a temp view → spark.sql query → Catalyst optimises (predicate pushdown, join reordering, constant folding) → runs as RDD stages → write CSV. Example: district-wise confirmed disease counts for 2024, top 20. Around it sit Spark SQL, MLlib, Structured Streaming and GraphX on Spark Core, with storage such as HDFS, S3 or HBase underneath. Limitation: less low-level control than RDDs.

Chapter 3 · Week 7Hive, HiveQL and SQL-on-Hadoop

Hive lets analysts query data on HDFS with SQL-like HiveQL, which it compiles into MapReduce, Tez or Spark jobs, using a Metastore that records every table's schema and location. The chapter covers how Hive runs a query, schema-on-read, partitioning and bucketing for speed, Presto for interactive queries, and Spotify's Discover Weekly pipeline as a case study.

Exam question pool 21 questions from the slides · click a question for its answer

MCQ
Q1. In Hive, where is table schema metadata (column names, types, partition info) stored?
  • (a) HDFS NameNode
  • (b) Hive Metastore (relational DB such as MySQL)
  • (c) HBase
  • (d) ZooKeeper
show answer
(b) Hive Metastore (relational DB such as MySQL)
The NameNode holds HDFS block metadata, not table schemas.
MCQ
Q2. What does "schema-on-read" mean in the context of Hive?
  • (a) Schema is validated when data is inserted into the table
  • (b) Schema is applied when data is queried, not when it is stored on HDFS
  • (c) Schema is stored separately from data in HDFS metadata
  • (d) Schema is inferred automatically from column names in the file header
show answer
(b) Schema is applied when data is queried, not when it is stored on HDFS
(a) describes schema-on-write, how MySQL works.
MCQ
Q3. Which Hive optimization technique physically organises data into separate HDFS sub-directories by a column's value to avoid full table scans?
  • (a) Bucketing
  • (b) Indexing
  • (c) Partitioning
  • (d) Compression
show answer
(c) Partitioning
Bucketing splits files inside a partition; it does not skip directories.
MCQ
Q4. Presto's primary performance advantage over Hive+MapReduce for interactive queries is:
  • (a) Presto supports more SQL functions than HiveQL
  • (b) Presto has better native integration with HBase and Cassandra
  • (c) Presto executes queries entirely in memory without writing intermediate results to disk
  • (d) Presto has native Python UDF support
show answer
(c) Presto executes queries entirely in memory without writing intermediate results to disk
Hive+MR writes to HDFS between every stage.
True/False
1. HiveQL supports full ACID transactions on all table types, just like MySQL.
show answer
False. Corrected: HiveQL supports ACID only on ACID-enabled transactional ORC tables; MySQL (InnoDB engine) gives full ACID: INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and ROLLBACK on any row.
True/False
2. Partitioning in Hive reduces query time by eliminating irrelevant data partitions from the HDFS scan.
show answer
True. A WHERE filter on the partition key makes Hive read only the matching sub-directories (partition pruning).
True/False
3. Hive directly executes a HiveQL query on HDFS without any intermediate translation step.
show answer
False. Corrected: Hive compiles HiveQL (parse, analyse, plan, optimise) into MapReduce, Tez or Spark jobs, which then read HDFS.
True/False
4. Bucketing in Hive is particularly useful for optimising JOIN operations between two large tables.
show answer
True. Both tables bucketed on the join key with the same N let Hive join bucket i with bucket i, with no full shuffle.
Descriptive
D1. Explain the Hive architecture: what is the role of HiveServer2, the Driver, and the Metastore? How is a HiveQL query compiled into executable jobs? Draw the component diagram.
show answer
  • HiveServer2: JDBC/ODBC front door; authenticates users, accepts HiveQL from Beeline, BI tools, JDBC.
  • Driver: Parser → Semantic Analyser → Logical Planner → Optimizer → Physical Plan Generator; manages the query lifecycle.
  • Metastore: MySQL/PostgreSQL catalogue of schemas, types, partitions, HDFS paths.

Compilation: parse to AST → check names and types in the Metastore → logical plan (Filter, Join, GroupBy, OrderBy) → optimise (partition pruning) → MR/Tez/Spark jobs → submit to YARN; results to HDFS and client.

Diagram:

Descriptive
D2. Differentiate between partitioning and bucketing in Hive. When would you choose each? Describe a scenario in Bangladesh e-commerce data where both are used together.
show answer
PartitioningBucketing
UnitDirectory per valueFixed N files, hash(col) % N
BenefitPruning for WHERE filtersBucket map join
ColumnLow cardinality (date, division)High cardinality key (customer_id)

Choose partitioning when queries filter on a few-valued column; bucketing when large tables are joined on a many-valued key.

Scenario: a Bangladeshi e-commerce orders table, PARTITIONED BY (order_date, division) and CLUSTERED BY (customer_id) INTO 32 BUCKETS. The Dhaka daily report reads one folder; the join with customers (also 32 buckets on customer_id) pairs bucket i with i.

Descriptive
D3. What is "schema-on-read"? What are its advantages and disadvantages compared to "schema-on-write" in a traditional RDBMS such as MySQL? Give one example where schema-on-read causes a data quality problem.
show answer

Schema-on-read: Hive applies column types at query time; loading is a raw file copy. MySQL (schema-on-write) checks each row at INSERT.

AdvantagesDisadvantages
Fast load, no validationSlower queries (parse every time)
Load before the schema is known; many schemas on the same files; no ETL at ingestBad data silent: becomes NULL
Open formats (ORC, Parquet, CSV, JSON, Avro)Unfit for OLTP (banking, orders)

Example: abc in a DOUBLE amount column. MySQL rejects the INSERT; Hive stores it and returns NULL, so SUM(amount) under-reports revenue with no warning. Fix: Great Expectations or dbt tests downstream.

Analytical
A1. Partitioning & Bucketing Design: You have a 5 TB e-commerce transaction table with columns: (txn_id, user_id, product_id, amount, country, txn_date). Design a complete partitioning and bucketing strategy to optimise: – Daily revenue reports filtered by country and txn_date – JOINs between this table and a 10 M-row user profile table (keyed on user_id). Write the complete CREATE TABLE DDL. Justify why you chose a specific number of buckets and which column(s) you partitioned on.
show answer

Partition on txn_date, country (low cardinality, used in report filters). Bucket on user_id (high-cardinality join key), same N on both tables for a bucket map join. Never partition on user_id or txn_id.

Bucket count (assume 3 years, 5 countries):

Partitions3 × 365 × 5 = 5,475
Per partition5,000 GB / 5,475 ≈ 0.913 GB
8 buckets≈ 114 MB/file (near 128 MB block), 43,800 files
32 buckets≈ 28.5 MB/file, 175,200 files: too small
CREATE TABLE transactions (
  txn_id BIGINT, user_id BIGINT,
  product_id BIGINT, amount DOUBLE)
PARTITIONED BY (txn_date STRING, country STRING)
CLUSTERED BY (user_id) SORTED BY (user_id) INTO 8 BUCKETS
STORED AS ORC;

CREATE TABLE user_profile (
  user_id BIGINT, name STRING, city STRING)
CLUSTERED BY (user_id) SORTED BY (user_id) INTO 8 BUCKETS
STORED AS ORC;

Result: a one-day, one-country report reads 1 of 5,475 partitions (≈ 0.018%); the join pairs bucket i with i.

Analytical
A2. Query Compilation to MapReduce: Write HiveQL to find the top 5 products by total revenue in 2023. Then trace how Hive compiles this query into MapReduce stages: what is emitted by the Map phase, what is the shuffle key, and what does the Reduce phase compute? How would switching the execution engine from MapReduce to Tez change the execution plan?
show answer
SELECT product_id, SUM(amount) AS total_revenue
FROM transactions
WHERE YEAR(txn_date) = 2023
GROUP BY product_id
ORDER BY total_revenue DESC
LIMIT 5;
Job 1: aggregateJob 2: sort + limit
Map emits(product_id, amount) for 2023 rows; combiner gives partial sums(total_revenue, product_id); each mapper keeps its top 5
Shuffle keyproduct_idtotal_revenue DESC, to one reducer
ReduceSUM(amount) per product, written to HDFSFirst 5 rows

Tiny check: (P1,100), (P2,50), (P1,30), (P3,80), (P2,20) → P1 130, P3 80, P2 70.

Tez: one DAG, Map 1 → Reducer 2 → Reducer 3. No HDFS write between the jobs, one submission, container reuse. Same result, faster.

Discussion
1. Spotify's co-occurrence query runs GROUP BY song_a, song_b over 2 billion playlist rows. Estimate the output size if there are 30M songs. Propose a partitioning + bucketing scheme for the playlist_songs Hive table that would reduce the shuffle size for this query. [CLO4 – Apply]
show answer

Claim: output is far below the 30M-song bound. Bound: 30M × (30M − 1) / 2 ≈ 4.5 × 10^14 pairs. Realistic (assume 20 songs per playlist): 10^8 playlists × 190 = 1.9 × 10^10 pairs, ≤ 304 GB at 16 B/row, much less after HAVING > 5.

Scheme: PARTITIONED BY (week), CLUSTERED BY (playlist_id) SORTED BY (playlist_id, song_id) INTO 1024 BUCKETS. For: the self-join runs inside each bucket with no shuffle, and weekly partitions cut 8 h to 2 h. Against: it does not shrink the GROUP BY shuffle on song pairs; rely on map-side aggregation there.

Discussion
2. The ALS collaborative filtering model is a matrix factorisation over a 75M-user × 30M-song matrix. At 4 bytes per float and rank k = 50, calculate the model size and explain whether it fits in a 5,000-node YARN cluster's aggregate RAM. [CLO4 – Analyse]
show answer

Size: (75M + 30M) × 50 × 4 B = 21 GB (users 15 GB + songs 6 GB).

Fits? Assume 64 GB/node: 5,000 × 64 GB = 320 TB; 21 GB ≈ 0.0066%, about 4.2 MB per node. For: it fits easily, even on one large server; the dense 9 PB matrix would not. Against: training also holds the interaction data, shuffled factor blocks and JVM overhead, several times the model size, but still well within the cluster.

Discussion
3. Spotify uses Luigi while other companies use Apache Airflow. Compare the two on: dependency declaration style, backfill capability, and UI/observability. Which would you choose for a 100-step daily pipeline? [CLO3 – Evaluate]
show answer

Claim: choose Airflow for a 100-step daily pipeline. Luigi: target-based requires()/output(), skips finished outputs, backfill by date parameters, simple UI. Airflow: DAG with a scheduler, built-in catchup/backfill, rich UI with logs, retries and alerts.

For: at 100 steps you need to see which step failed, read its logs and re-run past days, which Airflow gives out of the box. Against: Luigi is lighter and its skip-if-output-exists model fits file-based Hadoop pipelines like Spotify's.

Live Exercise
Task. Task (25 minutes, pairs): Simulate a Hive session using PySpark's SQL interface (which reads the same Hive Metastore). Dataset: a 10,000-row synthetic play_events CSV (generate with the skeleton below).
from pyspark.sql import SparkSession
import random, datetime

spark = SparkSession.builder \
        .appName("HiveDemo") \
        .getOrCreate()
spark.setLogLevel("WARN")

# Generate synthetic data
rows = []
for i in range(10_000):
    rows.append({
      "user_id":    random.randint(1, 500),
      "song_id":    random.randint(1, 200),
      "playlist_id":random.randint(1, 1000),
      "dt":         "2024-10-01"
    })

df = spark.createDataFrame(rows)
df.createOrReplaceTempView("play_events")

# Co-occurrence query
result = spark.sql("""
    SELECT a.song_id AS song_a,
           b.song_id AS song_b,
           COUNT(*) AS co_count
    FROM play_events a
    JOIN play_events b
      ON a.playlist_id = b.playlist_id
     AND a.song_id < b.song_id
    GROUP BY a.song_id, b.song_id
    HAVING COUNT(*) > 2
    ORDER BY co_count DESC
""")
result.show(10)
show answer

Write the data as a real partitioned table, then time and inspect the queries.

import time
random.seed(42)  # rows as in the skeleton, dt from 2 dates

  .format("parquet").saveAsTable("play_events_part")
t0 = time.time()
spark.sql(cooc_sql).show(10)      # skeleton query on play_events_part, with a.dt = b.dt = '2024-10-01'
print(time.time() - t0)
songs = spark.createDataFrame(
  [(i, "genre" + str(i % 10)) for i in range(1, 201)], ["song_id", "genre"])
songs.createOrReplaceTempView("songs")   # 200-row dimension table
spark.conf.set("spark.sql.autoBroadcastJoinThreshold", -1)
hint_sql = """SELECT /*+ BROADCAST(s) */ s.genre, COUNT(*)
  FROM play_events_part p JOIN songs s ON p.song_id = s.song_id
  WHERE p.dt = '2024-10-01' GROUP BY s.genre"""
spark.sql(hint_sql).explain(mode="formatted")

Topic cards 6 topics · everything the pool tests

Hive architecture and query compilation

Facts
  • Apache Hive (Facebook, 2008) is a data warehouse layer on Hadoop. You write HiveQL, a SQL-like language; Hive turns it into distributed jobs.
  • HiveServer2: the JDBC/ODBC server. It authenticates users and accepts HiveQL from Beeline, BI tools (Tableau, Power BI) and JDBC drivers.
  • Driver: the query pipeline Parser → Semantic Analyser → Logical Planner → Query Optimizer → Physical Plan Generator.
  • Metastore: a relational database (MySQL/PostgreSQL) holding table schemas, column types, partition values with HDFS paths, bucket counts, SerDe, statistics, privileges.
  • SerDe (Serializer/Deserializer): turns raw bytes on HDFS (ORC, Parquet, CSV, JSON, Avro) into Hive rows and back.
  • Execution engine is pluggable: MapReduce (legacy default), Tez (DAG of tasks, faster) or Spark (SET hive.execution.engine=spark). HDFS holds data: table = directory.
How it works
  1. The client (Beeline, JDBC, Tableau) sends HiveQL to HiveServer2, which authenticates the user.
  2. The parser turns the query text into an abstract syntax tree (AST), a tree of the query's parts.
  3. Semantic analysis looks up every table and column in the Metastore and checks types (e.g. user_id is BIGINT, dt is a partition column).
  4. The logical planner builds operators: Filter → Join → GroupBy → OrderBy.
  5. The optimizer adds partition pruning and map-side aggregation; the physical plan generator turns the plan into MR, Tez or Spark jobs.
  6. The execution engine submits the jobs to YARN in dependency order; they read and write HDFS.
  7. The result goes back to the client through HiveServer2.
Why · pros · limits
  • Before Hive, a simple GROUP BY was a 100+ line Java MapReduce program and an answer took days. Analysts know SQL, not Java.
  • Declare, don't program: the analyst writes SELECT and Hive writes the distributed job.
  • The Metastore keeps the schema apart from the files, so Spark, Presto/Trino and Impala read the same tables. It is Hive's most lasting contribution.
  • If the Metastore is lost, no table can be queried even though HDFS data is intact. Production runs it highly available on replicated MySQL.
  • Limitation: on MapReduce every stage writes to HDFS, so even simple queries take minutes.
Compare
MapReduceTez
Plan shapeChain of separate MR jobsOne DAG of tasks
Between stagesWrites to HDFS, next job reads it backPasses data directly
Top-5 products query2 job submissions1 DAG: Map 1 → Reducer 2 → Reducer 3
Start-up costNew containers per jobContainers can be reused
ResultSameSame; only plan and speed change
Hive MetastoreHDFS NameNode
StoresTable schemas, types, partitions, HDFS paths, SerDeFile-to-block map and block locations
What it isRelational DB (MySQL/PostgreSQL)HDFS master daemon
If lostNo table can be queriedNo file can be found
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
Tableau connects over JDBC to run HiveQLHiveServer2
a query fails with 'column not found' before any job startsSemantic analysis against the Metastore (Driver)
every HDFS file is intact but no table can be queriedMetastore failure
one SET command makes the same query faster with no HDFS writes between stagesEngine switched from MapReduce to Tez or Spark
each mapper sums counts per country before the shuffleMap-side aggregation (combiner)
Calculation

P2 slide 10: SELECT country, COUNT(*) FROM logs GROUP BY country, 50 million rows, 200 countries. Without map-side aggregation the mappers emit one (country, 1) row per input row: 50,000,000. With it they emit one partial count per country: 200. Reduction = 50,000,000 / 200 = 250,000× less shuffle data.

Step by step

  1. Given 50,000,000 rows and no combiner → shuffle rows = 50,000,000
  2. Given 200 countries and one mapper with map-side aggregation → shuffle rows = 200
  3. Given 50,000,000 and 200 → reduction = 50,000,000 / 200 = 250,000×
  4. Given M mappers that each see all 200 countries → shuffle = M × 200 rows, reduction = 50,000,000 / (M × 200)

Variants

  • 120 million rows, 400 regions: 120,000,000 / 400 = 300,000×
  • 50 million rows, 10 mappers each seeing all 200 countries: shuffle = 2,000 rows, reduction = 25,000×
Traps
  • Hive never runs HiveQL directly on HDFS. It compiles the query to MR, Tez or Spark jobs first.
  • Schemas live in the Metastore, not the NameNode. The NameNode only knows files and blocks.
  • Switching to Tez changes the plan and the speed, never the answer.
  • ORDER BY needs one reducer for a global order, so the final sort stage is a bottleneck.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Hive is a data warehouse layer on Hadoop that compiles SQL-like HiveQL queries into MapReduce, Tez or Spark jobs over files on HDFS.
3 marks
HiveServer2 accepts and authenticates queries; the Driver parses, checks, plans and optimises them; the Metastore (MySQL/PostgreSQL) supplies schemas and HDFS paths. The physical plan runs as MR, Tez or Spark jobs on YARN. Example: GROUP BY country becomes one MR job where mappers emit (country, 1) and reducers sum.
5 marks

Hive turns HiveQL into distributed jobs over HDFS.

Structure: client → HiveServer2 → Driver ↔ Metastore; Driver → execution engine (MR/Tez/Spark on YARN) → HDFS.

  1. Parse to AST.
  2. Semantic analysis against the Metastore.
  3. Logical plan (Filter, Join, GroupBy, OrderBy).
  4. Optimise (partition pruning, map-side aggregation) and build the physical plan.
  5. Submit jobs to YARN; results return via HiveServer2.

Example: 50M rows grouped by 200 countries; map-side aggregation cuts the shuffle 250,000×.

Limitation: MR writes to HDFS between stages, so queries take minutes.

show answer

Explain the steps

show answer
  1. Parse text to AST.
  2. Semantic analysis: resolve tables, columns, types in the Metastore.
  3. Logical plan of operators.
  4. Optimise, then generate MR/Tez/Spark jobs.
  5. Submit to YARN in dependency order; return results.
Why / justify
Why is the Metastore called the control plane of a Hadoop data lake?
show answer
Every engine (Hive, Spark, Presto, Impala) asks it where a table's files are and how to parse them. Without it no query runs, even with HDFS intact.
Distinguish
Distinguish MapReduce and Tez as Hive execution engines.
show answer
MR runs a chain of separate jobs and writes to HDFS between them. Tez runs one DAG and passes data directly between stages, with container reuse, so it is faster. Same HiveQL, same result.
Scenario

i) Which component accepts the Tableau connection? ii) Which component has failed? iii) Why do the intact files not help? iv) How should production guard against this?
show answer
i) HiveServer2. ii) The Metastore. iii) Hive needs the Metastore to know where a table's files are and how to parse them. iv) Run the Metastore highly available on a replicated MySQL cluster with automatic failover.

show answer
80,000,000 / 400 = 200,000×
Full calc

show answer
Without: 30,000,000 rows. With: 4 × 150 = 600 rows. Reduction = 30,000,000 / 600 = 50,000×.
Long answer

show answer
Use the 5-mark answer as the skeleton. Diagram: Beeline/JDBC → HiveServer2 → Driver; Driver ↔ Metastore (schema lookup); Driver → Execution Engine → HDFS. Trace: parse; check logs and country in the Metastore; plan Scan → GroupBy; one MR job: map emits (country, 1) with a combiner, shuffle key = country, reduce sums. Result returns through HiveServer2.

Schema-on-read and HiveQL vs SQL

Facts
  • Schema-on-read: Hive applies column types at query (SELECT) time. Loading just copies raw files to HDFS.
  • Schema-on-write: an RDBMS such as MySQL checks each row at INSERT time and rejects bad rows.
  • Under schema-on-read, text abc in a DOUBLE amount column is stored, and the query returns NULL.
  • HiveQL ACID is limited: UPDATE/DELETE work only on ACID-enabled transactional ORC tables.
  • Hive has no B-tree indexes (it uses partitions and buckets) and supports only non-correlated sub-queries.
  • Latency: SQL milliseconds; Hive minutes on MR, seconds on Tez/Spark. COUNT(*) on 1 TB can take 5 to 10 minutes.
How it works
  1. CREATE TABLE writes column names, types and the SerDe to the Metastore. No data is checked.
  2. LOAD DATA moves the file into the table's HDFS directory unchanged.
  3. At SELECT time the SerDe reads each record and splits it into fields.
  4. Each field is cast to its declared type. A failed cast becomes NULL.
  5. Aggregates such as SUM skip NULLs, so a wrong total comes back with no error.
Why · pros · limits
  • Fast loading: a plain file copy, no validation cost.
  • Flexible: load raw web logs before the final schema is known, and define several tables over the same files. No ETL at ingest.
  • Open file formats: ORC, Parquet, CSV, JSON, Avro.
  • Limitation: every query parses and checks the data again, so queries are slower.
  • Limitation: bad data stays silent. Add downstream checks (Great Expectations, dbt tests).
Compare
Schema-on-write (MySQL)Schema-on-read (Hive)
Schema enforcedAt INSERT / LOADAt SELECT
Invalid dataRejected immediatelyStored; NULL at query
Write speedSlower (validation)Fast (raw file copy)
Query speedFaster (pre-validated)Slower (parse each query)
Schemas per dataOne per tableMany over the same files
Use caseOLTP: orders, bankingOLAP: log analysis, batch reports
SQL (RDBMS)HiveQL
ACIDFull: INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, ROLLBACKOnly ACID-mode ORC tables
UPDATE / DELETEAny row, any timeOnly transactional tables
IndexingB-tree, hash, bitmapPartitions + buckets
LatencyMillisecondsMinutes (MR) / seconds (Tez, Spark)
ScaleTerabytesPetabytes
WorkloadOLTP (real-time reads/writes)OLAP (batch analytics)
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
raw clickstream logs are loaded today; the schema will be decided next monthSchema-on-read
a revenue report is too low with no error; some amount values were textSchema-on-read silent NULLs
malformed money transfers must be refused the moment they arriveSchema-on-write (RDBMS, OLTP)
an analyst expects a millisecond reply from Hive COUNT(*) on 1 TBMisconception: Hive is batch; use Presto or Spark SQL
someone runs UPDATE on a plain CSV Hive tableNot allowed: needs an ACID transactional ORC table
Traps
  • Schema-on-read does not mean the schema is inferred. You still write CREATE TABLE (MCQ 2 option d).
  • The schema is stored separately under both models. The key word is when it is applied.
  • HiveQL looks like SQL but runs as batch jobs: minutes, not milliseconds.
  • Hive ACID is not on all tables, only transactional ORC tables.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Schema-on-read means Hive applies the table's column types when data is queried, not when it is loaded.
3 marks
Schema-on-read (Hive) stores files as they are and applies types at SELECT; schema-on-write (MySQL) checks each row at INSERT and rejects bad rows. Loading is faster and more flexible with schema-on-read, but bad values turn into NULL silently. Example: abc in a DOUBLE column is rejected by MySQL but returns NULL in Hive.
5 marks

Definition: schema applied at query time; load is a raw file copy.

  1. CREATE TABLE stores the schema in the Metastore.
  2. LOAD moves files to HDFS unchecked.
  3. At SELECT the SerDe parses fields and casts types; failures become NULL.

For: fast load, no ETL at ingest, many schemas on one file set, open formats. Suits OLAP.

Example: a transaction file has abc in amount; SUM(amount) skips it and the revenue total is too low.

Limitation: slower queries and silent data errors; add Great Expectations or dbt tests.

  • (b) The row is stored and the value returns NULL at query time
  • (c) Hive changes the column type to STRING

Schema-on-read: nothing is checked at load; a failed cast becomes NULL.

show answer

Define / list
Define schema-on-read and list three of its advantages.
show answer
Schema applied at query time, not at load. Advantages: fast load (file copy), flexible (load before the schema is known, many schemas over the same files), open formats (ORC, Parquet, CSV, JSON, Avro).
Explain the steps
Describe what happens to a bad value from LOAD to SUM under schema-on-read.
show answer
LOAD copies the file unchecked → SELECT makes the SerDe parse fields → the cast to DOUBLE fails → the value becomes NULL → SUM skips it, so the total is low with no error.
Why / justify
Why is schema-on-write still preferred for banking transactions?
show answer
Banking is OLTP: each row must be valid the moment it is written, and bad rows must be refused, not discovered later as NULLs. It also needs full ACID and millisecond updates, which Hive lacks.
Distinguish
Distinguish HiveQL from SQL on ACID, indexing and latency.
show answer
ACID: SQL full; HiveQL only on transactional ORC tables. Indexing: SQL B-tree/hash/bitmap; Hive partitions and buckets. Latency: SQL milliseconds; Hive minutes on MR, seconds on Tez/Spark.
Scenario
A mobile wallet dumps daily transaction CSVs into Hive. Finance notices last month's revenue is 3% below the bank statement, and no job reported an error.
i) Which Hive property explains the silence? ii) What did the bad values become? iii) Would MySQL have behaved the same? iv) Suggest a fix.
show answer
i) Schema-on-read. ii) NULL, which SUM skipped. iii) No: schema-on-write rejects the INSERT. iv) Add downstream validation (Great Expectations, dbt tests) or reject bad rows in an ETL step.

show answer
Use the 5-mark answer plus the schema-on-write contrast table (when enforced, invalid data, write speed, query speed, schemas per data, use case).

Partitioning and partition pruning

Facts
  • Partitioning stores table data in separate HDFS sub-directories by column value, e.g. /data/sales/year=2024/month=01/.
  • Partition pruning: when WHERE filters on the partition key, Hive reads only the matching sub-directories and skips the rest.
  • Good key: a low-cardinality column (few distinct values) used often in WHERE: date, country, region, status.
  • Never partition on high-cardinality columns (user_id, txn_id): 100 M users means 100 M directories, crashing the NameNode.
  • DDL: PARTITIONED BY (year INT, month INT). Partition columns are not repeated in the column list.
  • Dynamic insert needs hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true and mode=nonstrict; partition columns go last in SELECT.
How it works
  1. Create the table with PARTITIONED BY (year INT, month INT).
  2. Load: a static insert names the values, PARTITION (year=2024, month=1); a dynamic insert takes them from the last SELECT columns.
  3. Hive writes each partition's files to its own sub-directory and records the value and path in the Metastore.
  4. A query arrives with WHERE year=2024 AND month=1.
  5. The optimizer checks the Metastore's partition list and keeps only matching paths.
  6. Mappers read only year=2024/month=01/; every other directory is skipped.
Why · pros · limits
  • Avoids a full table scan, so data read and job time drop in proportion.
  • Pruning uses the partition list in the Metastore, so Hive need not list every file.
  • Limitation: it helps only when the query filters on the partition column.
  • Limitation: too many partitions create millions of directories and small files that overload the NameNode.
Compare
Static partition insertDynamic partition insert
Partition valuesWritten in PARTITION (year=2024, month=1)Taken from the last SELECT columns
SettingsNonedynamic.partition=true, mode=nonstrict
Partitions per statementOneAll at once (e.g. 36 months)
Typical slipWrong value typedColumns out of order: values mis-assigned silently
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
daily reports filter on date but scan the whole tablePartition by date
a designer proposes partitioning a 50M-customer table by customer_idPartition explosion; bucket on customer_id instead
36 months are loaded into their partitions in one INSERTDynamic partition insert
after a dynamic insert, month values sit in the year= foldersPartition columns out of order in SELECT
EXPLAIN shows PartitionFilters: [dt = 2024-10-01] in the scanPartition pruning
Calculation

P2 slide 11: play_events partitioned by dt (365 days) and country (200). P_total = 365 × 200 = 73,000. The query reads dt='2024-10-01' for BD, IN, US: P_read = 3. Fraction read = 3 / 73,000 ≈ 0.0041%; 72,997 partitions skipped. P1 slide 20: one month of 36 = 1/36 ≈ 2.78%.

Step by step

  1. Given 365 dates and 200 countries → P_total = 365 × 200 = 73,000
  2. Given dt = one day and country IN (BD, IN, US) → P_read = 1 × 3 = 3
  3. Given 3 of 73,000 (equal-size partitions) → fraction = 3 / 73,000 ≈ 0.0041%
  4. Given 73,000 total and 3 read → skipped = 72,997

Variants

  • 36 months, query one month: 1 / 36 ≈ 2.78% read
  • 24 months × 50 countries, one month for 2 countries: 2 / 1,200 ≈ 0.167% read
Traps
  • Partitioning splits by directory; bucketing splits into files inside a partition. Only partitioning skips directories.
  • In a dynamic insert, partition columns must be last in SELECT and in PARTITION order, or Hive assigns values wrongly without error.
  • Pruning needs a filter on the partition column itself. A filter on any other column scans every partition.
  • The fraction formula assumes equal-size partitions. Say so.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Partitioning stores a Hive table in separate HDFS sub-directories by the value of a column, so queries filtering on it read only matching directories.
3 marks
Each partition value gets its own sub-directory, e.g. year=2024/month=01/. A WHERE filter on the partition key lets Hive skip every other directory (partition pruning). Example: one month out of 36 reads 1/36 ≈ 2.78% of the data.
5 marks

Definition: table split into HDFS sub-directories by a low-cardinality column.

  1. PARTITIONED BY (year INT, month INT).
  2. Load by static or dynamic insert (partition columns last).
  3. Metastore records each value and path.
  4. Query on the key reads only matching folders.

Example: 365 days × 200 countries = 73,000 partitions; one day for 3 countries reads 3 of them (≈ 0.0041%).

Limitation: a high-cardinality key (user_id) creates millions of directories and crashes the NameNode.

Practise this topic in 10 formats
MCQ

  • (b) sale_date
  • (c) txn_id
  • (d) amount

Fill in the blank
Skipping all partitions that do not match the WHERE filter is called partition ________.
show answer
pruning
Define / list
Define partitioning and list four typical partition columns.
show answer
Storing table data in separate HDFS sub-directories by column value. Typical: date, country, region, status.
Explain the steps
Explain how a dynamic partition insert loads all months at once.
show answer
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true and mode=nonstrict → INSERT INTO sales PARTITION (year, month) → SELECT the data columns, then YEAR(sale_date) AS year, MONTH(sale_date) AS month last → Hive creates one directory per (year, month) value.
Why / justify
Why must you never partition on user_id?
show answer
It has millions of distinct values: 100 M users would make 100 M directories, overloading the NameNode's metadata. Bucket on user_id instead.
Distinguish
Distinguish static and dynamic partition inserts.
show answer
Static: you write the values in PARTITION (year=2024, month=1); one partition per statement. Dynamic: Hive takes values from the last SELECT columns and loads many partitions at once; needs the dynamic-partition settings.
Scenario
A ride-share firm keeps 3 years of trips in one Hive table. A query for last Tuesday's Dhaka trips scans all 3 years.
i) What feature is missing? ii) Which columns would you choose? iii) What is the read fraction for one day of 1,095 if city is ignored? iv) Name one column you must not use.
show answer
i) Partitioning (and so pruning). ii) trip_date and city. iii) 1 / 1,095 ≈ 0.091%. iv) trip_id or rider_id (high cardinality).
One-step calc
A table has 1,200 partitions of equal size. A query matches 6. What fraction is read?
show answer
6 / 1,200 = 0.5%
Full calc
A table is partitioned by month (24 months) and country (50). A query asks for one month and 2 countries. Find total partitions, partitions read, fraction read and partitions skipped.
show answer
P_total = 24 × 50 = 1,200; P_read = 1 × 2 = 2; fraction = 2 / 1,200 ≈ 0.167%; skipped = 1,198.
Long answer
Explain partitioning in Hive with DDL, a directory diagram and a pruning example. State its main risk.
show answer
Use the 5-mark answer. Diagram: /data/sales/year=2023/, year=2024/month=01/ ... month=12/; WHERE year=2024 AND month=01 reads only month=01/. Risk: partition explosion on high-cardinality keys.

Bucketing and bucket map join

Facts
  • Bucketing splits each partition into a fixed number N of files (buckets) by hashing a chosen column.
  • A row goes to bucket hash(column) % N. For an integer key the hash is the value itself.
  • DDL: CLUSTERED BY (user_id) INTO 32 BUCKETS STORED AS ORC. Add SORTED BY for a sort-merge bucket join.
  • Bucket map join: both tables bucketed on the join key with the same N; Hive joins bucket i with bucket i, no full shuffle.
  • Good bucket column: a high-cardinality join key such as user_id.
  • The Metastore stores the bucket count per table; Hive uses it to decide whether a join can skip the shuffle.
How it works
  1. Declare CLUSTERED BY (user_id) INTO N BUCKETS on both tables, with the same N.
  2. On insert, Hive computes hash(user_id) % N for each row.
  3. The row is written to bucket file k, where k is that remainder.
  4. The same key gives the same k in both tables, so matching rows sit in matching files.
  5. At join time Hive reads the bucket counts from the Metastore and pairs bucket 0 with 0, 1 with 1, and so on.
  6. Each pair is joined locally, with no full sort-merge shuffle.
Why · pros · limits
  • Joining two large tables no longer shuffles both of them, a large speed-up.
  • Works on high-cardinality keys, where partitioning would explode into millions of directories.
  • Limitation: both tables need the same key and the same bucket count, or the benefit is lost.
  • Limitation: too many buckets give small files. Aim for files near the 128 MB HDFS block.
Compare
PartitioningBucketing
Physical unitDirectory per valueFixed number of files
Decided byColumn valuehash(column) % N
Main benefitSkips data for WHERE filters (pruning)Faster joins (bucket map join)
Good columnLow cardinality: date, countryHigh cardinality join key: user_id
RiskToo many directories on high-cardinality keysMismatched key or bucket count kills the benefit
DDLPARTITIONED BY (year INT)CLUSTERED BY (user_id) INTO 32 BUCKETS
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
two huge tables are joined every day on customer_idBucket both on customer_id with the same N
a join still shuffles although both tables are bucketed, one on user_id and one on emailDifferent bucket keys: no bucket map join
find which file holds user_id 98 with 4 buckets98 % 4 = bucket 2
a 5 TB fact table needs daily reports by date and country plus joins on user_idPartition by txn_date, country; bucket by user_id
Calculation

Bucket number (P1 slide 24, 4 buckets on user_id): user_id 98 → 98 % 4 = 2, in both sales and users.

File size (A1, assume 3 years and 5 countries): P = 3 × 365 × 5 = 5,475 partitions; 5 TB / 5,475 ≈ 0.913 GB each; with 8 buckets ≈ 114 MB per file (near a 128 MB block), 5,475 × 8 = 43,800 files.

Step by step

  1. Given user_id = 98, N = 4 → bucket = 98 % 4 = 2
  2. Given 3 years, 365 days, 5 countries → P = 1,095 × 5 = 5,475
  3. Given 5 TB = 5,000 GB and P = 5,475 → ≈ 0.913 GB per partition
  4. Given 0.913 GB and N = 8 → ≈ 114 MB per bucket file
  5. Given P = 5,475 and N = 8 → files = 43,800

Variants

  • user_id 1,234,567 with 32 buckets: 1,234,567 % 32 = 7
  • N = 32 in the 5 TB design: ≈ 28.5 MB per file and 175,200 files: too small
  • 2 TB, 730 daily partitions, 16 buckets: 2,000 GB / 11,680 ≈ 171 MB per file
Traps
  • Bucketing does not skip directories. It cuts join cost.
  • Buckets are numbered from 0: with 4 buckets the files are 0 to 3.
  • Same key and same bucket count on both tables, or no bucket map join.
  • The 5 TB question gives no years or countries. State your assumptions before sizing.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Bucketing splits each Hive partition into a fixed number of files by hash(column) % N.
3 marks
Each row is written to bucket file hash(column) % N, so equal keys always land in the same bucket number. If two tables are bucketed on the join key with the same N, Hive joins bucket i with bucket i and skips the full shuffle. Example: user_id 98 with 4 buckets goes to bucket 98 % 4 = 2 in both tables.
5 marks

Definition: fixed N files per partition, chosen by hash of a column.

  1. CLUSTERED BY (user_id) INTO N BUCKETS on both tables.
  2. Each row goes to bucket hash(user_id) % N.
  3. Matching keys sit in matching bucket numbers.
  4. Bucket map join pairs bucket i with i; no full shuffle.

Example: 5 TB over 5,475 partitions, 8 buckets ≈ 114 MB per file, near one HDFS block.

Limitation: both tables need the same key and bucket count; too many buckets make small files.

MCQ
What is the main benefit of bucketing two tables on the join key with the same number of buckets?
  • (a) UPDATE and DELETE become possible
  • (b) Bucket i joins bucket i, so no full shuffle is needed
  • (c) The table needs no Metastore entry
  • (d) Whole directories are skipped by WHERE filters
show answer
(b) Bucket i joins bucket i, so no full shuffle is needed
That is the bucket map join; directory skipping is partitioning.
Fill in the blank
In Hive, a row goes to bucket number hash(column) ____ N.
show answer
% (mod)
Define / list
Define a bucket map join and state its two conditions.
show answer
A join that pairs bucket i of one table with bucket i of the other, with no full shuffle. Conditions: both tables bucketed on the join key; same number of buckets.
Explain the steps
Describe how Hive writes rows into buckets.
show answer
Read CLUSTERED BY (col) INTO N BUCKETS → compute hash(col) % N per row → write the row to bucket file k = remainder → equal keys always land in the same file number.
Why / justify
Why bucket on user_id instead of partitioning on it?
show answer
user_id has millions of values; partitioning would create millions of directories and crash the NameNode. Bucketing keeps a fixed N files and still speeds up joins on user_id.
Distinguish
Distinguish partitioning and bucketing.
show answer
Partitioning: a directory per value of a low-cardinality column; skips data for WHERE filters. Bucketing: a fixed N files by hash of a high-cardinality column; speeds up joins.
Scenario
An e-shop joins a 3 TB orders table with a 20 M-row customers table on customer_id every night. Both are bucketed on customer_id: orders into 32 buckets, customers into 32 buckets.
i) Which join can Hive use? ii) Which bucket holds customer_id 100? iii) What if customers had been bucketed on email? iv) What column should orders be partitioned on for daily reports?
show answer
i) Bucket map join (bucket i with bucket i). ii) 100 % 32 = 4. iii) Keys differ, so matching rows are in different buckets and a full shuffle is needed. iv) order_date.
One-step calc
A table has 16 buckets on an integer order_id. Which bucket holds order_id 1,005?
show answer
1,005 % 16 = 13
Full calc
A 2 TB table is partitioned by day for 2 years and has 16 buckets per partition. Find partitions, total files and file size. Is the size sensible?
show answer
P = 2 × 365 = 730; files = 730 × 16 = 11,680; size = 2,000 GB / 11,680 ≈ 171 MB. Sensible: above the 128 MB block, not tiny files.
Long answer
Design a partitioning and bucketing scheme for an orders table filtered by date and division and joined with customers on customer_id. Give the DDL and justify it.
show answer
CREATE TABLE orders (order_id BIGINT, customer_id BIGINT, amount DOUBLE)
PARTITIONED BY (order_date STRING, division STRING)
CLUSTERED BY (customer_id) INTO 32 BUCKETS STORED AS ORC;
Partition on the low-cardinality filter columns for pruning; bucket on the high-cardinality join key, with customers also in 32 buckets on customer_id, for a bucket map join. Limitation: needs matching bucket counts.

Presto and the SQL-on-Hadoop engines

Facts
  • Presto (Meta, 2013; fork: Trino) runs interactive SQL over petabytes on HDFS: 5 to 60 seconds versus Hive+MR's 5 to 15 minutes.
  • No intermediate disk writes: data flows through an in-memory pipeline (split → stage → output).
  • Vectorized execution: processes column chunks of 1,024 rows at a time, not row by row.
  • Cost-based optimizer: uses table statistics to pick join order and join strategy.
  • Connector architecture: one query can read HDFS, Hive, Cassandra, MySQL, Kafka and S3 together.
  • Other engines: Impala (Cloudera, 2012, native C++ MPP), Spark SQL (2014, Catalyst optimizer), Drill (MapR, 2012, schema-free JSON).
How it works
  1. Hive+MR reads HDFS and runs the map phase.
  2. It writes the map output to disk.
  3. It runs the reduce phase and writes the result to HDFS, again for every stage.
  4. Presto reads HDFS splits into worker memory.
  5. It streams the data through its stages in memory, in batches of 1,024 rows.
  6. It returns the result to the client with no intermediate disk write.
Why · pros · limits
  • Disk writes between stages are the main cost of Hive+MR; Presto removes them.
  • Dashboards and ad-hoc questions need seconds; Hive's minutes are too slow.
  • Hive still suits large ETL and batch jobs, where MR/HDFS give it fault tolerance.
  • Presto reads the same Hive Metastore, so it swaps the engine and keeps the tables.
Compare
ToolExecution modelLatencyBest for
Hive (Facebook, 2008)MR / Tez / Spark, compiledMinutesBatch analytics, large ETL
Presto/Trino (Meta, 2013)In-memory pipelineSecondsInteractive BI dashboards
Impala (Cloudera, 2012)Native C++ MPPSecondsLow-latency SQL on HDFS/S3
Spark SQL (Databricks, 2014)Catalyst optimizer, in-memorySecondsUnified batch + streaming SQL
Drill (MapR, 2012)Schema-free, JSON-nativeSecondsExploratory SQL on JSON/Parquet
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a dashboard over Hive tables must answer in secondsPresto/Trino (or Spark SQL)
a nightly 6-hour ETL over 50 TBHive
one query joins Kafka data with a MySQL table and S3 filesPresto connectors
SQL over raw JSON files with no schema definedApache Drill
an engine that processes 1,024-row column batches instead of single rowsVectorized execution (Presto)
Traps
  • Presto's speed comes from no intermediate disk writes, not from extra SQL functions, connectors or Python UDFs.
  • Presto replaces the engine, not the catalogue: it reads the Hive Metastore.
  • The slide title says 10× faster; the ranges (5–15 min vs 5–60 s) give 5× to 180×.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Presto is Meta's in-memory SQL engine that answers interactive queries over HDFS in seconds by never writing intermediate results to disk.
3 marks
Hive+MR writes intermediate data to HDFS after every map and reduce phase; Presto streams data through an in-memory pipeline. It adds vectorized 1,024-row batches and a cost-based optimizer. Example: a query taking 5 to 15 minutes on Hive+MR takes 5 to 60 seconds on Presto.
5 marks

Definition: Presto (Meta, 2013) is a distributed SQL engine for interactive queries on HDFS and other stores.

  1. Read HDFS splits into memory.
  2. Pipeline through stages in memory, no disk writes.
  3. Vectorized: 1,024-row column batches.
  4. Cost-based optimizer picks join order.
  5. Connectors: Hive, Cassandra, MySQL, Kafka, S3 in one query.

Example: Hive+MR 5–15 min vs Presto 5–60 s.

Limitation: Hive is still the choice for long batch ETL, where MR's disk checkpoints give fault tolerance.

Hive: compiles to MR/Tez/Spark jobs, minutes, batch ETL. Presto: in-memory pipeline, seconds, interactive dashboards and ad-hoc queries.

Case study: Spotify Discover Weekly

Facts
  • Scale (2015): about 3 billion events/day, 30 million songs, 2 billion playlists, 75M users; output 30M personalised playlists.
  • Data path: Kafka events → HDFS → HiveQL (analytics, features) and Spark MLlib (collaborative filtering).
  • Co-occurrence: songs found together in more than N playlists are similar: GROUP BY song_a, song_b HAVING COUNT(*) > 5.
  • ALS (Alternating Least Squares) factorises the user × song matrix into a user matrix and a song matrix of k factors each.
  • Luigi (Spotify, Python, 2012) chains HiveQL and Spark jobs. Each task declares its output path and inputs; finished tasks are skipped.
  • Partitioning play_events by week cut the co-occurrence query from 8 hours to 2 hours.
How it works
  1. Apps log plays, skips, pauses and playlist adds as Kafka events.
  2. Kafka consumers land the events on HDFS.
  3. HiveQL self-joins playlist_songs on playlist_id to form song pairs, then GROUP BY counts each pair.
  4. Spark ALS learns user and song factor matrices from the interactions.
  5. Luigi runs the steps in dependency order each week and skips finished outputs.
  6. The scores produce 30M Discover Weekly playlists.
Why · pros · limits
  • Implicit feedback (2 billion playlists) was a richer signal than explicit star ratings.
  • SQL suits aggregation; iterative ML (ALS) suits Spark. Luigi joins them in one pipeline.
  • Schema design decides speed: the right partition column changes runtime by hours.
  • ALS stores (users + songs) × k numbers instead of users × songs, so the model fits in memory.
  • Limitation: cold start. A new song with no playlist appearances needs audio features instead.
Compare
LuigiAirflow
Dependency styleTarget-based: requires(), output(), run(); pulls back from the final taskDAG file of tasks and edges (t1 >> t2), run on a schedule
BackfillPass past dates; finished outputs skipped; usually cron-triggeredBuilt in: catchup and airflow dags backfill
UISimple status and dependency graph pageRich UI: graph/grid views, logs, retries, alerts
Best fitSmall, file-based Hadoop pipelinesLarge pipelines (e.g. 100 steps)
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a re-run skips every step whose HDFS output already existsLuigi (target-based)
a song released yesterday gets no playlist-based recommendationsCold start
50 taste scores per user and per song replace the full rating matrixALS matrix factorisation
the same HiveQL got 4× faster after a table redesignPartitioning play_events by week
Calculation

ALS model size (Discussion 2): (75M + 30M) × 50 × 4 B = 2.1 × 10^10 B = 21 GB. The full matrix would be 75M × 30M × 4 B = 9 × 10^15 B = 9 PB. Speed-up (lesson 3): 8 h / 2 h = 4×, 75% time saved.

Step by step

  1. Given 75M users, k = 50, 4 B → user factors = 75 × 10^6 × 50 × 4 = 15 GB
  2. Given 30M songs → song factors = 30 × 10^6 × 50 × 4 = 6 GB
  3. Given 15 GB + 6 GB → model = 21 GB
  4. Given 5,000 nodes × 64 GB (assumed) → 320 TB RAM; 21 GB ≈ 0.0066% of it: fits
  5. Given 8 h before, 2 h after → speed-up = 4×, saved = (8 − 2) / 8 = 75%
  6. Given a playlist of L = 20 songs → pairs = 20 × 19 / 2 = 190

Variants

  • 10M users, 1M songs, k = 100: 11M × 100 × 4 B = 4.4 GB
  • Dense matrix 9 PB vs 320 TB RAM: about 28× too big, does not fit
  • 2B (playlist, song) rows at 20 songs each: 10^8 playlists × 190 = 1.9 × 10^10 pairs
Traps
  • ALS size uses users plus songs, not users times songs.
  • The slides give no RAM per node. State the assumption (e.g. 64 GB).
  • Bucketing playlist_songs by playlist_id speeds the self-join, not the GROUP BY on song pairs.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Discover Weekly is Spotify's weekly recommendation playlist, built from playlist co-occurrence in HiveQL and ALS collaborative filtering in Spark, scheduled by Luigi.
3 marks
Events go Kafka → HDFS; HiveQL counts how often song pairs share a playlist, and Spark ALS learns user and song factors. Luigi runs the jobs in order and skips finished outputs. Example: the ALS model is (75M + 30M) × 50 × 4 B = 21 GB.
5 marks

Goal: 30M personalised weekly playlists from 3B events/day.

  1. Kafka → HDFS.
  2. HiveQL co-occurrence: self-join on playlist_id, GROUP BY song_a, song_b HAVING COUNT(*) > 5.
  3. Spark ALS factorises users × songs.
  4. Luigi orders the jobs and skips done outputs.

Example: ALS model 21 GB vs 9 PB dense; partitioning by week cut 8 h to 2 h (4×).

Limitation: cold start for new songs, handled with audio features.
