Chapter 4 · Week 8Data integration: Kafka, Lambda/Kappa, data lakes

How data moves from the systems that create it to the systems that analyse it: batch tools (Sqoop, Flume) and the Kafka event log. How Lambda and Kappa architectures combine batch and stream processing, and how a data lake stays usable instead of turning into a swamp.

Exam question pool 23 questions from the slides · click a question for its answer

MCQ
Q1. Which tool is specifically designed to transfer data in bulk between an RDBMS (e.g., MySQL) and HDFS?
  • (a) Apache Flume
  • (b) Apache Kafka
  • (c) Apache Sqoop
  • (d) Apache NiFi
show answer
(c) Apache Sqoop
Sqoop (SQL-to-Hadoop) bulk-copies tables between a database and HDFS; Flume is for logs.
MCQ
Q2. Apache Kafka's data model is based on which fundamental storage abstraction?
  • (a) B-tree indexes for fast point lookups
  • (b) Column-family storage (like HBase)
  • (c) Append-only immutable distributed logs (partitioned topics)
  • (d) Document storage with secondary indexes
show answer
(c) Append-only immutable distributed logs (partitioned topics)
Kafka is a distributed, partitioned, replicated commit log.
MCQ
Q3. In the Lambda architecture, which layer handles real-time, low-latency query views?
  • (a) Batch Layer
  • (b) Serving Layer
  • (c) Speed Layer
  • (d) Ingestion Layer
show answer
(c) Speed Layer
The Serving layer only merges views; the Speed layer computes the seconds-old views.
MCQ
Q4. The Kappa architecture differs from the Lambda architecture primarily by:
  • (a) Adding a third processing layer for machine learning
  • (b) Using only relational databases for both batch and stream storage
  • (c) Eliminating the batch layer and using Kafka log replay for all reprocessing
  • (d) Replacing Hadoop with Spark in the batch layer while keeping the speed layer
show answer
(c) Eliminating the batch layer and using Kafka log replay for all reprocessing
Kappa removes the whole batch layer; (d) is the common misconception.
True/False
1. Apache Sqoop is designed for real-time streaming ingestion from transactional systems.
show answer
False. Sqoop runs batch MapReduce imports. Corrected: Sqoop is designed for bulk batch transfer between an RDBMS and HDFS; real-time ingestion uses Kafka.
True/False
2. Kafka retains messages for a configurable period, allowing consumers to replay events from any past offset.
show answer
True. Messages stay for the retention period (e.g. 7 days) after being read, so a consumer can reset its offset and replay.
True/False
3. The Lambda architecture requires maintaining two separate code paths implementing the same business logic.
show answer
True. The same logic is written for the batch layer (Spark/Hive) and the speed layer (Spark Streaming) and must be kept in sync.
True/False
4. Apache Flume is the preferred tool for collecting and aggregating server log data into HDFS.
show answer
True. Flume moves logs into HDFS through Source, Channel and Sink with fan-in from many servers.
Descriptive
D1. Explain the Lambda architecture with a labelled diagram. What are its three layers? What is its primary engineering disadvantage compared to the Kappa architecture?
show answer
  • Batch: master dataset on HDFS, recomputed hourly/nightly; accurate, 1–24 h stale.
  • Speed: stream processing of data since the last batch; approximate, results within seconds.
  • Serving: query = batch view UNION real-time view.

Disadvantage vs Kappa: two code paths for the same logic, kept in sync (30–40% of effort), plus two clusters and a runtime merge. Kappa has one stream code path.

Descriptive
D2. Describe how Apache Kafka achieves high throughput and fault tolerance. Explain the roles of topics, partitions, offsets, brokers, and replication factor in the Kafka data model.
show answer
TermRole
TopicNamed append-only log for one event type
PartitionOrdered slice of a topic; P partitions run in parallel; same key → same partition
OffsetMessage position; each consumer tracks its own
BrokerServer storing partitions
Replication factor R1 leader + R − 1 followers per partition (default 3)

Throughput: sequential writes, zero-copy sendfile(), batching, partition parallelism.

Fault tolerance: a follower takes over when a leader's broker fails; retention lets a crashed consumer resume from its offset. Example: bkash-txns, 3 partitions, RF = 2 survives 1 broker failure.

Descriptive
D3. What is a data lake? How does it differ from a data warehouse in terms of schema enforcement, storage format, and typical use cases? What is a “data swamp” and how is it prevented?
show answer

Data lake: central store of raw data in any format, no upfront schema.

LakeWarehouse
SchemaOn readOn write
FormatRaw files, any typeCleaned relational tables
UseML, data science, explorationBI reports, dashboards

Data swamp: a lake with no governance: no catalogue, stale vs current unknown, unversioned copies, PII mixed in.

Prevention: catalogue at ingest, owner per dataset, access control, quality checks, Delta Lake/Iceberg for ACID.

Analytical
A1. Pipeline Design for a Bank: Design a data integration pipeline for a Bangladeshi commercial bank that must:
  • (a) Synchronise 10 million customer records nightly from Oracle to Hadoop for analytics
  • (b) Detect fraudulent card-swipe events in real time with a decision within 200 ms
  • (c) Power a risk management dashboard with data no older than 5 minutes
Specify which tool (Sqoop, Flume, Kafka, Spark Streaming) you would use at each stage. Justify each choice with reference to latency, throughput, and fault-tolerance requirements. Which architecture (Lambda or Kappa) fits best, and why?
show answer
NeedToolLatencyThroughputFault tolerance
(a) 10 M rows nightlySqoop (incremental import)Hours is fine: batchParallel map tasks by key range, GB–TB/hFailed map task retried; HDFS replicated
(b) Fraud < 200 msKafka topic (keyed by card no.) + Spark StreamingMillisecondsPartitions scale consumersRF = 3; restart from last offset
(c) Dashboard < 5 minSecond consumer group on same topic + Spark Streaming aggregatesMinutesSame topic, no extra ingestReplay from offset

Flume is not used: it is for logs, not tables or card events.

Architecture: Lambda. The bank has a real batch need (nightly sync, regulatory batch reports), which the slide lists as a reason Kappa is not suitable. Batch = Sqoop + HDFS + Hive; Speed = Kafka + Spark Streaming; Serving = dashboard. Cut the dual-path cost by making Kafka the single source both layers read.

Analytical
A2. Lambda to Kappa Migration: A team using Lambda architecture complains that maintaining two code paths (batch + stream) doubles development cost. Their CTO suggests migrating to Kappa. Analyse: (a) what must be true about the stream processing engine for Kappa to be viable; (b) what are the risks of migrating; (c) under what conditions is Kappa not suitable (give at least two concrete scenarios from the Bangladesh financial or healthcare sector)?
show answer

(a) Conditions: the engine reprocesses history about as fast as live data; full history sits in a replayable log (long Kafka retention or Iceberg on S3; default is only 7 days); the logic fits a stream job; exactly-once processing so replays match.

(b) Risks: TB+ replay may take days; batch throughput falls to stream-engine speed; storage cost of long retention; every Hive/Spark batch job must be rewritten and run in parallel during the switch; staff must learn stream SQL.

(c) Not suitable:

  1. Regulatory reconciliation: Bangladesh Bank requires a daily end-of-day batch reconciliation; the batch layer must stay.
  2. TB+ history: a national health-records system recomputing 10 years of disease statistics after a coding change; one Spark batch job is far faster than replay.
  3. Batch SQL preferred: yearly credit-risk model training written in Hive/Spark SQL over full history.

Verdict: move streaming workloads to one Kappa path; keep batch where regulation or huge history demands it.

Discussion
L1. Kafka as the single source of truth: all events written to Kafka first; the speed and batch layers are both consumers of the same topic — eliminating the data inconsistency problem of classic Lambda (two separate ingestion paths)
show answer
Claim: Netflix writes each event to Kafka once; Flink (speed, < 1 min) and the S3 sink + Spark/Iceberg (batch, ~1 day) read the same topic, so both layers see the same data. For: no mismatch between layers; new consumers just subscribe. Against: Kafka becomes a single critical dependency (2,000+ brokers to keep up), and the two code paths still exist; only ingestion is unified.
Discussion
L2. Retention enables reprocessing: 7-day retention lets Netflix replay any event window when a Flink job has a bug or a Spark job fails
show answer
Claim: reading does not delete events, so a fixed job resets its offset and recomputes the last week. For: bugs are fixed after the fact without asking devices to resend. Against: only 7 days; a bug found on day 8 needs the S3/Iceberg copy. At 100 billion events/day, 7 days is 700 billion events of storage; tiered storage (Kafka 3.6) moves old segments to S3.
Discussion
L3. Kappa simplification: Netflix increasingly moved batch jobs to Flink stream processing (processing historical S3 data as a bounded stream), reducing pipeline complexity
show answer
Claim: a bounded stream (data with a start and an end, such as last month's S3 files) can be read by the same Flink code as live data, so one code path covers both. For: less duplicate code, fewer systems. Against: Netflix still keeps Spark + Iceberg for ML training and billing, where batch is easier or cheaper; the move is gradual.
Discussion
L4. Schema Registry is non-negotiable at scale: 4,000 topics × evolving schemas ⇒ consumer breakage without schema governance
show answer
Claim: a schema is a message's field names and types; if a producer renames a field, consumers break. Schema Registry stores the Avro/Protobuf schema per topic so consumers can read any past producer version. For: 4,000+ topics and 150+ Flink jobs cannot be coordinated by hand. Against: another service to run, and teams must follow compatibility rules. It is the streaming form of the data-swamp lesson.
Discussion
L5. Backpressure is the real production problem: during Super Bowl commercials, event rates spike 5×; Kafka's durable log absorbs the burst; Flink consumers catch up within minutes
show answer
Claim: backpressure is data arriving faster than consumers can process. If the 5× spike landed on the 1.3 M/s peak, that would be 6.5 M/s; Kafka appends the excess to disk and Flink catches up later. For: no data lost, no need to size consumers for the rare peak. Against: results lag by minutes during the spike (the 5-minute A/B latency slips), and brokers need spare disk.
Live Exercise
Task. Simulate a Kafka producer/consumer pipeline using the kafka-python library with a local single-broker cluster (Docker Compose provided below).
Quick-start: pip install kafka-python; docker run -p 9092:9092 apache/kafka:3.7.0
import json, random, time
from kafka import KafkaProducer, KafkaConsumer

TOPIC   = "play-events"
BROKER  = "localhost:9092"
CONTENT = [f"show_{i}" for i in range(20)]

# --- Producer ---
producer = KafkaProducer(
  bootstrap_servers=BROKER,
  value_serializer=lambda v:
      json.dumps(v).encode())

for _ in range(1_000):
    event = {
      "user_id":    random.randint(1, 500),
      "content_id": random.choice(CONTENT),
      "action":     random.choice(
                       ["play","pause","seek"]),
      "ts":         int(time.time())
    }
    producer.send(TOPIC, event)
producer.flush()
print("Produced 1,000 events")

# --- Consumer ---
consumer = KafkaConsumer(
  TOPIC,
  bootstrap_servers=BROKER,
  group_id="cse4345-group",
  auto_offset_reset="earliest",
  value_deserializer=lambda m:
      json.loads(m.decode()))

counts = {}
for msg in consumer:
    cid = msg.value["content_id"]
    counts[cid] = counts.get(cid, 0) + 1
    if sum(counts.values()) >= 1_000:
        break
print(sorted(counts.items(),
             key=lambda x: -x[1])[:5])
show answer

The producer sends 1,000 JSON events (user 1–500, one of 20 shows, play/pause/seek); flush() waits until queued messages are sent. The consumer (group cse4345-group, auto_offset_reset="earliest") reads from the start, counts events per content_id, stops after 1,000 and prints the top 5 shows. Broker: docker run -p 9092:9092 apache/kafka:3.7.0.

Replay (goal 3): consumer.seek_to_beginning(*consumer.assignment()). New group (goal 4): change group_id.

Live Exercise
1. Run the producer — send 1,000 synthetic Netflix play events to topic play-events.
show answer
Expected output: Produced 1,000 events. With one partition the messages sit at offsets 0–999. Running it again appends offsets 1,000–1,999; nothing is overwritten.
Live Exercise
2. Run the consumer — read all messages, count plays per content_id.
show answer
Expected: 1,000 events read. The slide’s consumer counts every event per content_id (play, pause and seek alike), so each of the 20 shows gets about 1,000 / 20 = 50; the top-5 print shows the largest. To count plays only, add if msg.value["action"] == "play": about 1,000 / 3 ≈ 333 plays, about 16.7 per show. Exact counts change each run because the producer is random.
Live Exercise
3. Reset the consumer offset to the beginning and re-consume — verify idempotent results.
show answer
After seek_to_beginning the same group re-reads all 1,000 events and gets identical counts. Reading did not delete anything, so the result is idempotent (same answer when repeated). If the producer ran again in between, there are 2,000 events: that is new data, not a broken replay.
Live Exercise
4. Change the consumer group ID and observe that a new group starts from offset 0.
show answer
A new group has no committed offset, so with auto_offset_reset="earliest" it reads all 1,000 events from offset 0. The old group's offset is untouched. Trap: kafka-python's default is "latest", which makes a new group read 0 events.

Topic cards 6 topics · everything the pool tests

From point-to-point ETL to event pipelines

Facts
  • ETL (Extract, Transform, Load) is a job that copies data from one system into another.
  • Point-to-point ETL joins every pair of systems with its own job: O(N²) connections, 2,450 jobs at 50 systems.
  • Four faults of point-to-point ETL: brittle (schema change breaks jobs), no replay, no real time (latency ≥ 24 h), no decoupling.
  • Event-driven pipeline: one central message bus (Kafka). Sources publish events, consumers subscribe.
  • Adding a new consumer to a bus needs zero changes to any source system.
  • Tool landscape: Sqoop = bulk DB sync, Flume = log collection, Kafka = real-time events, NiFi = visual no-code routing, Spark Streaming = processing streams.
How it works
  1. Each source system publishes its events to the bus once
  2. The bus stores the events durably, in order
  3. Each consumer subscribes to the topics it needs and reads at its own pace
  4. A new consumer subscribes and can replay stored history; no source changes
Why · pros · limits
  • N systems on a bus need N connections instead of N × (N − 1) jobs.
  • Decoupling: a source does not know or care who reads its events.
  • Replay: stored events let a late or crashed consumer catch up; nightly ETL loses them.
  • Real time: events flow in seconds, not after a 24-hour batch.
  • Cost: the bus is one critical system; if it is down, every flow stops.
Compare
Point-to-point ETLEvent-driven pipeline
ConnectionsN × (N − 1), O(N²)N (one per system, to the bus)
Latency≥ 24 hours (nightly batch)Seconds
ReplayNone; offline consumer loses dataYes, from the retained log
Schema changeBreaks the jobs on that pairOnly affects that topic's readers
New consumerNew job per sourceSubscribe; zero source changes
ToolDirectionUse it for
SqoopRDBMS ↔ HDFS, batchNightly bulk copy of database tables
FlumeLogs → HDFS, batch/micro-batchCollecting server logs
KafkaAny → any, real-timeCentral event bus: fraud events, clickstream, IoT
NiFiAny → any, visualDrag-and-drop routing, IoT edge to cloud
Spark StreamingKafka → HDFS/DBStateful aggregation, enrichment, joins on streams
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
every new report needs a new nightly job from each source systempoint-to-point ETL; replace with a message bus
a consumer was down overnight and its data is goneno replay: a fault of point-to-point ETL
a new analytics team plugs in without touching the source appsevent-driven pipeline (decoupling)
an operator wants to route IoT files by dragging boxes, no codeApache NiFi
Calculation

Slide: 50 systems. Point-to-point jobs = 50 × 49 = 2,450. With a bus: 50 connections.

Step by step

  1. Given N = 50 systems -> each system sends to N − 1 = 49 others
  2. Given 50 senders × 49 targets -> 2,450 jobs (ordered pairs)
  3. Given 2,450 ordered jobs -> one job per pair = 2,450 / 2 = 1,225
  4. Given a bus -> connections = N = 50

Variants

  • N = 10: 10 × 9 = 90 jobs (45 if each pair is counted once), bus 10
  • N = 20: 20 × 19 = 380 jobs, bus 20
  • N = 30: 30 × 29 = 870 jobs, bus 30
Traps
  • The slide's 2,450 counts A→B and B→A as separate jobs. If you count each pair once, say so and divide by 2.
  • Spark Streaming processes streams; it does not ingest from a database. Do not list it as an ingestion tool.
  • NiFi is the visual tool, not Flume.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Point-to-point ETL connects every pair of systems with its own job, giving O(N²) jobs.
3 marks
Point-to-point ETL needs N × (N − 1) jobs, so 50 systems need 2,450. An event-driven pipeline puts one message bus (Kafka) in the middle. Sources publish once and consumers subscribe, so 50 systems need 50 connections.
5 marks

Definition: an event-driven pipeline replaces direct ETL jobs with a central message bus.

  1. Sources publish events to the bus.
  2. The bus keeps them in order and retains them.
  3. Consumers subscribe and read at their own pace.

Example: 50 systems need 2,450 point-to-point jobs (50 × 49) but only 50 bus connections. Point-to-point ETL is also brittle, cannot replay and is ≥ 24 h late.

Limitation: the bus is a single critical dependency, so it must be replicated.

show answer

Define / list
List the four faults of point-to-point ETL.
show answer
Brittle (schema change breaks jobs); no replay; no real time (≥ 24 h); no decoupling.
Explain the steps
Explain how an event-driven pipeline moves data from sources to consumers.
show answer
Sources publish events to a central bus; the bus stores them in order; each consumer subscribes and reads at its own pace; a new consumer just subscribes and can replay history.
Why / justify
Why does adding a consumer to an event bus need no change to the sources?
show answer
Sources only write to the bus and never address a consumer, so a new subscriber reads the same topic without the source knowing.
Distinguish
Distinguish point-to-point ETL from an event-driven pipeline on connections and latency.
show answer
ETL: N × (N − 1) jobs, ≥ 24 h late. Pipeline: N connections to one bus, seconds of latency.
Scenario
A retailer has 8 systems that each send nightly CSV files to every other system. Last week the stock system was down and lost a night of sales data.
i) Name this integration style. ii) How many jobs exist? iii) Which fault caused the lost data? iv) What should replace it?
show answer
i) Point-to-point ETL. ii) 8 × 7 = 56. iii) No replay. iv) An event-driven pipeline with Kafka as the bus (8 connections).
One-step calc

show answer
12 × 11 = 132.
Full calc
A bank has 40 systems. Compute the point-to-point jobs (ordered), the jobs if counted once per pair, and the connections with a bus.
show answer
40 × 39 = 1,560; 1,560 / 2 = 780; bus = 40.
Long answer
Explain why modern enterprises replace point-to-point ETL with event-driven pipelines. Use numbers.
show answer
Point-to-point ETL gives every pair its own job: 50 systems need 50 × 49 = 2,450. Those jobs break on schema changes, cannot replay lost data, run nightly (≥ 24 h) and bind each source to each consumer. A pipeline puts Kafka in the middle: sources publish once, consumers subscribe, so 50 systems need 50 connections, new consumers need zero source changes, and the retained log allows replay. Cost: the bus must be replicated because everything depends on it.

Batch ingestion: Sqoop and Flume

Facts
  • Sqoop (SQL-to-Hadoop) is a command-line tool for bulk transfer between an RDBMS (MySQL, Oracle, PostgreSQL, SQL Server) and HDFS, Hive or HBase.
  • Sqoop import: RDBMS → HDFS as CSV, Avro, Parquet or SequenceFile. Export: HDFS → RDBMS.
  • Sqoop incremental import copies only rows newer than a last-value checkpoint on a timestamp or auto-increment column.
  • Flume moves semi-structured logs (web server, application, IoT) into HDFS.
  • A Flume agent has three parts: Source (reads), Channel (buffers), Sink (writes).
  • Flume channel types: Memory (fast, lost on crash) or File (on disk, survives agent restart).
How it works
  1. Sqoop client connects to the database over JDBC
  2. It submits a MapReduce job; each map task reads one primary-key range of rows in parallel
  3. Rows land in HDFS (or Hive/HBase) as Parquet, CSV or Avro
  4. Next run, incremental mode reads only rows past the last-value (for example txn_date > 2024-06-12)
  5. Flume: a Source reads log lines (Exec tail, Syslog, HTTP, Avro)
  6. The Channel buffers the events
  7. The Sink writes them to HDFS (or Kafka, HBase)
Why · pros · limits
  • Sqoop lets Hive analytics run on HDFS without touching the live OLTP database.
  • Parallel map tasks give GB–TB/hour; a failed map task is retried by MapReduce.
  • Flume fan-in (many sources → one agent) and fan-out (one source → many sinks) aggregate logs from many servers.
  • File channel trades speed for durability: events survive an agent crash.
  • Limitation: both are batch. Real-time needs Kafka; modern stacks often replace Flume with Kafka producers.
Compare
SqoopFlumeKafka
Source dataDatabase tablesLog filesAny events
ModeBatch (nightly)Batch / micro-batchReal-time stream
TargetHDFS, Hive, HBase (and back)HDFS (also Kafka, HBase)Any consumer
ReplayRe-run the importNoYes, from offset
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
nightly at 01:00, copy 10 M wallet transactions from Oracle into HDFS as ParquetSqoop import (bKash)
only copy rows added since yesterday's runSqoop incremental import (check-column + last-value)
push Spark results back into MySQLSqoop export
three web servers' access.log files collected into /logs/YYYY/MM/DD/Flume (Source → Channel → Sink, fan-in)
log events must not be lost if the agent restartsFlume File channel
Traps
  • Sqoop is batch, not real-time streaming (pool T/F 1 is False).
  • Memory channel is faster but loses buffered events on a crash; File channel survives.
  • Flume is for logs, not database tables. Database → HDFS is Sqoop.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Sqoop is a batch tool that copies tables in bulk between an RDBMS and HDFS.
3 marks
Sqoop imports database tables into HDFS by running a MapReduce job where each map task reads a primary-key range in parallel. Incremental import copies only rows past a last-value checkpoint. Example: bKash imports 10 M rows from Oracle nightly at 01:00.
5 marks

Sqoop moves database tables to and from HDFS in bulk. Steps: JDBC connect → MapReduce job splits rows by key range → map tasks write Parquet/CSV/Avro to HDFS; incremental mode copies only new rows. Flume moves logs to HDFS through an agent: Source reads, Channel buffers (Memory or File), Sink writes; fan-in gathers many servers. Example: bKash's nightly 10 M-row Sqoop import; web-server logs via Flume to /logs/YYYY/MM/DD/. Limitation: both are batch, with no real-time replay; Kafka covers that.

show answer

The File channel keeps buffered events on disk, so they survive an agent crash; Memory is faster but loses them.

Scenario

i) Tool for the table? ii) How to avoid copying old rows again? iii) Tool for the logs? iv) Which part of that tool must survive a crash?

i) Sqoop. ii) Incremental import with a last-value checkpoint. iii) Flume (fan-in). iv) Use a File channel.
Long answer
Compare Sqoop and Flume as batch ingestion tools, with one use case each.
show answer
Sqoop copies tables between an RDBMS and HDFS/Hive/HBase. It runs a MapReduce job that reads key ranges in parallel, supports import, export and incremental import. Use: bKash's 10 M nightly Oracle rows into HDFS as Parquet. Flume collects logs through agents: Source → Channel (Memory or File) → Sink, with fan-in and fan-out. Use: web-server access logs into HDFS by date. Both are batch; for real-time events and replay use Kafka.

Kafka: the append-only log

Facts
  • Kafka is a distributed, partitioned, replicated commit log, built at LinkedIn by Kreps, Narkhede and Rao (2011).
  • Topic: a named, append-only log for one event type. Partition: an ordered slice of a topic; P partitions run in parallel.
  • Offset: the integer position of a message in a partition. The consumer tracks its own offset (in the __consumer_offsets topic).
  • Broker: a Kafka server. Each partition has 1 leader and R − 1 follower replicas (default R = 3).
  • Consumer group: consumers that share a topic; each partition goes to exactly one consumer in the group.
  • Retention: messages stay for a set time (default 7 days) or size quota, even after being read.
How it works
  1. A producer sends a message to a topic; the key's hash (or round-robin) picks the partition
  2. The partition leader appends it at the end and gives it the next offset
  3. Followers on other brokers copy it
  4. Each consumer in a group pulls from its assigned partitions and advances its own offset
  5. To replay, a consumer resets its offset to an earlier value (for example 0)
  6. Old segments are deleted after the retention period
  7. If a leader's broker fails, a follower becomes leader (ZooKeeper before Kafka 3.0, KRaft from 3.0)
Why · pros · limits
  • Append-only gives durability, replay and decoupling for free (the founding insight).
  • Same key → same partition, so order is kept per key (for example per driver_id).
  • Replicas on other brokers let the cluster survive R − 1 broker failures.
  • The broker does not track consumers, so one log serves any number of consumer groups.
  • Limit: a consumer group cannot use more consumers than partitions; extras sit idle.
Compare
Traditional queueKafka log
After a readMessage deletedMessage kept until retention ends
Late consumerMisses dataReads from offset 0
Who tracks positionBrokerConsumer (its own offset)
Many readersMessage goes to oneEvery group reads every message
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a new fraud model must be trained on last week's events without re-ingesting from the databaseretention + replay from offset 0
fraud detection, balance update and audit all read every bKash transactionthree consumer groups on one topic
all pings from one driver must be processed in orderkey by driver_id: same key → same partition
one server dies but no messages are lostreplication factor > 1 (follower takes over as leader)
a fifth consumer joins a group reading a 4-partition topic and gets nothingidle consumer: one partition per consumer per group
Calculation

Slide: topic bkash-txns, P = 3 partitions, RF = 2. Copies = 3 × 2 = 6; followers per partition = 1; survives 1 broker failure. Pathao driver-location: P = 10; a group of 4 gets 3, 3, 2, 2 partitions.

Step by step

  1. Given P and R -> total partition copies = P × R
  2. Given R -> followers per partition = R − 1
  3. Given R -> broker failures survived = R − 1
  4. Given P partitions and C consumers in one group -> active = min(P, C), idle = C − P if C > P
  5. Given P = 10, C = 4 -> 10 / 4 = 2.5, so two consumers get 3 and two get 2

Variants

  • page-views, 3 partitions, group of 5: 3 active, 2 idle
  • P = 10, default R = 3: 30 copies, 20 followers, survives 2 failures
  • P = 6, C = 4: two consumers get 2, two get 1
Traps
  • Reading does not delete a message; retention decides deletion.
  • Order is guaranteed only within a partition, not across a whole topic.
  • Two different consumer groups each get every message; within one group a message goes to one consumer.
  • The broker does not track consumer position; the consumer does.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
A Kafka offset is the integer position of a message inside a partition.
3 marks
Kafka keeps messages for a set period (default 7 days) even after they are read. Each consumer tracks its own offset, so it can reset to 0 and replay history. Example: a new bKash analytics job reads the whole week without touching the source database.
5 marks

Definition: Kafka is a distributed, partitioned, replicated append-only log.

  1. A producer writes to a topic; the key hash picks a partition.
  2. The leader appends it at the next offset; R − 1 followers copy it.
  3. Consumer groups split partitions, one consumer per partition, and track their own offsets.
  4. Messages stay until retention ends, so any consumer can replay.

Example: bkash-txns, 3 partitions, RF = 2: 6 copies, survives 1 broker failure.

Limitation: consumers beyond the partition count sit idle; order holds only per partition.

show answer

Each partition is assigned to exactly one consumer in a group.
Fill in the blank

show answer

Define / list
Define topic, partition, offset and broker.
show answer
Topic: named append-only log. Partition: ordered slice of a topic. Offset: message position in a partition. Broker: Kafka server storing partitions.
Explain the steps
Explain how a consumer replays a week of events.
show answer
Messages are retained 7 days; the consumer resets its offset to 0 (or an earlier position); it reads forward through the log; it catches up to live data.
Why / justify
Why are messages with the same key sent to the same partition?
show answer
Order is kept only inside a partition, so one key's messages stay in order.
Distinguish
Distinguish a Kafka log from a traditional message queue.
show answer
Queue deletes on read and the broker tracks position; Kafka keeps messages for the retention period and each consumer tracks its own offset, so late consumers can replay.
Scenario
Pathao sends GPS pings to topic driver-location (10 partitions, keyed by driver_id). Routing and dispatch both read it.
i) Why key by driver_id? ii) How do routing and dispatch both get every ping? iii) Routing runs 12 consumers; how many idle? iv) Routing crashes for an hour; is data lost?
show answer
i) Keeps each driver's pings in order in one partition. ii) Each is a separate consumer group. iii) 12 − 10 = 2. iv) No; it resumes from its saved offset within retention.
One-step calc
A topic has 8 partitions and replication factor 3. How many partition copies are stored?
show answer
8 × 3 = 24.
Full calc
Topic orders: P = 6, R = 3, one group of 4 consumers. Find copies, followers, broker failures survived, and partitions per consumer.
show answer
Copies 6 × 3 = 18; followers 6 × 2 = 12; survives 2 failures; 6 / 4 = 1.5 so two consumers get 2, two get 1.
Long answer
Explain the Kafka data model and how it gives fault tolerance.
show answer
A topic is a named append-only log split into P partitions for parallel work. Each message gets an offset; consumers track their own offset. Brokers store partitions; each partition has a leader and R − 1 followers on other brokers (default R = 3). Consumer groups divide partitions, one consumer per partition. Fault tolerance: a follower takes over if a leader's broker dies (ZooKeeper/KRaft election), and retention (7 days) lets a crashed consumer resume or replay. Example: bkash-txns, 3 partitions, RF = 2, survives 1 broker loss.

Why Kafka is fast

Facts
  • Four throughput tricks (P1): sequential disk writes, zero-copy transfer, batching, partition parallelism.
  • Sequential writes: about 600 MB/s vs about 100 MB/s random (paper slide); P1 quotes < 1 MB/s random.
  • Zero-copy: the sendfile() system call sends data disk → kernel buffer → NIC: 2 copies instead of 4.
  • Batching: many messages per network round trip, set by batch.size and linger.ms; batches are compressed 5–10×.
  • Paper benchmark (100-byte messages, one broker): producer Kafka 50.5 MB/s, RabbitMQ 14.7, ActiveMQ 4.3.
  • Consumer benchmark: Kafka 22.0 MB/s, RabbitMQ 8.2, ActiveMQ 4.0.
How it works
  1. Producer groups messages into a compressed batch
  2. Broker appends the batch to the end of the active segment file (sequential write)
  3. OS page cache holds recent pages and prefetches the next ones
  4. A consumer pulls from an offset; the .index file maps offset to byte position
  5. Broker calls sendfile(): data goes from page cache to the network card, skipping user space
  6. More partitions let more producers and consumers work at once
Why · pros · limits
  • Disk heads do not jump when writing only at the end, so spinning disks get near-SSD throughput.
  • Zero-copy removes 2 copies and 2 context switches, so about 2× throughput with CPU near zero.
  • The broker acknowledges a batch, not each message, cutting network overhead.
  • Consumers pull and track their own offsets, so the broker keeps no per-consumer queue.
  • Old MOM systems (ActiveMQ, RabbitMQ) were built for a few thousand transactional messages per second.
Compare
KafkaActiveMQ / RabbitMQ
Built forLog aggregation, 100 MB/s per brokerTransactional messaging, few thousand msgs/s
StorageAppend-only log, kept for daysQueue, deleted after delivery
AcknowledgementPer batchPer message
Producer MB/s (paper)50.54.3 / 14.7
ReplayYesNo
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
data goes from disk to network without passing through the application's memoryzero-copy (sendfile())
producer waits a few milliseconds to fill a bigger requestbatching (linger.ms, batch.size)
Kafka runs on spinning disks yet nearly matches SSD speedsequential append-only writes + OS page cache
throughput doubles when the topic goes from 4 to 8 partitionspartition parallelism
Calculation

Paper, producer, 100-byte messages: 50.5 MB/s ÷ 100 B = 50,500,000 / 100 = 505,000 msgs/s. Kafka vs ActiveMQ = 50.5 / 4.3 ≈ 11.7×; vs RabbitMQ = 50.5 / 14.7 ≈ 3.4×. Disk: 600 / 100 = 6×. Zero-copy: 4 / 2 = 2×.

Step by step

  1. Given MB/s -> bytes/s = MB/s × 1,000,000
  2. Given bytes/s and message size -> msgs/s = bytes/s ÷ size
  3. Given two throughputs -> speedup = Kafka ÷ other
  4. Given sequential and random MB/s -> advantage = sequential ÷ random
  5. Given 4 copies (traditional) and 2 (sendfile) -> 2 copies saved per message, about 2×

Variants

  • Consumer: 22.0 MB/s ÷ 100 B = 220,000 msgs/s; vs ActiveMQ 22.0 / 4.0 = 5.5×; vs RabbitMQ ≈ 2.7×
  • 1,000-byte messages at 50.5 MB/s: 50,500 msgs/s
  • Netflix: 100 billion events/day ÷ 86,400 ≈ 1,157,407 events/s average; if the 5× Super Bowl spike hit the 1.3 M/s peak, that would be 6.5 M/s
  • Netflix 7-day retention holds 100 B × 7 = 700 billion events
Traps
  • Zero-copy removes copies through user space; it does not mean no copies (2 remain).
  • Two different random-disk numbers appear: < 1 MB/s (P1) and about 100 MB/s (paper). Quote the one the question gives.
  • The benchmark table treats 1 MB as 1,000,000 bytes: 50.5 MB/s of 100-byte messages = 505,000 msgs/s.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Zero-copy uses sendfile() to send data from disk to the network without copying it through user space.
3 marks
Kafka writes only at the end of the log, so disk writes are sequential (about 600 MB/s vs much slower random). It batches messages and uses sendfile() zero-copy (4 copies down to 2). In the 2011 paper Kafka produced 50.5 MB/s against ActiveMQ's 4.3 MB/s, about 11.7×.
5 marks

Kafka reaches high throughput with four tricks.

  1. Sequential writes: append-only, about 600 MB/s vs about 100 MB/s random (6×).
  2. Zero-copy: sendfile(), disk → kernel → NIC, 2 copies instead of 4.
  3. Batching: compressed batches, one acknowledgement per batch.
  4. Partition parallelism: P partitions, up to P consumers per group.

Example: paper benchmark 50.5 MB/s = 505,000 msgs/s of 100 B, vs 43,000 for ActiveMQ.

Limitation: batching adds a little latency (linger.ms).

Practise this topic in 10 formats
MCQ
Which system call gives Kafka zero-copy transfer?
  • (a) sendfile()
  • (b) fsync()
  • (c) fork()
  • (d) mmap() of user buffers
show answer
(a) sendfile()
sendfile() moves data from page cache to the socket without a user-space copy.
Fill in the blank
Traditional brokers copy data ____ times per transfer; Kafka with sendfile() copies it ____ times.
show answer
4; 2
Define / list
List Kafka's four throughput techniques from Part 1.
show answer
Sequential disk writes, zero-copy transfer, batching, partition parallelism.
Explain the steps
Trace a message from producer to consumer showing where Kafka saves time.
show answer
Producer batches and compresses; broker appends sequentially to the active segment; page cache holds it; consumer pulls by offset via .index; sendfile() sends page cache → NIC.
Why / justify
Why does append-only storage make Kafka fast on spinning disks?
show answer
Writing only at the end means the disk head never seeks, so writes run at sequential speed (about 600 MB/s).
Distinguish
Distinguish Kafka from ActiveMQ on design goal and producer throughput.
show answer
Kafka: log aggregation, retained log, 50.5 MB/s. ActiveMQ: transactional queue, deletes on delivery, 4.3 MB/s.
Scenario
A team moves from RabbitMQ to Kafka. Throughput jumps and CPU on the brokers drops.
i) Which trick cut CPU? ii) Which trick lets slow disks keep up? iii) Which setting makes the producer wait to fill a batch? iv) Paper speedup over RabbitMQ (producer)?
show answer
i) Zero-copy sendfile(). ii) Sequential append-only writes. iii) linger.ms (with batch.size). iv) 50.5 / 14.7 ≈ 3.4×.
One-step calc
Kafka consumes at 22.0 MB/s with 100-byte messages. Messages per second?
show answer
22,000,000 / 100 = 220,000.
Full calc
From the paper's producer table, find Kafka's msgs/s (100 B messages) and its speedup over ActiveMQ and RabbitMQ.
show answer
50.5 × 1,000,000 / 100 = 505,000; 50.5 / 4.3 ≈ 11.7×; 50.5 / 14.7 ≈ 3.4×.
Long answer
Explain why Kafka outperforms traditional message-oriented middleware.
show answer
Traditional MOM handled a few thousand messages per second with per-message acks and deletion. Kafka appends to a log, so writes are sequential (about 600 MB/s vs about 100 random). sendfile() zero-copy removes 2 of 4 copies and cuts CPU. Producers send compressed batches with one ack each. Partitions let many producers and consumers work in parallel, and consumers track their own offsets so the broker keeps no queues. Result in the 2011 paper: 50.5 MB/s vs 4.3 (ActiveMQ) and 14.7 (RabbitMQ).

Lambda and Kappa architectures

Facts
  • Lambda (Nathan Marz, 2011): Batch layer, Speed layer, Serving layer.
  • Batch layer: immutable master dataset on HDFS, recomputed by Spark/Hive; accurate but 1–24 hours stale.
  • Speed layer: Kafka + Spark Streaming/Flink on data since the last batch; approximate but only seconds old.
  • Serving layer: merges views; query = batch view UNION real-time view. It does no processing.
  • Kappa (Jay Kreps, 2014): two parts only, log storage (Kafka or Iceberg on S3) and one stream engine (Flink, Spark Structured Streaming).
  • Lambda's weakness: the same logic is coded twice; 30–40% of engineering effort goes on keeping them in sync.
How it works
  1. Lambda: raw events go to both the batch and the speed layer
  2. Batch layer recomputes accurate views hourly or nightly; speed layer covers the gap since
  3. Serving layer unions both views to answer a query
  4. Kappa reprocessing: deploy a new version of the stream job
  5. Start it at Kafka offset 0
  6. It processes all history, then catches up to live events
  7. When caught up, swap it into production and retire the old job
Why · pros · limits
  • Lambda strengths: accuracy (batch recomputes from raw), low latency, fault tolerance, separation of concerns.
  • Lambda costs: two code paths, two clusters, a runtime merge, harder debugging.
  • Kappa works only if the stream engine can reprocess history as fast as it handles live data.
  • Kappa is NOT suitable when: TB+ history is too slow to replay; batch SQL (Hive, Spark) is preferred; regulation demands a separate batch reconciliation.
  • Netflix mixes both: Kafka is the single source both layers read, removing two ingestion paths.
Compare
LambdaKappa
Code pathsTwo (batch + stream), kept in syncOne (stream only)
ReprocessingRe-run batch job on HDFSReplay Kafka log from offset 0
LatencyLow (speed) + high (batch)Low throughout
ComplexityHigh: two systems + mergeLower: one pipeline
Batch throughputVery high (Spark/Hive)Limited by stream engine
Use whenComplex logic, batch ML training, regulatory batch reportsStream engine fast enough; one code path worth the storage cost
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
dashboard shows yesterday's totals from a nightly Hive job plus today's from Spark StreamingLambda (batch + speed, merged in serving)
a fee rule change must be coded in both the Hive job and the streaming jobLambda's dual code path weakness
to fix a bug, a new stream job reads from offset 0 and then replaces the old oneKappa reprocessing
the central bank requires a separate end-of-day batch reconciliationKappa not suitable; keep Lambda
the layer that only merges and answers queriesServing layer
Traps
  • The Serving layer does not process data; real-time views come from the Speed layer (pool MCQ 3).
  • Kappa is not 'Spark instead of Hadoop'; it removes the whole batch layer (pool MCQ 4, option d).
  • There is no Ingestion layer in Lambda; that is a data-lake layer.
  • Kappa keeps a serving store; what it drops is the batch layer.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Kappa architecture drops Lambda's batch layer and reprocesses history by replaying the Kafka log.
3 marks
Lambda has a Batch layer (accurate, hours old), a Speed layer (approximate, seconds old) and a Serving layer that unions both. Its main cost is writing the same logic twice. Kappa keeps one stream job and replays from offset 0 to reprocess.
5 marks

Lambda (Marz, 2011) serves accurate and real-time views from the same data. Batch layer: HDFS + Spark/Hive, 1–24 h stale. Speed layer: Kafka + Flink, seconds. Serving layer: batch view UNION real-time view. Kappa (Kreps, 2014): a long-retention log plus one stream job; to reprocess, start a new job at offset 0, let it catch up, swap it in. Example: a bank dashboard = nightly Hive totals + today's stream. Limitation: Lambda needs two code paths (30–40% of effort); Kappa fails when TB+ history replays too slowly or regulators require batch reconciliation.

Practise this topic in 8 formats
MCQ
Which component does Kappa remove from Lambda?
  • (a) The speed layer
  • (b) The batch layer
  • (c) The serving store
  • (d) The Kafka log
show answer
(b) The batch layer
Kappa keeps only log storage and one stream engine.
Fill in the blank
In the Lambda serving layer, a query result = batch view ____ real-time view.
show answer
UNION
Define / list
List the three Lambda layers and the latency of each.
show answer
Batch (1–24 h, accurate), Speed (seconds old, approximate), Serving (merges and answers queries).
Explain the steps
List the Kappa reprocessing workflow.
show answer
Deploy new job version; start at offset 0; process all history and catch up to live; swap into production and retire the old job.
Why / justify
Why does Lambda cost so much to maintain?
show answer
The same business logic lives in a batch job and a stream job; every change must be made twice and kept consistent (30–40% of effort), plus two clusters and a merge.
Distinguish
Distinguish Lambda and Kappa on code paths and reprocessing.
show answer
Lambda: two code paths; reprocess by re-running the batch job on HDFS. Kappa: one stream code path; reprocess by replaying Kafka from offset 0.
Scenario
A telecom runs nightly Hive billing and a Flink job for live usage alerts. Engineers complain every tariff change takes two releases.
i) Which architecture is this? ii) Name the problem. iii) What would Kappa change? iv) Give one reason to keep the batch side.
show answer
i) Lambda. ii) Dual code paths. iii) One stream job; reprocess by replay from offset 0. iv) Regulated billing needs separate batch reconciliation (or TB+ history replays too slowly).
Long answer
Compare Lambda and Kappa and state when Kappa is not suitable.
show answer
Lambda has batch (HDFS + Spark/Hive, accurate, hours old), speed (Kafka + Flink, seconds) and serving (union) layers; it is accurate and fast but needs two code paths, two clusters and a merge. Kappa keeps one stream job over a long-retention log and reprocesses by replaying from offset 0, so it is simpler, with batch throughput limited by the stream engine. Kappa is not suitable when (1) TB+ history is too slow to replay, (2) teams prefer batch SQL, (3) regulation demands batch reconciliation.

Data lakes and the data swamp

Facts
  • Data lake: a central store for raw data at any scale and in any format, with no schema defined up front.
  • Four layers: Ingestion (Sqoop, Flume, Kafka, NiFi), Storage (HDFS, S3, ADLS), Processing (Spark, Hive, Presto), Catalogue/Governance (Atlas, Glue, Hive Metastore).
  • Storage is organised in zones: raw → processed → curated.
  • Data swamp: a lake without governance, where files pile up with no metadata, owners or quality guarantees.
  • Swamp symptoms: no catalogue, unclear current vs stale files, unversioned copies, PII mixed with other data.
  • Prevention: catalogue at ingest, owner per dataset, access control, automated quality checks, table format (Delta Lake, Iceberg) for ACID.
How it works
  1. Ingestion tools pull data from every source and write raw files
  2. Files land in the raw zone of HDFS or object storage
  3. Processing engines transform them into processed and curated zones on demand
  4. The catalogue records schema, lineage, owner and access policy for each dataset
Why · pros · limits
  • Cheap, fast ingest: nothing is rejected for not fitting a schema.
  • Keeps full raw history for data science and ML.
  • Schema-on-read: structure is applied only when a query reads the data.
  • Risk: without the catalogue layer the lake becomes a swamp nobody can use or trust.
  • Mixed PII without access control breaks privacy rules.
Compare
Data lakeData warehouse
SchemaSchema-on-read (applied at query time)Schema-on-write (must fit before load)
Storage formatRaw files of any type on cheap storageCleaned, structured relational tables
Users / useData science, ML, exploration, raw historyFixed reports, BI dashboards, SQL by business users
Data qualityVaries; needs governanceHigh, enforced at load
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
analysts cannot find datasets and nobody knows which copy is currentdata swamp
images, JSON logs and CSV exports all stored as-is for later MLdata lake (schema-on-read)
finance needs a fixed monthly report from cleaned tablesdata warehouse
Apache Atlas tracks lineage and owners for every datasetcatalogue/governance layer
patient IDs are visible to every analyst in the lakeswamp symptom (PII mixed); fix with access control
Traps
  • A lake still needs a schema; it is applied on read, not removed.
  • The swamp is caused by missing governance, not by data volume.
  • Delta Lake and Iceberg are table formats that add ACID; they are not separate lakes.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
A data swamp is a data lake with no governance: files without metadata, owners or quality checks.
3 marks
A data lake stores raw data of any format with schema applied on read, unlike a warehouse that loads only cleaned tables (schema-on-write). Without a catalogue and owners it turns into a swamp. Example: years of lab CSVs in S3 with no names or owners.
5 marks

Definition: a data lake is a central store of raw data in any format, schema-on-read.

  1. Ingestion: Sqoop, Flume, Kafka, NiFi.
  2. Storage: HDFS/S3 in raw, processed, curated zones.
  3. Processing: Spark, Hive, Presto.
  4. Catalogue: Atlas, Glue, Hive Metastore.

Example: a hospital dumps all lab files to S3; with no catalogue, analysts cannot tell which is current and patient IDs leak. That is a swamp.

Limitation: needs governance (owners, access control, quality checks, Delta Lake/Iceberg) to stay usable.

The catalogue tracks schema, lineage, owners and access.

show answer

show answer
Ingestion, Storage, Processing, Catalogue/Governance.

show answer
Ingest raw files; store in raw zone; process into processed and curated zones on demand; catalogue records schema, lineage, owner.
Why / justify
Why do Delta Lake and Iceberg help prevent a swamp?
show answer
They add ACID guarantees and versioning to lake files, so copies and current-vs-stale data are tracked.
Distinguish
Distinguish a data lake from a data warehouse.
show answer
Lake: raw files, any format, schema-on-read, ML/exploration. Warehouse: cleaned tables, schema-on-write, BI reports.
Scenario
A telco has put three years of call logs, JSON app events and scanned forms into S3. Analysts now find five versions of the same table and customer phone numbers are open to everyone.
i) Lake or warehouse? ii) What has it become? iii) Name two symptoms shown. iv) Give two fixes.
show answer
i) Data lake. ii) A data swamp. iii) Unversioned copies; PII mixed with other data. iv) Catalogue with owners; access control (or Iceberg/Delta Lake versioning, quality checks).
Long answer
Explain the data lake architecture, the data swamp risk and how to prevent it.
show answer
A lake stores raw data in any format, schema-on-read, through four layers: ingestion (Sqoop, Flume, Kafka, NiFi), storage (HDFS/S3 zones raw → processed → curated), processing (Spark, Hive, Presto) and catalogue (Atlas, Glue). Without governance it becomes a swamp: no catalogue, stale vs current unknown, unversioned copies, PII mixed in. Prevent it with a catalogue at ingest, owners, access control, quality checks and a table format such as Iceberg or Delta Lake.

Chapter 5 · Week 9Data visualization

How to draw big data so people see the truth in it: Tufte's rules for clean, honest charts, Shneiderman's rule for interactive ones, and picking the chart from the question. Then the three problems scale adds (overplotting, latency, streaming), the tools that solve them, and the NYT COVID tracker as a worked case.

Exam question pool 19 questions from the slides · click a question for its answer

MCQ
Q1. Edward Tufte's “data-ink ratio” principle states that a visualization should:
  • (a) Use more colours to highlight different categories of data
  • (b) Maximise the fraction of ink devoted to representing actual data, removing all decorative elements
  • (c) Use ink sparingly to reduce printing costs
  • (d) Always include data labels using a different ink colour from the chart body
show answer
(b) Maximise the fraction of ink devoted to representing actual data, removing all decorative elements
The ratio is about what the ink encodes, not saving ink.
MCQ
Q2. Which visualization type is most appropriate for showing the distribution and spread of a continuous variable?
  • (a) Pie chart
  • (b) Line chart
  • (c) Histogram
  • (d) Treemap
show answer
(c) Histogram
Bins along the number line show shape and spread.
MCQ
Q3. Shneiderman's visualization mantra begins with which step?
  • (a) Details first, then overview
  • (b) Overview first, zoom and filter, then details on demand
  • (c) Filter first, then provide an overview
  • (d) Details on demand first, then zoom out
show answer
(b) Overview first, zoom and filter, then details on demand
Filtering only makes sense after the overview shows what to filter.
MCQ
Q4. Which visualization tool is best suited for a real-time production operations dashboard monitoring server metrics and application error rates with 5-second refresh intervals?
  • (a) Tableau
  • (b) D3.js
  • (c) Power BI
  • (d) Grafana
show answer
(d) Grafana
Native time-series DB integration with 5 s auto-refresh; D3 would need custom code.
True/False
1. A pie chart is a recommended choice for comparing more than 5 categories.
show answer
False. People judge angles poorly. Corrected: use a horizontal bar chart; pies only for ≤ 4 categories.
True/False
2. Hexbin plots are an effective technique for reducing overplotting when visualising millions of data points.
show answer
True. Counting points per hexagon turns 10 million dots into ≈ 10,000 coloured cells.
True/False
3. D3.js generates static PNG or PDF image files as its primary output format.
show answer
False. Corrected: D3.js binds data to DOM elements and produces interactive SVG in the browser.
True/False
4. Choropleth maps are used to visualise the geographic distribution of a quantitative variable.
show answer
True. Regions are shaded by a value, best a rate such as cases per 100,000.
Descriptive
D1. Explain Tufte's data-ink ratio principle. Provide a “before and after” example: describe a cluttered bar chart (what chart-junk elements are present) and then describe the clean version after applying the principle.
show answer

Principle: data-ink ratio = ink encoding data ÷ total ink; maximise it. If erasing an element loses no information, erase it.

Before (quarterly sales)After
Title "CHART TITLE!!!"Plain short title
Legend for one seriesNo legend; Q1-Q4 on the x-axis
3-D bars with shadowsFlat 2-D bars, one colour
Gradient fill, borderRemoved
Heavy dark gridlinesNone, or one light line; y starts at 0

Same data, but almost all remaining ink is data, so the trend reads at once.

Descriptive
D2. What is overplotting? Describe three techniques to address it when visualising millions of data points in a scatterplot. For each technique, explain what information it preserves that the original overplotted chart lost.
show answer

Overplotting: millions of dots overlap into a blob that only says "data exists here".

TechniqueHowPreserves
Alpha transparencyEach point α = 0.02; stacks look darkerRelative density (fails at very high density)
HexbinCount points per hexagon, colour by count; 10 M → ≈ 10,000 cellsActual count per area
KDE contourSmooth density, contour linesShape, modes (peaks), tails
Descriptive
D3. What is a data cube? How does pre-aggregating a billion-row fact table into a data cube enable interactive visualization at sub-second latency? What is the trade-off of this approach?
show answer

Data cube: pre-computed aggregates (SUM, COUNT) of a fact table for combinations of dimensions, e.g. district × date × product × channel.

  • A nightly Spark/Hive job builds it once.
  • It holds millions of rows, not billions.
  • Tableau/Superset filters read the cube, so they answer in under 1 s instead of minutes.

Trade-off: staleness up to 24 h; only pre-built dimensions can be sliced; extra storage. Alternative: AQP (sampling) trades accuracy for speed.

Analytical
A1. Public Health Dashboard Design: A DGHS public health dashboard needs to communicate: (a) COVID-19 confirmed case counts by district over 24 months (b) Real-time hospitalisation rate updated every 5 minutes (c) Demographic breakdown (age group, gender) of confirmed cases For each sub-requirement: specify the appropriate visualization type, justify using Shneiderman's data-type taxonomy, identify which big data visualization challenge applies (if any), and name the tool you would use. Apply Tufte's data-ink principle to state one specific chart-junk element to eliminate.
show answer
Req.Data typeChartChallengeTool
(a) Cases by district, 24 monthsTemporal (+ 2-D map)Small multiples of line charts, same scale; choropleth of rate per 100,000Latency: pre-aggregate to daily counts per districtTableau / Power BI
(b) Hospitalisation every 5 minTemporal (live)Stat panel + sparkline, 24 h line chartStreamingGrafana on InfluxDB/Prometheus
(c) Age × genderCategorical / multi-dimensionalGrouped horizontal barNone (small table)Tableau / Power BI

Sizes: (a) 730 days × 64 districts = 46,720 rows after aggregation. (b) 24 × 60 ÷ 5 = 288 points per day, so 5 s polling is ample.

Mantra: national total → district dropdown and date slider → hover tooltip.

Chartjunk to remove: 3-D effect on the demographic bars (also heavy gridlines on the line charts).

Analytical
A2. Streaming Sentiment Visualization: Design a complete visualization system for a streaming Twitter (X) sentiment analysis pipeline processing 10,000 tweets per second. Specify: (a) which Kafka aggregation window you would use and why; (b) which chart types best convey sentiment drift over time and topic clustering; (c) how you would handle the velocity challenge at the visualization layer without overloading the browser; (d) which tools form the pipeline from Kafka to browser render.
show answer

Givens: 10,000 tweets/s → 600,000 per minute, 864,000,000 per day.

(a) 1-minute tumbling window per topic: count, mean sentiment, % positive/neutral/negative. One point replaces 600,000 tweets, smooths noise, still shows drift. Optional 15-min sliding window for a smoother trend.

(b) Drift: line chart (or 100% stacked area) per topic as small multiples. Clustering: t-SNE/UMAP scatter drawn as hexbin or KDE to avoid overplotting.

(c) Send only window aggregates (one refresh of 5 s replaces 50,000 tweets); keep a rolling buffer of the last 60 points; throttle redraws; pre-bin the cluster view server-side.

(d) X API → Kafka → Spark Structured Streaming/Flink → InfluxDB/Prometheus or Elasticsearch → Grafana (5 s poll) or Kibana; WebSocket → D3.js for sub-second views.

Discussion
Lesson 1. Data quality is a visualisation problem: the NYT's biggest engineering effort was cleaning data, not drawing charts; retroactive state corrections forced D3 charts to support negative bars (days where cases were revised down)
show answer

Claim: a chart is only as right as its data.

For: most NYT work was normalising 50 state schemas; downward revisions made daily changes negative, so chart design had to show negative bars.

Against: cleaning is really a data-engineering task, and no chart design can fix bad source data. Even so, the chart must show corrections honestly (negative bars) rather than hide them.

Discussion
Lesson 2. Accessibility is non-negotiable at 6M readers/day: every chart had a data table alternative for screen readers; colour scales were tested for colour-blindness (deuteranopia, protanopia)
show answer

Claim: at 6 M readers a day, even a small share of blind or colour-blind readers is many people.

For: a screen reader cannot describe the shapes of a chart, so a data table gives blind readers the same facts; avoiding red/green scales serves red-green colour-blind readers of public-health data.

Against: tables and testing cost time on daily deadlines and restrict palettes; the NYT judged it worth paying.

Discussion
Lesson 3. Small multiples beat animation: the 50-state grid (Tufte small multiples) was more actionable than an animated map of spreading cases
show answer

Claim: showing every state at once beats one frame at a time.

For: a shared scale lets readers compare states side by side and find their own without waiting or remembering frames.

Against: animation grabs attention and shows geographic spread vividly; 50 small charts can be dense on a phone. Grid for analysis, animation for a headline.

Discussion
Lesson 4. Rolling averages are editorial decisions: choosing 7-day vs. 14-day average changes what story the chart tells; these are journalistic choices with ethical weight
show answer

Claim: the window length shapes the story readers see.

For 7-day: removes weekend reporting dips and reacts to a real turn within days.

For 14-day: smoother, fewer false alarms, but hides a turning point longer.

Readers made health decisions from it, so the choice should be stated on the chart.

Discussion
Lesson 5. GitHub as data infrastructure: publishing the raw CSV on GitHub enabled 1,000+ derivative dashboards worldwide within days
show answer

Claim: open data multiplies the value of expensive cleaning.

For: others reused it for 1,000+ dashboards; version history makes corrections traceable and numbers checkable.

Against: GitHub is not a database; others can misread the data, and the publisher must keep files consistent as corrections arrive.

Live Exercise
Task. Live Exercise — D3.js Bar Chart with Tufte Styling Task (25 minutes, pairs) Build a minimal D3.js bar chart that follows Tufte's data-ink principles: 1. No gridlines (or only a single light reference line) 2. y-axis starts at 0 3. No fill gradient — use a flat colour 4. Tooltip on hover (Shneiderman: details-on-demand) 5. Add a sparkline for each bar showing the 7-day trend Dataset: weekly Kafka message counts for 5 topics (generate 7×5 random values). Extension: add a dropdown filter to select one topic and zoom into its 7-day line chart.
<!DOCTYPE html>
<meta charset="utf-8">
<style>
  .bar { fill: steelblue; }
  .bar:hover { fill: #b5651d; }
  .axis path, .axis line {
    stroke: #ccc; stroke-width: 0.5px; }
  .tooltip {
    position: absolute; background: #fff;
    border: 1px solid #ccc; padding: 6px;
    font-size: 11px; pointer-events: none; }
</style>
<body>
<script src=
  "https://d3js.org/d3.v7.min.js"></script>
<script>
const topics = ["orders","clicks",
                "errors","logins","search"];
const data = topics.map(t => ({
  topic: t,
  count: Math.floor(Math.random()*50000)
         + 10000
}));
const svg = d3.select("body").append("svg")
  .attr("width", 500).attr("height", 300);
const x = d3.scaleBand()
  .domain(data.map(d=>d.topic))
  .range([50,480]).padding(0.3);
const y = d3.scaleLinear()
  .domain([0, d3.max(data,d=>d.count)])
  .range([260, 20]);
svg.append("g").attr("transform","translate(0,260)")
  .call(d3.axisBottom(x));
svg.append("g").attr("transform","translate(50,0)")
  .call(d3.axisLeft(y).ticks(4));
svg.selectAll(".bar").data(data).join("rect")
  .attr("class","bar")
  .attr("x",d=>x(d.topic))
  .attr("y",d=>y(d.count))
  .attr("width",x.bandwidth())
  .attr("height",d=>260-y(d.count));
</script>
show answer

Key code (added to the skeleton; 7 daily values per topic, bar = weekly sum; SVG height raised to 340 for the sparklines; CSS .ref { stroke: #ddd; stroke-dasharray: 3 3; }):

const data = topics.map(t => { const days = d3.range(7)
  .map(() => Math.floor(Math.random()*50000) + 10000);
  return {topic: t, days, count: d3.sum(days)}; });
svg.selectAll("g").attr("class","axis");   // skeleton forgot this
const mean = d3.mean(data, d => d.count);  // one light line at the mean
svg.append("line").attr("class","ref").attr("x1",50).attr("x2",480).attr("y1",y(mean)).attr("y2",y(mean));
const tip = d3.select("body").append("div").attr("class","tooltip").style("opacity",0);
svg.selectAll(".bar").on("mouseover",(e,d)=>tip.style("opacity",1)
      .style("left",(e.pageX+8)+"px").style("top",(e.pageY-28)+"px")
      .html(d.topic+": "+d.count))
    .on("mouseout",()=>tip.style("opacity",0));
const sx = d3.scaleLinear().domain([0,6]).range([0,x.bandwidth()]);
data.forEach(d => { const sy = d3.scaleLinear()
  .domain(d3.extent(d.days)).range([325,290]);
  svg.append("path").attr("fill","none").attr("stroke","#555").attr("transform",`translate(${x(d.topic)},0)`)
   .attr("d", d3.line().x((v,i)=>sx(i)).y(v=>sy(v))(d.days)); });
  1. Gridlines: D3 axes draw none; add only one dashed light line at the mean.
  2. y from 0: domain [0, max]; a higher start would push the lie factor above 1.
  3. Flat colour: fill: steelblue, no gradient.
  4. Tooltip: mouseover shows topic and count; mouseout hides it. This is details on demand.
  5. Sparkline: 7-point line, no axes, under each bar, own min-max scale (shape, not size).

Extension: a select of the 5 topics; on change, fade other bars and draw that topic's 7-day line chart below.

Expected: 5 flat bars; tallest reaches y = 20 (240 px); bar width ≈ 56.8 px (step 430 ÷ 5.3 ≈ 81.1); weekly totals between 70,000 and 419,993.

Topic cards 6 topics · everything the pool tests

Tufte: data-ink, chartjunk, lie factor

Facts
  • Edward Tufte, The Visual Display of Quantitative Information (1983). Motto: "Above all else, show the data."
  • Data-ink ratio = ink encoding data ÷ total ink in the chart. Goal: make it as high as possible (maximum 1).
  • Chartjunk: ink that carries no data. Slide list: 3-D effects, gradient fills, redundant legends, heavy gridlines, decorative borders, vibrations, ducks.
  • Lie factor = size of effect shown in graphic ÷ size of effect in data. 1 = truthful, above 1 overstates, below 1 understates.
  • Small multiples (trellis or facet chart): the same small chart repeated per subset (district, year), all on the same axis scale.
  • Sparkline: a word-sized trend line placed inside text or a table cell, next to the number it describes.
How it works
  1. List every element in the chart: bars, axes, gridlines, legend, title, borders, shading
  2. For each element ask: if I erase it, does the chart lose information?
  3. Erase it if not: 3-D depth, gradients, borders, a legend for a single series
  4. Lighten what must stay: thin light axes, at most one light reference line
  5. Label data directly on the axis instead of in a legend
  6. Check the y-axis starts at 0 so the lie factor stays near 1
Why · pros · limits
  • Non-data ink competes with the data for the reader's attention, so the trend takes longer to see.
  • 3-D depth and perspective distort bar heights: never use 3-D for 2-D data.
  • A cut y-axis inflates differences; the lie factor puts a number on that distortion.
  • Small multiples let the eye compare all panels at once; a shared scale makes the comparison valid.
  • Limitation: the ratio is about what ink shows, not how little ink is used. One thick bar for one key value still scores high.
Compare
Data-inkChartjunk
What it isInk that encodes a valueInk that encodes nothing
ExamplesBar length, data point, trend line3-D shading, gradient, heavy grid, single-series legend
Erase testErasing loses informationErasing loses nothing
Effect on ratioRaises itLowers it
Small multiplesSparklineAnimation
ShowsSame chart per subset, gridTiny trend inside text/tableOne frame at a time
ComparisonAll subsets at once, same scaleTrend shape next to one numberViewer must remember earlier frames
AxesShared scaleNone; shows shape and direction onlyChanges over time
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a chart has 3-D bars, gradient fill, and a legend for one serieschartjunk; low data-ink ratio
the y-axis starts at 50, so a 10% rise looks like a doublinglie factor ≈ 10
the same line chart drawn for every district in a grid, same y-scalesmall multiples
a table of district case counts with a tiny trend line beside each numbersparklines
a dashboard designer removes gridlines and leaves one light line at the previous peakraising the data-ink ratio
Calculation

Lie factor (P2 slide 7). y-axis starts at 50 instead of 0. Data rises +10%, but the bar doubles in height (+100%). Lie factor = 100 ÷ 10 = 10.

Data-ink ratio. 1,000 units of ink, 300 draw bars: 300 ÷ 1,000 = 0.3. Remove 500 units of junk: 300 ÷ 500 = 0.6.

Step by step

  1. Given old and new data values → effect in data = (new − old) ÷ old
  2. Given old and new drawn heights → effect shown = (new height − old height) ÷ old height
  3. Given both effects → lie factor = shown ÷ data; compare with 1
  4. Given data-ink and total ink → ratio = data-ink ÷ total ink
  5. Given ink removed as junk → new total = old total − removed; data-ink unchanged; recompute ratio

Variants

  • Revenue 100 → 110 (+10%); bars drawn 40 px → 60 px (+50%). Lie factor = 50 ÷ 10 = 5 (overstates)
  • 400 data-ink + 600 decoration: ratio = 400 ÷ 1,000 = 0.4; delete all decoration: 400 ÷ 400 = 1.0
  • Data +20%, drawn +10%: lie factor = 10 ÷ 20 = 0.5 (understates)
Traps
  • Data-ink ratio is not about saving ink or printing cost (MCQ option (c)).
  • Lie factor uses percentage changes, not raw heights: divide the two changes, not the two bars.
  • Small multiples need the same scale on every panel, or cross-panel comparison is invalid.
  • Lie factor below 1 is also a distortion (understating), not "extra honest".
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
The data-ink ratio is the share of a chart's ink that encodes data, and Tufte says to maximise it.
3 marks
Tufte's data-ink ratio = ink encoding data ÷ total ink; erase any element whose removal loses no information (chartjunk such as 3-D bars or a single-series legend). Example: a cluttered quarterly sales chart becomes flat bars, labels on the axis, no gridlines.
5 marks

Definition: data-ink ratio = ink encoding data ÷ total ink (Tufte, 1983); maximise it.

  1. List every element.
  2. Erase what carries no data: 3-D, gradients, borders, redundant legend, heavy grid.
  3. Lighten what stays; label directly; start y at 0.

Example: 300 of 1,000 ink units are data (0.3); remove 500 units of junk and it becomes 300 ÷ 500 = 0.6. Related tools: lie factor (shown effect ÷ data effect; a y-axis starting at 50 gives ≈ 10), small multiples, sparklines.

Limitation: the ratio measures what ink shows, not minimal ink; some non-data ink (one reference line, axis labels) still helps reading.

Shneiderman's mantra and choosing the chart

Facts
  • Ben Shneiderman, "The Eyes Have It" (1996). Mantra: "Overview first, zoom and filter, then details on demand."
  • Seven data types: 1-D (linear), 2-D (map), 3-D (world), temporal, multi-dimensional, tree, network.
  • Seven tasks (P2 slide 9), the same for every data type: overview, zoom, filter, details-on-demand, relate, history, extract. P1 lists only the first five.
  • Treemap (Shneiderman, 1991): nested rectangles, area ∝ size. Dynamic queries: sliders and buttons that redraw the chart in under 100 ms (ancestor of Tableau's filter shelf).
  • Anscombe's Quartet (1973): same mean x 9.0, mean y 7.5, variance x 11.0, r 0.816, fit y = 3 + 0.5x; four different shapes.
  • Pie chart: only for ≤ 4 categories and the question "what fraction of the whole?" People judge angles worse than lengths.
  • Choropleth: regions shaded by a rate or proportion. For absolute counts use a proportional bubble map.
How it works
  1. Overview: show the whole dataset first (national total, whole map)
  2. Zoom: narrow to a region or time range of interest
  3. Filter: drop items you do not need (dropdown, slider)
  4. Details on demand: hover or click to get the raw record of one item
  5. Relate: cross-highlight the same item across linked charts
  6. To pick each chart: write the question, classify the data type, take the recommended chart, check the avoid list
Why · pros · limits
  • The user needs global context before knowing what to filter; filtering first hides where to look.
  • Details for every item at once would swamp the screen, so they appear only on request.
  • Equal summary statistics can hide different shapes (Anscombe), so plot before trusting a mean: visualization is the first quality check.
  • Bar lengths on a common baseline are read more accurately than angles or areas.
  • Choropleths of raw counts just show where people live; rates remove population size.
Compare
Data typeExampleBest chart
1-D linearRanked listBar chart, histogram
2-D mapDistrictsChoropleth, bubble map
3-D worldMRI, CT volumes3-D scatter, volume render
TemporalTime seriesLine, area, calendar heatmap
Multi-dimensional / treeCensus (10+ vars) / file systemScatterplot matrix, parallel coords / treemap, sunburst
NetworkSocial graphForce-directed graph, chord diagram
QuestionUseAvoid
How did X change over time?Line, area, calendar heatmapPie chart
Which category is largest?Horizontal bar (more than 5 cats), grouped bar3-D pie
How is X spread across regions?Choropleth (rate), bubble map (count)Plain bar
What is the spread of X?Histogram, box plot, violinLine chart
Does X predict Y?Scatterplot, scatterplot matrixBar chart
Pattern across more than 3 variables?Parallel coordinates, t-SNE/UMAP3-D bar
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
the dashboard opens on a national total, then a state dropdown, then a hover tooltipShneiderman's mantra
a date-range slider redraws the chart as it movesdynamic query (zoom and filter)
disk usage by department drawn as nested rectangles sized by storagetreemap (tree data)
show how monthly cholera cases changed over three yearsline chart (temporal)
show dengue cases per 100,000 people by districtchoropleth map (2-D)
Traps
  • Overview comes first. "Filter first" is the classic wrong option.
  • A pie chart for more than 5 categories is never recommended (T/F trap).
  • A histogram shows spread; a treemap shows hierarchy, not spread.
  • The same r = 0.816 does not mean the same relationship (Anscombe).
  • Seven data types (1-D … network) and seven tasks (overview … extract) are different lists; do not mix them.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Shneiderman's mantra is "overview first, zoom and filter, then details on demand".
3 marks
Shneiderman (1996): show the whole data first, let the user zoom and filter, then give one item's details on request. The data type picks the chart: line for time, horizontal bar for categories, choropleth for regional rates, histogram for spread. Example: Google Maps shows the country, you zoom to a city, then click a shop.
5 marks

Mantra: overview first, zoom and filter, then details on demand (Shneiderman, 1996).

  1. Overview: whole dataset.
  2. Zoom/filter: narrow the subset (sliders = dynamic queries).
  3. Details on demand: raw record on hover or click.

Chart by data type: temporal → line; categorical → horizontal bar; 2-D → choropleth (rate); distribution → histogram; correlation → scatterplot; tree → treemap; network → force-directed graph. Why plot at all: Anscombe's four datasets share mean, variance and r = 0.816 but differ in shape. Limitation: the mantra fixes interaction order, not how clean the chart is; Tufte covers that.

Overplotting: alpha, hexbin, KDE

Facts
  • Overplotting: with millions of points, dots cover each other and the scatterplot becomes a solid blob.
  • Three fixes in increasing sophistication: alpha transparency, hexbin aggregation, KDE density contours.
  • Alpha transparency: each point nearly see-through (slide: α = 0.02), so dense areas look darker.
  • Hexbin: hexagonal grid, count points per hex, colour by count. 10 million points become about 10,000 cells.
  • KDE (kernel density estimation): a smooth density surface drawn as contour lines; shows shape, modes and tails.
How it works
  1. Spot the blob: the scatter shows only that data exists
  2. Try alpha: set opacity low so stacked points darken the area
  3. If dense areas all turn black, bin instead: divide the plane into hexagons
  4. Count the points in each hexagon and colour it by the count
  5. For shape, estimate a KDE and draw contours at chosen probability levels
Why · pros · limits
  • Alpha keeps relative density, but stacked points saturate to full black at very high densities.
  • Hexbin keeps the count per area and draws thousands of cells instead of millions of dots, so it stays interactive.
  • KDE keeps the shape of the distribution: peaks (modes), clusters and tails.
  • Limitation of binning: individual points and outliers inside a cell are lost.
Compare
AlphaHexbinKDE contour
HowLow opacity per pointCount per hexagon, colour by countSmooth density, contour lines
PreservesRelative densityExact count per areaShape, modes, tails
WeaknessSaturates at high densityHides points inside a cellSmooths away detail; needs a bandwidth
Marks drawnAll N points≈ number of cellsA few contour lines
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a scatter of 10 million transactions is a solid colouroverplotting
the analyst needs the count of points in each region of the plothexbin
the analyst needs to see two peaks in the dataKDE density contour
points made 2% opaque, but the centre is still uniformly blackalpha saturation; switch to hexbin or KDE
Calculation

Hexbin (P1 slide 17): 10,000,000 points → ≈ 10,000 hex cells. Reduction = 10,000,000 ÷ 10,000 = 1,000× fewer shapes.

Alpha saturation (standard compositing, explains "fails at very high densities"): opacity after n stacked points = 1 − (1 − α)ⁿ. At α = 0.02, n = 100 gives 0.867; n = 200 gives 0.982.

Step by step

  1. Given points and cells → reduction = points ÷ cells
  2. Given α → single-point transparency = 1 − α
  3. Given n stacked points → combined opacity = 1 − (1 − α)ⁿ
  4. Given target opacity p → n = ln(1 − p) ÷ ln(1 − α)

Variants

  • 2,000,000 GPS pings into 500 hexes: 2,000,000 ÷ 500 = 4,000×
  • α = 0.02, n = 10: 1 − 0.98¹⁰ = 0.183
  • Points for 50% opacity at α = 0.02: ln 0.5 ÷ ln 0.98 ≈ 34.3, so 35 points
Traps
  • Alpha is the simplest fix but still fails at very high density; do not call it the best.
  • Hexbin shows counts, KDE shows shape; do not swap what each preserves.
  • Hexbins are True for reducing overplotting (T/F).
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Overplotting is when millions of scatter points overlap into a solid blob that shows nothing.
3 marks
Overplotting hides density because points cover each other. Fixes: alpha transparency (darker = denser), hexbin (count per hexagon), KDE contours (shape of the distribution). Example: 10 million points become ≈ 10,000 hex cells, 1,000× fewer shapes.
5 marks

Definition: one dot per record; at millions of records the dots merge into a blob.

  1. Alpha α = 0.02: keeps relative density; saturates when hundreds of points stack.
  2. Hexbin: count per hexagon, colour by count; keeps exact density; 10 M points → ≈ 10,000 cells.
  3. KDE contours: smooth density; keeps shape, modes, tails.

Example: 10 M mobile payments by amount and hour: hexbin shows the busiest cells, KDE shows a lunch and an evening peak. Limitation: binning hides individual outliers inside a cell.
