Long answer
What is overplotting? Explain three fixes and what each preserves.
show answer
Millions of points merge into a blob. Alpha (α = 0.02) preserves relative density but saturates. Hexbin preserves count per area: 10 M points → ≈ 10,000 cells. KDE contours preserve shape, modes and tails. Limitation: binning hides outliers inside cells.

Latency: data cubes and AQP

Facts
  • Analysts expect a dashboard filter to answer in under 1 second; a Hive query over 100 billion rows takes minutes.
  • Fact table: one row per event (each sale). Data cube: pre-computed aggregates for combinations of dimensions, e.g. district × date × product × channel.
  • The cube is built by a scheduled (nightly) Spark/Hive job and holds millions of rows, not billions.
  • AQP (approximate query processing): answer from a sample in milliseconds. Tools: Apache DataSketches, BlinkDB.
  • Cube trade-off: staleness up to 24 h for speed. AQP trade-off: accuracy for speed.
How it works
  1. A nightly Spark or Hive job scans the fact table once
  2. It writes SUM/COUNT aggregates for each combination of dimensions
  3. Tableau or Superset points at the cube, not the raw table
  4. A filter such as "Q1 2024 only" reads millions of pre-summed rows
  5. The answer returns in under 1 second; the cube refreshes on the next run
Why · pros · limits
  • Reading millions of rows is far faster than scanning billions on every click.
  • Staleness: the cube is only as fresh as its last build (up to 24 h).
  • Fixed questions: only pre-computed dimensions can be sliced; a new one needs a rebuild.
  • Storage and batch compute grow with the number of dimension combinations.
  • AQP is fine for dashboards but not for financial reconciliation, where exact totals are required.
Compare
Raw Hive queryData cubeAQP
ReadsAll 100 B rowsMillions of pre-summed rowsA sample
LatencyMinutesUnder 1 sMilliseconds
AccuracyExact, currentExact, but staleApproximate
CostSlow every clickNightly build, storageError margin
Unfit forInteractive useLive data, new dimensionsFinancial reconciliation
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a Tableau filter on a 100-billion-row Hive table takes minuteslatency challenge
a nightly job writes sales totals per district per day per productdata cube (pre-aggregation)
the dashboard shows yesterday's numbers in the morningcube staleness trade-off
a dashboard returns an estimate in milliseconds from a sampleAQP (DataSketches, BlinkDB)
Calculation

Slide: 100 billion fact rows; cube of millions of rows. Taking 5 million: 100,000,000,000 ÷ 5,000,000 = 20,000× fewer rows to read; the cube is 99.995% smaller.

Step by step

  1. Given distinct values per dimension → finest cube rows ≤ d₁ × d₂ × … × dₖ
  2. Given fact rows and cube rows → reduction = fact rows ÷ cube rows
  3. Given k dimensions → group-by combinations = 2ᵏ (including the grand total)

Variants

  • 365 dates × 64 districts × 50 products × 4 channels = 4,672,000 rows at most
  • 100 billion ÷ 4,672,000 ≈ 21,404× fewer rows
  • k = 4 dimensions → 2⁴ = 16 group-by combinations
Traps
  • The cube's trade-off is staleness (and fixed dimensions), not wrong answers; AQP's trade-off is accuracy.
  • The cube is built in batch ahead of time, not when the user clicks.
  • Finest cube size is an upper bound: only combinations that occur are stored.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
A data cube is a set of pre-computed aggregate tables over combinations of dimensions, read instead of the raw fact table.
3 marks
A nightly Spark/Hive job summarises a billion-row fact table into a cube of millions of rows, so a dashboard filter reads the cube in under 1 second. Trade-off: data up to 24 h stale. Example: 100 billion ÷ 5 million = 20,000× fewer rows.
5 marks

Definition: a data cube stores pre-computed aggregates (SUM, COUNT) for every combination of dimensions such as district × date × product × channel.

  1. Nightly Spark/Hive job scans the fact table once.
  2. Writes the cube: millions of rows.
  3. Tableau/Superset filters read the cube in under 1 s.

Example: 100 billion rows → 5 million: 20,000× fewer to read. Limitation: staleness up to 24 h, only pre-built dimensions; AQP is the alternative, trading accuracy for speed.

Practise this topic in 10 formats
MCQ
What is the main trade-off of a nightly pre-aggregated data cube?
  • (a) Queries take minutes
  • (b) Data can be up to 24 hours stale
  • (c) Answers are approximate
  • (d) It needs WebSockets
show answer
(b) Data can be up to 24 hours stale
The cube is exact but only as fresh as its last build.
Fill in the blank
Approximate query processing tools named on the slide are Apache DataSketches and ______.
show answer
BlinkDB
Define / list
Define fact table and data cube.
show answer
Fact table: one row per event. Data cube: pre-computed aggregates of the fact table for combinations of dimensions.
Explain the steps
Explain how a data cube gives sub-second dashboard filters.
show answer
Nightly job scans the fact table; writes aggregates per dimension combination; dashboard queries the cube (millions of rows) instead of billions; answer in under 1 s.
Why / justify
Why is AQP unsuitable for financial reconciliation?
show answer
It returns an estimate from a sample; reconciliation needs exact totals.
Distinguish
Distinguish a data cube from AQP.
show answer
Cube: exact pre-computed aggregates, stale until rebuilt. AQP: computed on demand from a sample, current but approximate.
Scenario
A retailer's Superset dashboard sits on 100 billion sales rows in Hive. Each filter takes four minutes. Finance also needs exact monthly totals.
i) Name the challenge. ii) Fix for sub-second filters? iii) Its trade-off? iv) Why not use AQP for finance?
show answer
i) Latency. ii) A pre-aggregated data cube. iii) Staleness up to 24 h. iv) AQP is approximate.
One-step calc
A 100-billion-row fact table is summarised into 5 million cube rows. Reduction factor?
show answer
100,000,000,000 ÷ 5,000,000 = 20,000.
Full calc
Dimensions: 365 dates, 64 districts, 50 products, 4 channels. (a) Max finest cube rows? (b) Reduction vs 100 billion rows? (c) Number of group-by combinations?
show answer
(a) 365 × 64 × 50 × 4 = 4,672,000. (b) 100,000,000,000 ÷ 4,672,000 ≈ 21,404. (c) 2⁴ = 16.
Long answer
Explain the latency challenge in big data visualization and two solutions with trade-offs.
show answer
Analysts need sub-second filters but raw Hive queries over 100 B rows take minutes. Data cube: nightly pre-aggregation to millions of rows, read in under 1 s; trade-off staleness up to 24 h and fixed dimensions. AQP: sample-based estimate in milliseconds; trade-off accuracy, not for financial reconciliation.

Streaming dashboards and tools

Facts
  • Pipeline: producers → Kafka (durable event log) → Spark Structured Streaming or Flink window aggregation → time-series DB → Grafana or Kibana.
  • Slide window: 1-minute tumbling (fixed, non-overlapping) window. Time-series DBs: InfluxDB, Prometheus, Elasticsearch.
  • Poll: Grafana re-queries the DB on a schedule (5 s refresh). Push: for sub-second updates, a WebSocket server pushes to a D3.js chart.
  • Tools: business users → Tableau/Power BI; custom web charts → D3.js; real-time ops → Grafana; data-lake BI → Superset; logs → Kibana.
  • D3.js (Data-Driven Documents) binds data to DOM elements, so output is interactive SVG in the browser, not PNG/PDF files.
How it works
  1. Events arrive at a Kafka topic in real time
  2. Spark Structured Streaming or Flink aggregates over a window (e.g. 1-minute tumbling)
  3. Aggregates are written to a time-series DB
  4. Grafana or Kibana polls the DB every few seconds and redraws
  5. For sub-second needs, the server pushes each aggregate over a WebSocket to a D3.js chart
Why · pros · limits
  • A browser cannot redraw a chart for each of thousands of raw events per second; windows turn them into a few points per minute.
  • Grafana reads time-series DBs natively and auto-refreshes, so it suits ops monitoring with no custom code.
  • WebSocket push avoids polling delay, needed for live election counts or stock tickers.
  • Tableau and Power BI support only limited scheduled refresh; D3 can do real time but needs custom development.
Compare
ToolBest forReal-time
TableauBusiness dashboards, drag-and-dropLimited
Power BIMicrosoft ecosystem, executive reportsLimited
D3.jsCustom interactive web chartsYes (WebSocket)
GrafanaReal-time metrics, ops dashboardsYes (5 s poll)
SupersetOpen-source BI on data lakes (Hive, Spark SQL)Limited
KibanaLog search on ElasticsearchYes
Poll (Grafana)Push (WebSocket + D3)
Who startsBrowser asks every few secondsServer sends on each new value
LatencySecondsSub-second
EffortConfigurationCustom code
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
server metrics and error rates, refresh every 5 secondsGrafana
live election count updating many times a second in a news pageWebSocket push to D3.js
a manager wants drag-and-drop monthly sales reportsTableau or Power BI
searching application logs stored in ElasticsearchKibana
open-source BI over Hive tables on a Hadoop data lakeApache Superset
Calculation

Pool A2: 10,000 tweets/s with a 1-minute tumbling window → 10,000 × 60 = 600,000 tweets summarised into one point per topic. Per 5 s refresh: 50,000 tweets. Per day: 10,000 × 86,400 = 864,000,000.

Step by step

  1. Given event rate and window length → events per window = rate × seconds
  2. Given refresh interval → events between refreshes = rate × interval
  3. Given update interval → updates per day = 86,400 s ÷ interval

Variants

  • Update every 5 minutes (A1(b)): 24 × 60 ÷ 5 = 288 points per day
  • 1,000 events/s, 1-min window: 60,000 events per point
Traps
  • Tumbling windows do not overlap (each event counted once); sliding windows overlap.
  • D3 can do real time, but the 5 s ops dashboard answer is Grafana: native time-series support, no custom code.
  • D3.js output is SVG in the DOM, not static images (T/F).
  • Send aggregates, never raw events, to the browser.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Grafana is the tool for real-time ops dashboards that poll a time-series database every few seconds.
3 marks
Streaming charts use Kafka → Spark Structured Streaming window aggregation → time-series DB → Grafana polling every 5 s. For sub-second updates the server pushes over a WebSocket to a D3.js chart. Example: 10,000 tweets/s in a 1-minute window gives one point per 600,000 tweets.
5 marks

Problem: live data (ride counts, fraud alerts) needs charts that update continuously.

  1. Events land in a Kafka topic.
  2. Spark Structured Streaming/Flink aggregates per 1-minute tumbling window.
  3. Results go to InfluxDB/Prometheus/Elasticsearch.
  4. Grafana/Kibana polls every few seconds; or WebSocket pushes to D3.js for sub-second updates.

Example: 10,000 events/s → 600,000 per window → one point. Tools: Tableau/Power BI for business, Superset for data lakes. Limitation: window size trades smoothness for delay.

Case study: NYT COVID-19 tracker

Facts
  • NYT Graphics Desk: ≈ 50 journalists, designers and engineers; 6 million readers per day. Mike Bostock built D3.js there.
  • Data: Johns Hopkins CSSE, CDC, 50 state health departments, with inconsistent schemas, missing values and retroactive corrections.
  • Pipeline: Python scrapers → pandas cleaning → GitHub CSV → D3 charts updated every hour.
  • Design: 50-state small-multiples grid, no gridlines (one light line at the prior peak), 7-day rolling averages, county choropleths.
  • Mantra: national total (overview) → state dropdown and date slider (filter) → hover tooltip (details).
How it works
  1. Scrape data from many sources
  2. Normalise schemas and fix missing values in pandas
  3. Publish the clean CSV to GitHub
  4. Compute 7-day rolling averages
  5. Render D3 charts hourly, with data-table alternatives for screen readers
Why · pros · limits
  • The hardest work was data cleaning across 50 states, not drawing charts.
  • Corrections made some daily changes negative, so charts had to allow negative bars.
  • A 7-day average removes day-of-week reporting dips; the window choice changes the story.
  • Small multiples let readers compare all states at once, unlike an animated map.
  • At 6 M readers a day, accessibility (tables, colour-blind-safe palettes) reaches many people.
Compare
7-day14-day
Removes weekly cycleYesYes
Reacts to a real turnWithin daysLater
NoiseMoreLess
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
a state revises yesterday's cases down, so today's change is negativedata quality shows up as a design problem (negative bars)
every chart has a data table for screen readersaccessibility lesson
a grid of 50 state charts replaces an animated mapsmall multiples beat animation
outside groups build 1,000+ dashboards from a published CSVGitHub as data infrastructure
Traps
  • The lesson is that cleaning, not rendering, was the biggest effort.
  • A rolling average is an editorial choice, not a neutral fix.
  • Deuteranopia and protanopia are red-green colour-blindness: avoid red/green scales.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
The NYT COVID tracker showed that cleaning data from 50 sources was harder than drawing the charts.
3 marks
The NYT scraped 50 state feeds, cleaned them in pandas, published CSV on GitHub and rendered hourly D3 charts. It used small multiples, no gridlines and a mantra-style flow (national → state → tooltip). Example lesson: corrections forced charts to show negative bars.
5 marks

Case: NYT Graphics Desk COVID-19 tracker (2020-2022), 6 M readers/day.

  1. Scrapers → pandas cleaning → GitHub CSV → hourly D3 charts.
  2. Tufte: no gridlines, 50-state small multiples.
  3. Shneiderman: national total → state dropdown → tooltip.
  4. 7-day rolling average to remove weekday dips.

Lessons: data quality is a visualisation problem; accessibility at scale; small multiples beat animation; rolling windows are editorial; open CSV enabled 1,000+ dashboards. Limitation: good design cannot fix bad source data.

Chapter 6 · Week 10Big data in real applications

This chapter applies the course's stack (Kafka, Spark, HDFS, Hive, Grafana) to three domains: social media graphs, time series forecasting and healthcare data. A flu surveillance case study shows what big data signals can and cannot replace.

Exam question pool 22 questions from the slides · click a question for its answer

MCQ
Q1. Facebook's Prophet library is primarily designed for:
  • (a) Graph analytics and community detection in social networks
  • (b) Real-time stream processing of events
  • (c) Time series forecasting with decomposable trend, seasonality, and holiday components
  • (d) Text sentiment classification using deep learning
show answer
(c) Time series forecasting with decomposable trend, seasonality, and holiday components
Prophet fits y(t) = g(t) + s(t) + h(t) + ε(t).
MCQ
Q2. Which algorithm is most commonly used for community detection in large-scale social network graphs?
  • (a) K-Means clustering
  • (b) PageRank
  • (c) Louvain algorithm
  • (d) ARIMA
show answer
(c) Louvain algorithm
It maximises modularity in O(n log n); PageRank ranks importance, not groups.
MCQ
Q3. HIPAA in the United States primarily regulates the privacy and security of:
  • (a) Financial transaction data and bank records
  • (b) Healthcare data including patient records and medical information
  • (c) Social media user data and behavioural profiles
  • (d) Government data retention and public records
show answer
(b) Healthcare data including patient records and medical information
HIPAA is the Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act; financial data falls under GLBA.
MCQ
Q4. DeepMind's AlphaFold used big data and deep learning to solve:
  • (a) Real-time financial fraud detection at bank-card transaction scale
  • (b) Social network influence maximisation over billion-node graphs
  • (c) The protein structure prediction problem, predicting 3D structure from amino acid sequence
  • (d) Time series anomaly detection in IoT sensor streams
show answer
(c) The protein structure prediction problem, predicting 3D structure from amino acid sequence
Trained on 170,000 PDB structures with MSAs from 250M sequences; 2024 Nobel Prize in Chemistry.
True/False
1. Google Flu Trends successfully replaced traditional CDC flu surveillance and has been used as the primary source ever since.
show answer
False. GFT over-predicted flu by 2× in 2012-2013 and was discontinued.
Corrected: Google Flu Trends failed, and CDC still relies on clinical reporting, with big data signals only as a supplement.
True/False
2. Time series data is order-sensitive: randomly shuffling the records in a time series changes the analysis result.
show answer
True. Autocorrelation, trend and seasonality exist only in time order; shuffling destroys them.
True/False
3. Social network graphs are typically sparse, with most nodes connected to only a small fraction of all possible neighbours.
show answer
True. Real social networks are scale-free (power-law degrees): most nodes have few links and a few hubs have millions. Twitter had 1.47B edges among 41.7M users, about 35 follows each out of millions possible.
True/False
4. Medical imaging data such as MRI and CT scans is classified as structured data because it is stored in files.
show answer
False. Being in a file does not make data structured.
Corrected: MRI and CT scans are unstructured binary DICOM files (200 MB to 2 GB), analysed by deep learning, not SQL.
Descriptive
D1. Describe three types of analysis commonly performed on social media data (e.g., community detection, sentiment analysis, influence maximisation). For each, state one specific ethical risk that arises when the analysis is deployed at scale.
show answer
AnalysisWhat it doesEthical risk at scale
Community detectionLouvain finds groups more densely linked inside than outside, by maximising modularity.Profiling: group labels expose political or religious ties users never shared.
Sentiment analysisVADER scores each post in [−1, +1]; > 0.05 positive, < −0.05 negative.Suppressing dissent: flagging people who criticise a government policy.
Influence maximisationPicks k seed users for widest spread; greedy gives ≥ 63% of optimal.Manipulation: spreading misinformation or microtargeting vulnerable users.

Safeguard for all three: transparency and purpose limitation.

Descriptive
D2. What is the difference between ARIMA and Facebook Prophet for time series forecasting? Under what conditions would you prefer Prophet over ARIMA? When might ARIMA still be the better choice?
show answer

ARIMA(p, d, q) predicts from past p values and past q errors after differencing d times to make the series stationary. Prophet adds up trend with changepoints, Fourier seasonality and a holiday term: y = g + s + h + ε.

Prefer Prophet when the series has several seasonalities, holiday effects (Eid, Puja), trend shifts, missing data or outliers, or when thousands of series must be fitted automatically (5,000 SKUs in 12 minutes with applyInPandas).

ARIMA is still better for a short, stationary series with no strong seasonality and little data, where an expert can tune one important series closely.

Descriptive
D3. Identify three data integration and privacy challenges specific to healthcare big data analytics. For each, describe one technical mitigation strategy (e.g., differential privacy, federated learning, de-identification).
show answer
ChallengeMitigation
Records carry names, DOB, NID, phone; HIPAA bars research use as-isDe-identification: safe harbour removes the 18 identifiers, or expert determination certifies low risk
Models need 1M+ records, but data cannot leave each hospital (consent, PDPA)Federated learning: train at each hospital, share only model updates
Published counts for small areas can reveal one patientDifferential privacy: add calibrated noise to aggregates

Integration itself: FHIR, Parquet genomics, Kafka wearables and DICOM images meet in one HDFS/S3 lake with a Hive Metastore.

Analytical
A1. Flu Outbreak Early-Warning System for Bangladesh: Design a big data system to detect flu outbreaks in Bangladesh using Twitter/Facebook posts and pharmacy purchase records from major pharmacy chains. (a) How would you collect and ingest these two data sources? (Specify tools: Kafka, Sqoop, Flume, REST API) (b) What features would you extract from each source for a prediction model? (Name specific NLP features and pharmacy signal metrics) (c) What model would you use, and how would you validate it against ground-truth CDC/DGHS surveillance data? (d) What are the ethical risks of this system? How would you mitigate them?
show answer

Design: sources → Kafka → Spark Streaming → alert engine + Grafana (the CDC pattern).

PartSocial postsPharmacy sales
(a) IngestREST APIs (X API v2, Facebook Pages), keyword filter in Bangla and English, producer writes to Kafka topic social_rawSqoop for nightly bulk import from the chains' databases; Flume ships POS logs to Kafka topic pharmacy_sales
(b) FeaturesDaily flu-keyword post count per district; share of symptom terms (fever, cough, 'jor'); first-person illness flag; VADER/Bangla sentiment; ratio to 4-week baselineDaily paracetamol and cough-syrup units per district; week-on-week % change; z-score vs same week last year

(c) Model: Prophet per district on DGHS weekly ILI counts, yearly seasonality, Eid holidays, social and pharmacy features as extra regressors; run districts in parallel with applyInPandas. Validate with a rolling backtest against DGHS counts: error (MAE), lead time, hit rate, false alarms, and test on an unseen season (GFT had r² = 0.97 in-sample and still failed).

(d) Ethics: privacy (keep district counts only, drop user IDs, add differential-privacy noise); panic from false alarms (an officer confirms before any announcement); bias against rural and older people who post less (weight with pharmacy and DGHS data); misuse for watching individuals (purpose limitation, access logs, PDPA consent).

Analytical
A2. Retail Demand Forecasting Pipeline: A large Bangladeshi supermarket chain needs 90-day demand forecasts for 5,000 store–product SKU combinations. Design the end-to-end big data pipeline: (a) data ingestion from POS systems; (b) storage and partitioning strategy in HDFS/S3; (c) forecasting model choice (ARIMA vs. Prophet vs. LSTM) and justification; (d) how you parallelise model fitting across 5,000 SKUs using Spark; (e) how forecasts are served to the inventory management dashboard. Identify the primary bottleneck in your pipeline.
show answer
StageDesign
(a) IngestTills send sale events to Kafka topic pos_sales; Sqoop bulk-loads history; Spark aggregates to (sku_id, date, units)
(b) StorageRaw Parquet on HDFS/S3 partitioned by date; daily aggregate Hive table partitioned by date, bucketed by sku_id; forecasts partitioned by run_date
(c) ModelProphet: weekly and yearly seasonality, holidays (Eid, Puja, Pohela Boishakh), automatic changepoints, tolerates gaps. ARIMA needs hand tuning per series; LSTM needs much data, GPUs and is a black box
(d) Parallel fitdaily.groupBy("sku_id").applyInPandas(fit, schema); each group goes to one task, Prophet fits inside the Pandas UDF
(e) ServeWrite Parquet to Hive/S3; load latest run into a serving table; dashboard (Superset/Grafana) shows yhat with bands and reorder alerts

Numbers: 8 h = 480 min sequential; 12 min on 20 executors; speedup = 480 / 12 = 40×; 5.76 s per model; 250 models per executor.

Bottleneck: model fitting (Prophet via Stan, the 8-hour step). Skewed SKUs with long histories finish last; cap history and add cores.

Discussion
1. Kwak et al. found Twitter's average shortest path is 4.12 hops over 41.7M users. If a misinformation tweet is retweeted by a high-PageRank account, estimate how many users it reaches within 3 hops assuming an average out-degree of 45. What graph property makes this estimate an overestimate? [CLO4 — Apply]
show answer

Estimate: 45 + 45² + 45³ = 45 + 2,025 + 91,125 = 93,195 users (0.22% of 41.7M).

For an overestimate: clustering. Followers overlap, so the tree counts the same people again; most viewers also do not retweet.

Against: a high-PageRank account may have over a million followers, so hop 1 alone can beat 45.

Discussion
2. A Prophet model for weekly hospital admissions has strong annual and weekly seasonality but misses a sudden COVID-19 wave onset. Identify which Prophet component should detect this changepoint and explain what prior hyperparameter controls the model's sensitivity to new changepoints. [CLO4 — Analyse]
show answer

Component: the trend g(t). A wave is a structural break, not a repeating cycle or a listed holiday.

Hyperparameter: changepoint_prior_scale, the scale of the sparse prior on the slope changes δ. Default 0.05 keeps the trend stiff and smooths over the wave; raising it (e.g. 0.5) lets the trend bend.

Against raising it: the trend then chases noise and the forecast band widens, so tune on held-out weeks.

Discussion
3. Design a dengue fever surveillance system for Bangladesh using BioSense-style ED data, Twitter signals, and Prophet forecasting. Specify: data sources, pipeline architecture (Kafka + Spark), model inputs, and how you would validate the system before policy use. [CLO4 — Design]
show answer
  • Sources: ED visits (FHIR) as main signal, NS1 lab results, dengue tweets as leading signal, rainfall.
  • Pipeline: Kafka topics → Spark Streaming de-identifies and aggregates per district → Parquet → nightly Prophet per district via applyInPandas → alerts + Grafana.
  • Inputs: weekly dengue-like visits, yearly (monsoon) seasonality, Eid holidays, tweets and rainfall as regressors.
  • Validate: backtest on past seasons and an unseen season; compare with and without Twitter.

For: earlier warning. Against: tweets skew urban and young, so ED data stays primary.

Live Exercise
Task. Live Exercise — Prophet Flu Forecasting in Python. Task (25 minutes, pairs): Fit a Prophet model to synthetic weekly flu incidence data and produce a 12-week forecast. Install: pip install prophet matplotlib pandas
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
from prophet import Prophet
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
dates = pd.date_range("2021-01-04",
                      periods=156, freq="W")
# Annual flu seasonality + noise
ili = (5 + 3*np.sin(
          2*np.pi*np.arange(156)/52
          - np.pi/2)
       + rng.normal(0, 0.5, 156)).clip(0)
df = pd.DataFrame({"ds": dates, "y": ili})

# Optional: add Eid holiday
holidays = pd.DataFrame({
   "holiday": "Eid",
   "ds":       pd.to_datetime([
                 "2021-05-13","2022-05-03",
                 "2023-04-22"]),
   "lower_window": 0,
   "upper_window": 3,
})

m = Prophet(
    yearly_seasonality=True,
    weekly_seasonality=False,
    changepoint_prior_scale=0.05,
    holidays=holidays)
m.fit(df)
future = m.make_future_dataframe(
    periods=12, freq="W")
forecast = m.predict(future)
m.plot(forecast)
m.plot_components(forecast)
plt.show()
show answer

Run the skeleton, then refit with the changes below.

m = Prophet(yearly_seasonality=True,
            weekly_seasonality=False,
            changepoint_prior_scale=0.05,   # try 0.5
            holidays=holidays,
            interval_width=0.95)            # 95% band
m.fit(df)
fc = m.predict(m.make_future_dataframe(periods=12, freq="W"))
m.plot(fc); m.plot_components(fc)

Expected: dates run 2021-01-10 to 2023-12-31 (weeks end Sunday); the forecast covers 2024-01-07 to 2024-03-24 and rises from the yearly low (true curve 2.0) towards about 4.3.

Live Exercise
1. Generate 3 years of synthetic weekly ILI data with annual seasonality + noise.
show answer
The skeleton does it: 156 weekly dates (156 / 52 = 3 years), a sine with period 52 weeks running from 2 to 8 around a mean of 5, plus noise with SD 0.5. Columns must be named ds and y.
Live Exercise
2. Fit Prophet with yearly and weekly seasonality.
show answer
yearly_seasonality=True fits the annual Fourier wave (P = 365.25). Keep weekly_seasonality=False: with one point per week, always a Sunday, there is no within-week pattern to learn.
Live Exercise
3. Plot the forecast with 95% uncertainty intervals.
show answer
Pass interval_width=0.95 (default is 80%), then m.plot(forecast). Expected: data dots, the yhat line and a band from yhat_lower to yhat_upper that widens into the future.
Live Exercise
4. Add a holiday for “Eid” (spike in healthcare visits) and observe the effect.
show answer
Expected effect is near zero: the synthetic data has no Eid spike, and the 2022 window (05-03 to 05-06) contains no Sunday, so it matches no point. Add +3 to the Eid weeks and the components plot shows an Eid bar near +3.
Live Exercise
5. Change changepoint_prior_scale from 0.05 to 0.5 and compare trend flexibility.
show answer
At 0.05 the trend stays nearly flat around 5 (the true trend). At 0.5 it wiggles, absorbs noise and part of the seasonal wave, and the future band widens. Higher reacts faster to real breaks but overfits stable data.

Topic cards 6 topics · everything the pool tests

Social media as a graph: communities, influence, Twitter's shape

Facts
  • Four data dimensions of social media: graph-structured, temporal, text (NLP), multimedia.
  • The graph (who follows or retweets whom) is the main object of analysis, not the single post.
  • Community: nodes more densely linked to each other than to the rest of the graph. Found with Louvain (Blondel 2008), O(n log n).
  • Modularity: fraction of edges inside communities minus the fraction expected by chance. Louvain maximises it.
  • Influence maximisation: choose k seed users so a message spreads furthest. NP-hard; Kempe's greedy method guarantees (1 − 1/e) ≈ 63% of optimal.
  • Kwak 2010: 41.7M users, 1.47B directed edges, 22.1% mutual follows, power law α ≈ 2.276, average shortest path 4.12 hops, 85% of trends are news.
  • Kwak 2010 impact: seeding a campaign at high-PageRank users gives measurably different spread from seeding at high-follower users, so follower count is not the same as influence.
How it works
  1. Louvain start: every node is its own community
  2. Move each node into the neighbour community that gives the biggest modularity gain
  3. Collapse each community into one super-node (edges between communities become weighted edges)
  4. Repeat the move and collapse steps on the smaller graph
  5. Stop when no move raises modularity (gain is zero)
Why · pros · limits
  • Louvain is greedy and local, so it scales to billion-node graphs in Spark GraphX.
  • Modularity compares against chance, so a big group is not called a community just for being big.
  • Exact influence maximisation is NP-hard; greedy gives a proven 63% floor in reasonable time.
  • Twitter is sparse: 1.47B edges among 41.7M users is about 35 follows each, a tiny share of possible links.
  • Low reciprocity and fast news trends make Twitter a news medium, which is why health agencies watch it.
Compare
LouvainPageRankK-Means
AnswersWhich group is a node in?How important is a node?Which points are close in space?
InputGraph linksGraph linksFeature vectors
OutputCommunity labelsOne score per nodeCluster labels
GoalMaximise modularityScore by random-walk visit probability (no optimising)Minimise distance to centroid
Twitter (Kwak 2010)Facebook

If the question describes…Answer
find tightly linked groups of accounts, such as a coordinated bot networkcommunity detection (Louvain)
a ministry wants to pick 50 accounts to seed a vaccine message so it reaches the most peopleinfluence maximisation (Kempe greedy)
rank accounts by how important they are in the follow graphPageRank, not community detection

estimate how many users a tweet reaches in h hops with out-degree dtree estimate d + d² + … + d^h, an overestimate
Calculation

Discussion Q1 (P2 slide 15): out-degree d = 45, h = 3 hops, 41.7M users.

HopNew users
145
245² = 2,025
345³ = 91,125
Total93,195 (0.22% of 41.7M)

Step by step

  1. Given d = 45 -> hop 1 reaches 45 users
  2. Given hop 1 = 45 and d = 45 -> hop 2 = 45 × 45 = 2,025
  3. Given hop 2 = 2,025 -> hop 3 = 2,025 × 45 = 91,125
  4. Given hops 45, 2,025, 91,125 -> total = 93,195
  5. Given total 93,195 and 41.7M users -> share = 93,195 / 41,700,000 = 0.22%

Variants

  • d = 30, h = 3: 30 + 900 + 27,000 = 27,930 users.
  • d = 40, h = 2: 40 + 1,600 = 1,640 users.
  • d = 45, h = 4: adds 45⁴ = 4,100,625, total 4,193,820.
  • Greedy seeding, optimal reach 10,000: guaranteed at least 10,000 × (1 − 1/e) ≈ 6,321 users.
Traps
  • PageRank measures importance, not community membership; K-Means clusters vectors, not graph links.
  • The hop estimate is an upper bound: it counts shared followers again (clustering, overlapping neighbourhoods).
  • Kempe's result: the problem is NP-hard, the greedy answer is approximate (63%), not exact.
  • Sparse means few edges per node, not few nodes.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Community detection finds groups of nodes more densely linked to each other than to the rest of the graph, usually with the Louvain algorithm.
3 marks
Louvain starts with each node alone, greedily merges nodes to raise modularity (edges inside groups minus the chance level), collapses groups into super-nodes and repeats until modularity stops rising. It runs in O(n log n), so it handles billion-node graphs. Example: finding a bot network that retweets only itself.
5 marks

Social media data is a graph: users are nodes, follows and retweets are edges. Community detection (Louvain) finds densely linked groups: start with each node alone, merge to raise modularity, collapse into super-nodes, repeat until no gain. Influence maximisation picks k seeds for widest spread; it is NP-hard, so Kempe's greedy method is used with a (1 − 1/e) ≈ 63% guarantee. Example: Kwak 2010 found Twitter has 41.7M users, 22.1% mutual follows and 4.12 average hops, so it acts as a news medium. With out-degree 45, a tweet reaches at most 93,195 users in 3 hops. Limitation: that estimate ignores overlapping followers, so it is too high.

Sentiment (VADER), trend detection and ethics

Facts
  • VADER: Valence Aware Dictionary for Sentiment Reasoning (Hutto & Gilbert 2014), lexicon-and-rule based, tuned for social media text.
  • VADER handles capitalisation, punctuation (!!!), degree words (very, kind of), negation (not good) and emojis.
  • Compound score in [−1, +1]: > 0.05 positive, < −0.05 negative, otherwise neutral.
  • Trend detection: Locality-Sensitive Hashing (LSH) on character n-grams finds phrases whose frequency jumps against their own baseline.
  • Slide example: 10 per hour to 10,000 per hour is trending regardless of absolute volume. #QurbaniEid: 1,200× spike in 6 hours, 92% positive.
  • Ethical risks: suppressing dissent, microtargeting vulnerable users. Safeguards: transparency and purpose limitation.
How it works
  1. Read tweets into a Spark DataFrame (from Kafka)
  2. Apply VADER to each tweet with a UDF to get a compound score
  3. Label each tweet positive, neutral or negative with the 0.05 cut-offs
  4. Aggregate scores by time window and topic
  5. In parallel, count each phrase per hour and divide by its historical baseline to flag spikes
  6. Push the aggregates to the Grafana streaming dashboard (Week 9)
Why · pros · limits
  • Rules and a lexicon need no labelled training data, so VADER works out of the box on short, noisy posts.
  • Comparing to a phrase's own baseline catches a small topic that suddenly explodes, which raw counts would miss.
  • Sentiment on a spike tells organic celebration from coordinated activity (#QurbaniEid was 92% positive).
  • Limitation: an English lexicon misreads Bangla, slang and sarcasm, so it needs a Bangla lexicon added.
  • The same scores that track public mood can flag critics, so purpose limits are needed.
Compare
DoesEthical risk at scale
Community detectionFinds densely linked groupsProfiling: exposes political or religious ties
Sentiment analysisScores posts positive or negativeSuppressing dissent: flags critics of a policy
Influence maximisationPicks seed users for spreadManipulation: spreads misinformation, microtargets the vulnerable
Trend detectionFlags sudden frequency spikesAmplifies coordinated campaigns if not checked
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
classify posts as positive, neutral or negative, including emojis and 'NOT good'VADER sentiment analysis
a phrase normally seen 10 times an hour suddenly appears 10,000 timestrend detection (spike against baseline, LSH on n-grams)
a government flags accounts posting negative views on its policyethical risk: suppressing dissent
check whether a hashtag spike is real celebration or a bot campaignsentiment breakdown of the spike
Calculation

P1 slide 12: a phrase goes from 10/hour to 10,000/hour.

Spike ratio = current / baseline = 10,000 / 10 = 1,000×, so it is trending.

VADER on three posts: 0.62, 0.03, −0.40 → positive, neutral, negative.

Step by step

  1. Given baseline 10/hour and current 10,000/hour -> ratio = 10,000 / 10 = 1,000×
  2. Given a compound of 0.62 -> 0.62 > 0.05, positive
  3. Given a compound of 0.03 -> between −0.05 and 0.05, neutral
  4. Given a compound of −0.40 -> < −0.05, negative

Variants

  • #QurbaniEid spike 1,200× on a baseline of 50 posts/hour: peak = 50 × 1,200 = 60,000 posts/hour.
  • 20/hour to 6,000/hour: 6,000 / 20 = 300×.
  • Compound exactly 0.05: not greater than 0.05, so neutral. −0.06: negative.
Traps
  • The cut-offs are strict: 0.05 itself is neutral.
  • Trending depends on the ratio to baseline, not on absolute volume.
  • VADER is rule-based, not a deep-learning classifier.
  • LSH here is for detecting trends, not for sentiment.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
VADER is a lexicon-and-rule-based sentiment analyser for social media that outputs a compound score in [−1, +1].
3 marks
VADER scores each post using a word list plus rules for capitals, punctuation, degree words, negation and emojis. Compound above 0.05 is positive and below −0.05 is negative. Example: 'NOT good!!!' scores more negative than 'not good'.
5 marks

Sentiment analysis scores text as positive, neutral or negative. VADER uses a lexicon and rules (capitals, !!!, very, not, emojis) and gives a compound score in [−1, +1] with ±0.05 cut-offs. In Spark it runs as a UDF, then scores are aggregated by window and topic and sent to Grafana. Trend detection uses LSH on character n-grams and flags a phrase whose rate jumps against its baseline: 10 to 10,000 per hour is 1,000×. Example: #QurbaniEid spiked 1,200× and was 92% positive, so it was organic. Limitation: it can be used to flag critics, so transparency and purpose limitation are required.

Time series, ARIMA and Prophet at scale

Facts
  • Time series properties: order matters, autocorrelation, seasonality, trend, non-stationarity (mean and variance change over time).
  • ARIMA(p, d, q): AR uses past p values, I differences d times for stationarity, MA uses past q forecast errors.
  • Prophet (Taylor & Letham 2018): y(t) = g(t) + s(t) + h(t) + ε(t): trend, seasonality, holidays, noise.
  • g(t) is piecewise linear or logistic with automatic changepoints; s(t) is a Fourier series, P = 365.25 (yearly) or 7 (weekly).
  • changepoint_prior_scale sets trend flexibility: 0.05 default (stiff), 0.5 flexible. Default uncertainty band is 80%.
  • Spark: groupBy(sku_id).applyInPandas fits 5,000 Prophet models in parallel: 8 hours to about 12 minutes on 20 executors (40×).
How it works
  1. Load all SKU series into one Spark DataFrame, one row per day per SKU
  2. Group by sku_id, so each group is one independent series
  3. applyInPandas sends each group to one task as a Pandas DataFrame
  4. Inside the function, fit Prophet on columns ds and y and predict 90 days ahead
  5. All 5,000 models fit in parallel across the cluster
  6. Write forecasts to Hive/S3 as Parquet and serve them to the dashboard
Why · pros · limits
  • Shuffling a time series destroys trend and seasonality, so standard i.i.d. regression is invalid.
  • ARIMA needs stationarity, struggles with several seasonal cycles and needs an expert to pick p, d, q, so it cannot be hand-fitted for 10,000 series.
  • Prophet's parts are readable, so analysts (not statisticians) can add holidays and adjust trend flexibility.
  • A sparse prior on the slope changes δ keeps most changepoints near zero, which avoids overfitting.
  • Each series is fitted independently, so the job is embarrassingly parallel in Spark.
Compare
ARIMAProphetLSTM
KindClassical statisticsAdditive decomposable modelNeural network for sequences
InputMust be stationaryHandles trend and changepointsNeeds lots of data
SeasonalityStruggles with severalSeveral at once (Fourier)Learned implicitly
HolidaysNot built inh(t) termMust be fed as features
TuningExpert picks p, d, qAutomatic, readable knobsHeavy, black box
Best forShort stationary series, small dataThousands of business seriesLong complex series with GPU budget
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
forecast thousands of store-product series with Eid and Puja effectsProphet with a holiday list, parallel via applyInPandas
one short, stable series with no strong seasonalityARIMA
the model misses a sudden wave, a break in the trendtrend g(t) changepoints; raise changepoint_prior_scale
5,000 models take 8 hours on one machineSpark groupBy + applyInPandas (40× speedup)
rows are shuffled before training and the pattern disappearsorder matters / autocorrelation
Calculation

P1 slide 19: 5,000 SKUs, 8 hours sequential, about 12 minutes on 20 executors.

QuantityWorkingResult
Speedup480 min / 12 min40×
Time per model28,800 s / 5,0005.76 s
Models per executor5,000 / 20250

Prophet sum: Eid week with g = 100, s = +15, h = +30, ε = −2 gives y = 143.

Step by step

  1. Given 8 hours -> 8 × 60 = 480 minutes
  2. Given 480 min sequential and 12 min parallel -> speedup = 480 / 12 = 40×
  3. Given 8 h = 28,800 s and 5,000 models -> 5.76 s per model
  4. Given 5,000 models and 20 executors -> 250 models per executor
  5. Given g = 100, s = 15, h = 30, ε = −2 -> y = 143

Variants

  • 10,000 SKUs at 5.76 s, 40 models at once: sequential 57,600 s = 16 h; parallel 1,440 s = 24 min; speedup still 40×.
  • 400 wards at 2 s each, 20 in parallel: 800 s sequential, 40 s parallel, 20×.
  • Non-holiday week, y = 120, g = 110, ε = 0: s = +10.
  • Changepoint at t = 10: k = 0.5, m = 1, δ = 0.3, γ = −3. g(10) = 6 both sides, g(20) = 0.8 × 20 + 1 − 3 = 14.
Traps
  • A sudden wave is a trend changepoint, not seasonality; h(t) only covers dates you list in advance.
  • Weekly seasonality on weekly data learns nothing: every point is the same weekday.
  • Prophet's default interval is 80%; set interval_width=0.95 for 95%.
  • ARIMA's d is the number of differences, not the number of seasons.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Prophet is a time series forecasting model that splits a series into trend, seasonality, holidays and noise: y(t) = g(t) + s(t) + h(t) + ε(t).
3 marks
ARIMA(p, d, q) predicts from past values and past errors after differencing to stationarity, and needs an expert to tune it. Prophet adds a changepoint trend, Fourier seasonality and a holiday term, and tunes itself, so it scales to thousands of series. Example: 5,000 SKU forecasts drop from 8 hours to 12 minutes with applyInPandas.
5 marks

Time series data is ordered and autocorrelated, with trend and seasonality. ARIMA(p, d, q) needs stationary input and expert tuning, so it fails at 10,000 series. Prophet fits y(t) = g(t) + s(t) + h(t) + ε(t): piecewise trend with automatic changepoints, Fourier seasonality (P = 365.25, 7), user-listed holidays. At scale: group by sku_id, applyInPandas fits one Prophet per group in parallel, write Parquet to Hive/S3. Example: 5,000 SKUs, 8 h to 12 min on 20 executors, 40×. Limitation: with the default changepoint_prior_scale of 0.05 the trend is stiff and can miss a sudden break.

  5. All fit in parallel.

Anomaly detection: STL and Isolation Forest

Facts
  • Anomaly: a data point that does not conform to the expected pattern (outlier, change point or contextual deviation).
  • STL: Seasonal-Trend decomposition using LOESS. Splits a series into trend, seasonal and residual.
  • STL rule: flag a point if |residual| > k × IQR of the residuals (IQR = Q3 − Q1).
  • Isolation Forest: an ensemble of random trees; an anomaly needs fewer splits to isolate, so its path is shorter.
  • Isolation Forest assumes no data distribution and scales to millions of points in Spark MLlib.
  • Slide example: bKash agent jumps from 10 to 10,000 transactions per hour, flagged as possible money laundering.
How it works
  1. STL: decompose the series into trend, seasonal and residual parts
  2. Compute Q1, Q3 and IQR of the residuals
  3. Flag points with |residual| above k × IQR
  4. Isolation Forest: build many random trees, each splitting on a random feature at a random value
  5. Measure how many splits each point needs to be isolated
  6. Short average path length means anomaly
Why · pros · limits
  • Removing trend and seasonality first stops a normal winter peak from being flagged.
  • IQR ignores extreme values, so one huge spike does not hide itself by inflating the threshold.
  • STL is interpretable and works well when seasonality is regular.
  • Isolation Forest needs no distribution assumption and handles many features, such as per-agent volumes.
  • Odd points sit alone, so random cuts separate them quickly.
Compare
STLIsolation Forest
IdeaLarge leftover residualEasy to isolate (short path)
NeedsRegular seasonality in one seriesNo distribution assumption
StrengthInterpretableScales to millions in Spark MLlib
Slide useSeasonal seriesbKash agent volumes
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
an agent's hourly transactions jump from 10 to 10,000Isolation Forest anomaly (possible money laundering)
flag days whose value is far off after removing the weekly and yearly cycleSTL residual with k × IQR
a wearable streams heart rate per second and a sudden drop must raise an alertstreaming anomaly detection on time series
Calculation

Residuals: 0.2, −0.1, 0.3, −0.4, 0.1, 5.0, −0.2, 0.0; k = 3.

  1. Sorted: −0.4, −0.2, −0.1, 0.0, 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 5.0
  2. Q1 = −0.125, Q3 = 0.225 (inclusive interpolation)
  3. IQR = 0.35; threshold = 3 × 0.35 = 1.05
  4. Only |5.0| > 1.05: the 6th point is the anomaly

Step by step

  1. Given the residuals -> sort them
  2. Given the sorted list -> Q1 = −0.125, Q3 = 0.225
  3. Given Q1 and Q3 -> IQR = 0.225 − (−0.125) = 0.35
  4. Given IQR 0.35 and k = 3 -> threshold 1.05
  5. Given threshold 1.05 -> compare each |residual|; only 5.0 exceeds it

Variants

  • Same data, k = 1.5: threshold 0.525; largest normal |−0.4| = 0.4, so still only 5.0 is flagged.
  • IQR = 2 and k = 3: threshold 6, a residual of −7 is flagged (absolute value).
Traps
  • Use the absolute value of the residual: big negative residuals are anomalies too.
  • The threshold uses the IQR of residuals, not of the raw series.
  • In Isolation Forest, anomalies have shorter paths, not longer.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
An anomaly is a data point that does not conform to the expected pattern of the series.
3 marks
STL splits the series into trend, seasonal and residual, then flags points whose |residual| exceeds k × IQR. Isolation Forest builds random trees and flags points that need few splits to isolate. Example: a bKash agent jumping from 10 to 10,000 transactions an hour.
5 marks

An anomaly does not fit the expected pattern. STL (Seasonal-Trend decomposition using LOESS): decompose into trend, seasonal, residual; compute the residuals' IQR; flag |residual| > k × IQR. Example: residuals with IQR 0.35 and k = 3 give threshold 1.05, so a residual of 5.0 is flagged. Isolation Forest: random trees cut the feature space; anomalies are isolated in fewer splits. It assumes no distribution and scales in Spark MLlib, as in bKash's per-agent monitoring. Limitation: STL needs regular seasonality, and k is a choice the slide leaves open.

Explain the steps
Describe STL anomaly detection.
show answer
  1. Decompose into trend, seasonal, residual.
  2. Find Q1, Q3, IQR of residuals.
  3. Threshold = k × IQR.
  4. Flag |residual| above it.
Why / justify
Why remove trend and seasonality before looking for anomalies?
show answer
Otherwise a normal seasonal peak (every winter) looks extreme. The residual shows only what the pattern cannot explain.
Distinguish
Distinguish STL from Isolation Forest.
show answer
STL: one seasonal series, flags large residuals, interpretable. Isolation Forest: random trees on many features, flags short-path points, no distribution assumption, scales in Spark MLlib.
Scenario
bKash monitors hourly transactions per agent. One agent goes from 10 to 10,000.
i) Which method does the slide use? ii) Why is this point easy for it? iii) What is the spike ratio? iv) What might it indicate?
show answer
i) Isolation Forest. ii) It sits far from all others, so few splits isolate it. iii) 10,000 / 10 = 1,000×. iv) Possible money laundering.
One-step calc
Residual IQR is 0.35 and k = 3. What is the threshold?
show answer
3 × 0.35 = 1.05.
Full calc
Residuals 0.2, −0.1, 0.3, −0.4, 0.1, 5.0, −0.2, 0.0. With k = 1.5, which points are anomalies?
show answer
Q1 = −0.125, Q3 = 0.225, IQR = 0.35, threshold 0.525. Only 5.0 is flagged.
Long answer
Explain two methods for anomaly detection in big time series data, with an example.
show answer
Use the 5-mark answer: definition, STL steps and the worked threshold, Isolation Forest idea and bKash example, limitations.

Healthcare big data, privacy and AlphaFold

Facts
  • Four modalities: EHR (ICD-10, notes, FHIR), genomics (≈200 GB per genome; 10,000 patients = 2 PB), wearables (per-second), imaging (DICOM, 200 MB-2 GB, unstructured).
  • EHR ML beats physician rules: ICU mortality (random forest, AUROC > 0.90), 30-day readmission (20% fewer), sepsis 6 hours earlier. Needs 1M+ records.
  • HIPAA (USA): de-identify 18 PHI identifiers before research; consent. GDPR (EU): minimisation, purpose limitation, right to erasure.
  • PDPA Bangladesh: under development, GDPR-based; NID-linked records need explicit consent.
  • Protection: safe harbour, expert determination, differential privacy (calibrated noise on aggregates), federated learning (train at each hospital, data stays).
  • AlphaFold 2: 3D protein structure from amino-acid sequence; 170,000 PDB structures, MSAs over 250M UniProt sequences; 200M structures released 2022; 2024 Nobel.
How it works
  1. EHR arrives as FHIR resources, genomics as Parquet, wearables through Kafka, images as DICOM
  2. All land in a health data lake on HDFS/S3 with a Hive Metastore catalogue
  3. De-identify records before research use (remove the 18 identifiers)
  4. Train clinical ML models: readmission, mortality, imaging CNNs, variant calling
  5. Where records cannot move, train with federated learning; publish aggregates with differential privacy
Why · pros · limits
  • Images are binary DICOM files, so they need deep learning (CNN, ViT), not SQL.
  • Reliable clinical models need 1M+ patient records, which forces pooling data across hospitals.
  • Federated learning gets that scale without moving raw records, which consent and law forbid.
  • Small-area counts can identify one patient; calibrated noise blocks that inference.
  • AlphaFold is a big data problem because each query searches 250M sequences to build its MSA.
Compare
What it doesProtects against
Safe harbourRemoves 18 named identifiersDirect identification
Expert determinationStatistician certifies low re-identification riskIndirect identification
Differential privacyAdds calibrated noise to aggregatesInferring one person from statistics
Federated learningTrains at each site, shares only model updatesMoving raw records between hospitals
ScopeKey rule
HIPAAUS health dataRemove 18 PHI identifiers; consent
GDPREU personal dataMinimisation, purpose limitation, erasure
PDPABangladesh, draftExplicit consent for NID-linked records
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
five hospitals want one sepsis model but cannot share patient filesfederated learning
a health ministry publishes district counts of a rare diseasedifferential privacy
strip names, dates of birth, NIDs and phone numbers before researchde-identification (safe harbour)
predict a protein's 3D shape from its amino-acid sequenceAlphaFold
CT and MRI scans of 1 GB eachunstructured DICOM imaging, analysed with CNN/ViT
Calculation

P1 slide 21: 10,000 patients × 200 GB per genome = 2,000,000 GB = 2 PB (1 PB = 1,000,000 GB).

Step by step

  1. Given 200 GB per genome and 10,000 patients -> 2,000,000 GB
  2. Given 2,000,000 GB -> divide by 1,000,000 = 2 PB
  3. Given 2 PB and HDFS replication 3 -> 6 PB of raw disk

Variants

  • 2,500 patients with replication 3: 2,500 × 200 = 500 TB; × 3 = 1.5 PB.
  • 5,000 patients: 5,000 × 200 = 1,000,000 GB = 1 PB.
Traps
  • Stored in a file does not mean structured: DICOM images are unstructured.
  • HIPAA is for health data; US financial data falls under GLBA.
  • Federated learning moves the model, not the data; differential privacy adds noise, it does not encrypt.
  • AlphaFold predicts structure from sequence; it is not anomaly or fraud detection.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Federated learning trains a model at each hospital and shares only model updates, so raw patient records never leave the site.
3 marks
Healthcare big data comes as EHR, genomics, wearables and DICOM images, all about identifiable patients. HIPAA requires removing 18 identifiers, and differential privacy and federated learning protect data used for models. Example: 10,000 genomes at 200 GB each need 2 PB.
5 marks

Sources: EHR (FHIR), genomics (200 GB each), wearables (per-second streams), DICOM images (200 MB to 2 GB, unstructured). They meet in a data lake on HDFS/S3 with a Hive Metastore. Uses: ICU mortality (AUROC > 0.90), readmission (−20%), sepsis 6 h earlier; needs 1M+ records. Privacy: HIPAA 18 identifiers, GDPR, PDPA consent for NID records; safe harbour, expert determination, differential privacy, federated learning. Science: AlphaFold predicts 3D protein shape from 170,000 structures and 250M sequences. Limitation: records are split across hospitals and laws, so integration is the hard part.

show answer

show answer
Each query builds an MSA by searching 250M UniProt sequences (>200 GB), and training used 170,000 PDB structures on 128 TPU v3 cores for weeks.

Syndromic surveillance and the Google Flu Trends lesson

Facts
  • Physician-reported cases reach CDC 1 to 2 weeks after symptoms (ILINet: >3,000 sentinel physicians, weekly).
  • Syndromic surveillance uses pre-diagnosis signals: posts peak 7 to 10 days before ER visits; pharmacy antipyretic sales spike 3 to 5 days before confirmation.
  • Other signals: ER chief complaints via NLP; search volume (1-week lead).
  • BioSense: near real-time data from 3,500+ emergency departments; syndromes extracted automatically.
  • Google Flu Trends: r² = 0.97 on 2003-2008 data, then over-predicted flu by 2× in 2012-2013 and was discontinued.
  • Post-GFT: BioSense ED visits as the main signal, Prophet for seasonality and season-start changepoints, Twitter as a covariate, ensembles (FluSight).
  • Twitter adds lead time, not accuracy: its signal arrives about 2 weeks before ILINet but with about 30% higher error (latency vs precision).
How it works
  1. Sources: Twitter (NLP), pharmacy sales, ER chief complaints, search trends
  2. All publish events to a Kafka event bus
  3. Spark Streaming runs NLP and aggregates counts per region and time window
  4. An alert engine fires when a count breaches its threshold
  5. A Grafana dashboard shows the signals to health officers
  6. Officers confirm against clinical reporting before acting
Why · pros · limits
  • Outbreaks grow exponentially, so a 1 to 2 week reporting lag is costly.
  • Proxy data measures behaviour (health anxiety, media coverage), not incidence, so it drifts.
  • GFT failed partly because Google's own autocomplete changes, opaque to CDC, shifted the features.
  • Prophet's g + s + h parts let epidemiologists inspect each piece, unlike black-box models.
  • High in-sample r² does not guarantee out-of-sample accuracy when behaviour changes.
Compare
Traditional (ILINet)Syndromic
SourcePhysician-reported casesPosts, pharmacy, ER notes, searches
Speed1 to 2 weeks behindDays ahead
AccuracyGround truthNoisy proxy
RoleMain recordEarly warning, a supplement
Spot it in a scenario
If the question describes…Answer
paracetamol sales jump across a district before hospitals report casessyndromic surveillance (pharmacy signal, 3-5 days ahead)
a search-based model fit past years well but doubled the real flu rateGoogle Flu Trends lesson: supplement, not replace
media coverage drives searches up while cases stay lowproxy data amplifies noise
combine ED visits as the main input with tweets as an extra regressorpost-GFT Prophet nowcasting with a covariate
Traps
  • GFT did not replace CDC surveillance; it failed and was discontinued.
  • Syndromic signals are early warnings, not ground truth.
  • Twitter enters the post-GFT model as a covariate, not as the main signal.
  • r² = 0.97 was in-sample; it says nothing about future seasons.
Model answers at 1, 3 and 5 marks
1 mark
Syndromic surveillance uses non-traditional signals, such as posts, pharmacy sales and ER notes, that show illness before a diagnosis is reported.
3 marks
Sources stream through Kafka into Spark Streaming, which aggregates them and triggers an alert engine and a Grafana dashboard. The signals lead clinical reports by days. Google Flu Trends over-predicted by 2× in 2012-2013, so these signals must supplement clinical reporting, not replace it.
5 marks

Problem: physician reports reach CDC 1 to 2 weeks late. Syndromic surveillance uses earlier signals: posts (7-10 days before ER visits), pharmacy antipyretic sales (3-5 days), ER chief complaints, searches. Pipeline: sources → Kafka → Spark Streaming (NLP, aggregation) → alert engine + Grafana. Example: Google Flu Trends had r² = 0.97 in-sample but over-predicted by 2× in 2012-2013 because search behaviour changed. CDC now uses BioSense ED visits with Prophet and Twitter as a covariate. Limitation: proxy data reflects anxiety and media, so it supplements clinical reporting and never replaces it.

PracticeMock final paper

One 10-mark question per chapter, in the shape of the October 2026 Data Mining final from the same department: a short scenario, then short parts. Answer either the main pair or the OR pair. Click a part for its answer.

Q1 · Week 5 10 marks

a · 4 marks
A bank keeps 20 TB of transaction logs with 50 columns as gzip-compressed CSV on HDFS. A fraud report reads 5 columns.
i) Why does each file get only one Mapper? ii) Which format and codec should replace it? iii) How much data would the report read after the switch? iv) What in the new format lets it skip whole blocks of rows?
show answer
i) Gzip is not splittable, so reading must start at the file's beginning. ii) Parquet + Snappy (or ORC for Hive). iii) 20 TB × 5/50 = 2 TB, a 90% cut. iv) Min/max stats per column chunk in the footer: predicate pushdown.
b · 6 marks
A cluster of 1,000 nodes, each 128 GB RAM and 32 vCPU, runs YARN with queues analytics 50%, streaming 30%, adhoc 20%. Analytics runs Spark executors {8 GB, 4 vCPU}.
i) Which YARN component grants the containers? ii) Find the maximum executors in the analytics queue and the bottleneck. iii) A node running 6 executors dies: who detects it and who asks for replacements? iv) Which scheduler fits these three teams, and why?
show answer
i) The ResourceManager's Scheduler. ii) 500 nodes; RAM 64,000 GB / 8 = 8,000; vCPU 16,000 / 4 = 4,000; max 4,000, CPU bottleneck. iii) The RM detects missed heartbeats (>10 min) and marks the 6 containers failed; the Spark ApplicationMaster requests 6 replacements. iv) Capacity: each team gets a guaranteed share and can borrow idle capacity.
OR
a · 4 marks
A company runs a 5,000-node Hadoop 1.x cluster and wants to add Spark jobs.
i) Name two limits of the JobTracker. ii) What did Hadoop 2.0 split it into? iii) How many ApplicationMasters run for 12 active jobs? iv) What is a container?
show answer
i) MapReduce only; about 4,000-node ceiling (5,000 is past it). ii) A global ResourceManager and a per-job ApplicationMaster. iii) 12, one per job. iv) A slice of one node: set vcores plus set RAM, in which a task runs.
b · 6 marks
A ride-sharing app streams trip events through Kafka; the schema gains a field each month. Each night a Spark job builds reports; 10 GB of the day's data is written per night.
i) Which format at ingest, and why? ii) What happens when a new reader opens an old file missing the new field? iii) Which format and codec for the nightly analytics copy? iv) How many Mappers can read the 10 GB night file with 128 MB row groups, and how many if it were one gzip CSV?
show answer
i) Avro: row-oriented and append-friendly, with schema evolution. ii) The missing field gets its default value from the reader schema. iii) Parquet + Snappy. iv) 10,240 / 128 = 80 Mappers; gzip CSV: 1.

Q2 · Week 6 10 marks

a · 4 marks
A ride-sharing firm runs a PySpark job on its Hadoop cluster: textFile → filter(fare > 0) → map(to (zone, fare)) → reduceByKey(sum) → saveAsTextFile.
i) Which component builds the DAG and schedules tasks? ii) How many stages run, and where is the boundary? iii) Which call starts the computation, and why? iv) Why is reduceByKey better here than groupByKey?
show answer
i) The Driver (YARN is the Cluster Manager, Executors run the tasks). ii) Two: textFile → filter → map are narrow and pipelined in Stage 1; reduceByKey is wide, so the shuffle starts Stage 2. iii) saveAsTextFile, the only action; transformations are lazy. iv) It sums fares per zone inside each partition before the shuffle, so far fewer records cross the network.
b · 6 marks
The firm trains a demand model with 20 iterations over 40 GB. HDFS reads and writes at 400 MB/s; RAM cache reads at 4,000 MB/s.
i) Total HDFS I/O for MapReduce. ii) Spark's HDFS I/O and the reduction factor. iii) Wall-clock time for each. iv) The speedup, and one case where Spark would not win.
show answer
i) 20 × (40 + 40) = 1,600 GB = 1.6 TB. ii) 40 GB (read once); 1,600 / 40 = 40× less. iii) HDFS pass = 40,000 / 400 = 100 s, so T_MR = 20 × 200 = 4,000 s ≈ 66.7 min. RAM pass = 40,000 / 4,000 = 10 s, so T_Spark = 100 + 19 × 10 = 290 s ≈ 4.8 min. iv) 4,000 / 290 ≈ 13.8×. Spark would not win on a single-pass job, or if the 40 GB did not fit in cluster RAM.
OR
a · 4 marks
A job runs input → filter → map over 8 partitions and caches the result. The Executor holding partition 4 crashes.
i) What does “resilient” mean for this RDD? ii) List the recovery steps. iii) What fraction of the work is redone? iv) What changes if the next step is groupByKey?
show answer
i) Lost partitions are recomputed from lineage, not read from a replica. ii) Detect the lost partition → trace lineage to the input → re-read input partition 4 from HDFS → re-apply filter then map. iii) 1 / 8 = 12.5%. iv) groupByKey is wide: a lost output partition needs data from all 8 parent partitions, so recovery can rerun the whole earlier stage.
b · 6 marks
A word count runs on 6 partitions. Each partition holds 2,000 (word, 1) pairs with 40 distinct words.
i) Records shuffled by groupByKey. ii) Maximum records shuffled by reduceByKey. iii) Percentage reduction. iv) With numPartitions = 5, which partitions do keys with hash 23 and 40 go to, and is this step narrow or wide?
show answer
i) 6 × 2,000 = 12,000. ii) 6 × 40 = 240. iii) 1 − 240 / 12,000 = 0.98 → 98% fewer records. iv) 23 % 5 = 3 and 40 % 5 = 0, so partitions 3 and 0. Hash partitioning for a shuffle is a wide dependency.

Q3 · Week 7 10 marks

a · 4 marks
A food-delivery company stores 4 TB of orders in one Hive table. Managers run a daily report filtered by order_date and city, and analysts join orders with a 15 M-row riders table on rider_id. Today every report scans the whole table.
i) Which feature lets the daily report read only one folder? ii) Name the columns you would use for it. iii) Why not use rider_id for that feature? iv) What should be done to rider_id instead?
show answer
i) Partitioning (partition pruning). ii) order_date and city. iii) High cardinality: millions of directories would overload the NameNode. iv) Bucket both tables on rider_id with the same number of buckets for a bucket map join.
b · 6 marks
For the table in (a): i) The table is partitioned by 730 days and 20 cities. What fraction does a one-day, one-city report read? ii) With 4 TB and 8 buckets per partition, what is the size of each bucket file? iii) Which bucket holds rider_id 1,000,003? iv) Write the CREATE TABLE line that declares the partitions and buckets.
show answer
i) P = 730 × 20 = 14,600; 1 / 14,600 ≈ 0.0068%. ii) 4,000 GB / (14,600 × 8) = 4,000 / 116,800 ≈ 34 MB (small; fewer buckets, e.g. 2, give ≈ 137 MB). iii) 1,000,003 % 8 = 3. iv) PARTITIONED BY (order_date STRING, city STRING) CLUSTERED BY (rider_id) INTO 8 BUCKETS STORED AS ORC.
OR
a · 4 marks
A telecom's analysts send HiveQL from Tableau. A query SELECT region, COUNT(*) FROM calls GROUP BY region takes 9 minutes.
i) Which component receives the query? ii) Where does Hive find the table's HDFS path? iii) What does the map phase emit? iv) Why is it slow on MapReduce?
show answer
i) HiveServer2. ii) The Metastore. iii) (region, 1), or partial counts per region with map-side aggregation. iv) MapReduce writes intermediate results to HDFS between stages.
b · 6 marks
i) The calls table has 60 million rows and 300 regions; one mapper uses map-side aggregation. By what factor does the shuffle shrink? ii) The team moves the dashboard to Presto and the query drops from 9 minutes to 27 seconds. Find the speed-up. iii) Give two Presto design decisions behind it. iv) Does the team need to redefine its tables for Presto? Why?
show answer
i) 60,000,000 / 300 = 200,000×. ii) 540 s / 27 s = 20×. iii) No intermediate disk writes (in-memory pipeline); vectorized execution on 1,024-row batches (also: cost-based optimizer). iv) No: Presto reads the same Hive Metastore.

Q4 · Week 8 10 marks

a · 4 marks
A ride-sharing firm streams driver GPS pings every 3 seconds to a Kafka topic with 10 partitions, keyed by driver_id, replication factor 3. The routing engine and the billing service both read the topic.
i) Why key by driver_id? ii) How do routing and billing each get every ping? iii) How many broker failures can the topic survive? iv) Routing scales to 14 consumers in one group. How many sit idle?
show answer
i) Same key → same partition, so each driver's pings stay in order. ii) Each is its own consumer group. iii) R − 1 = 2. iv) 14 − 10 = 4 idle.
b · 6 marks
The same firm loads its 5 M-row trips table from MySQL into HDFS nightly and also wants a fraud check on payments within 1 second. Name the tool for each, choose Lambda or Kappa for the whole system, and give one weakness of your choice.
show answer
Trips table: Sqoop (incremental import by last-value, parallel map tasks, retry on failure). Payments: Kafka topic + Spark Streaming (milliseconds, RF for fault tolerance, replay from offset). Architecture: Lambda, because a real nightly batch path exists (batch = Sqoop + HDFS + Hive; speed = Kafka + Spark Streaming; serving merges). Weakness: two code paths for shared logic, 30–40% of effort to keep in sync; reduce it by making Kafka the single source both layers read.
OR
a · 4 marks
A hospital stores five years of lab CSVs, scanned reports and device logs in S3 with no catalogue. Analysts find four versions of the same table, and patient IDs are open to every user.
i) Lake or warehouse? ii) What has it become? iii) Name the layer that is missing. iv) Give two fixes.
show answer
i) Data lake. ii) A data swamp. iii) Catalogue/Governance layer. iv) Catalogue with an owner per dataset; access control on PII (also quality checks, Iceberg/Delta Lake versioning).
b · 6 marks
The hospital's team runs Lambda: nightly Spark jobs plus a Flink stream for ICU alerts. The CTO wants Kappa. State what must be true of the stream engine, one risk of migrating, and one reason Kappa may not suit them. Show how Kappa reprocesses history.
show answer
Must be true: the engine replays history about as fast as it handles live data, and full history is kept in a replayable log (Kafka long retention or Iceberg on S3). Risk: replaying TB+ of records may take days, and all batch jobs must be rewritten. Not suitable if: health regulators require a separate batch reconciliation, or batch SQL is preferred. Reprocessing: deploy new job version → start at offset 0 → catch up to live → swap in, retire the old job.

Q5 · Week 9 10 marks

a · 4 marks
A ride-hailing company in Chattogram shows a live map of 2 million driver GPS pings. The scatter is a solid blob. A filter on the 80-billion-row trip table in Hive takes minutes.
i) Name the problem with the map. ii) Which fix shows the count of pings per area? iii) Name the problem with the filter. iv) Which fix gives sub-second filters, and its trade-off?
show answer
i) Overplotting. ii) Hexbin aggregation. iii) Latency. iv) A pre-aggregated data cube; data can be up to 24 h stale.
b · 6 marks
The same company's report shows trips per district as 3-D bars with a gradient, a one-series legend, and a y-axis starting at 900. Trips rose from 1,000 to 1,050, but the bar grew from 100 px to 150 px.
i) Name two chartjunk elements. ii) Compute the effect in the data. iii) Compute the effect shown. iv) Compute the lie factor and say what it means.
show answer
i) 3-D bars, gradient fill (also the single-series legend). ii) (1,050 − 1,000) ÷ 1,000 = 5%. iii) (150 − 100) ÷ 100 = 50%. iv) 50 ÷ 5 = 10: the chart overstates the rise ten times; start y at 0.
OR

A news site builds a live election dashboard. It opens on a national map, has a party dropdown, and shows a constituency's full result on click.

Votes arrive at 2,000 events per second through Kafka. Results must refresh in under a second on the public page, and every 5 seconds on the operations screen.

Q6 · Week 10 10 marks

a · 4 marks
Dhaka's health office streams Bangla Facebook posts and pharmacy paracetamol sales into a cluster to catch dengue early.
i) Which tool should carry both event streams? (1)
ii) A dengue phrase rises from 20 to 6,000 posts an hour. What is the spike ratio? (1)
iii) A post scores a VADER compound of 0.04. Which class? (1)
iv) Why must this system not replace DGHS reports? (1)
show answer
i) Kafka. ii) 6,000 / 20 = 300×. iii) Neutral (between −0.05 and 0.05). iv) Proxy signals reflect behaviour, not cases; Google Flu Trends over-predicted by 2×. They supplement clinical reporting.
b · 6 marks
A hospital group forecasts weekly admissions for 400 wards with Prophet.
i) Write the Prophet model and name each term. (2)
ii) In an Eid week, g = 80, s = +6, h = +12, ε = 0. Forecast? (1)
iii) Each ward's model takes 2 s; Spark fits 20 at a time. Sequential time, parallel time? (1)
iv) The model missed a sudden COVID wave. Which term should catch it, and which hyperparameter controls that? (2)
show answer
i) y(t) = g(t) + s(t) + h(t) + ε(t): trend, seasonality, holidays, noise. ii) 80 + 6 + 12 + 0 = 98. iii) 400 × 2 = 800 s; 800 / 20 = 40 s (20×). iv) Trend g(t) via changepoints; changepoint_prior_scale (raise from 0.05 to bend faster).
OR
a · 4 marks
DGHS wants one sepsis model from 30 hospitals and a public dashboard of cases by upazila.
i) How can it train without pooling records? (1)
ii) How can it stop small counts revealing a patient? (1)
iii) Which US law requires removing 18 identifiers? (1)
iv) A linked genomics study has 5,000 patients at 200 GB each. Storage in PB? (1)
show answer
i) Federated learning. ii) Differential privacy. iii) HIPAA. iv) 5,000 × 200 = 1,000,000 GB = 1 PB.
b · 6 marks
A researcher studies a misinformation cascade on X.
i) Give the steps of Louvain for finding the groups that spread it. (2)
ii) Average out-degree is 40. Upper bound on users reached in 2 hops? (1)
iii) Why is that an overestimate? (1)
iv) Kwak et al. found 22.1% reciprocity. What does that say about Twitter compared with Facebook? (2)
show answer
i) Each node alone; move nodes to the neighbour community with the biggest modularity gain; collapse to super-nodes; repeat until no gain. ii) 40 + 1,600 = 1,640. iii) Followers overlap (clustering), so people are counted twice, and not everyone reshares. iv) Most follows are one-way, so Twitter is a directed news medium; Facebook friendship is mutual (≈100%), a social network.

Last ten minutesFormula and fact sheet

Formulas

WhatFormulaNote
W5 · Columnar data readRead = D × k / ND table size, k columns selected, N total columns
W5 · Columnar I/O reductionReduction = 1 − k / N; speedup ≈ N / k10 TB, 3 of 100: 97%, 33×
W5 · Pushdown savingSpeedup = D / RR = bytes actually read; 10 TB / 50 GB = 200×
W5 · Mappers per fileSplits = ⌈F / B⌉, or 1 if gzipB = block or row group, about 128 MB
W5 · Containers per queuemin(nodes·f·RAM / c_ram, nodes·f·CPU / c_cpu)smaller term is the bottleneck
W5 · Nodes for a job⌈T / min(RAM / c_ram, CPU / c_cpu)⌉T containers needed at once
W5 · Extra work from utilisationU_new / U_old − 178/45 − 1 = 73%
W6 · MapReduce iterative disk I/OMR_IO = k × (D_read + D_write)k iterations; slide: 100 × (50 + 50) GB = 10 TB
W6 · Spark HDFS I/OSpark_IO = D (read once)later iterations read RAM, not HDFS
W6 · Transfer timet = size / speed50,000 MB / 500 MB/s = 100 s
W6 · Iterative wall-clock timeT_MR = k × (D/R_hdfs + D/W_hdfs); T_Spark = D/R_hdfs + (k − 1) × D/R_ramk − 1 RAM passes, not k
W6 · SpeedupSpeedup = T_old / T_newA1: 20,000 / 1,090 ≈ 18.3×; RDD paper 127 / 0.96 ≈ 132×
W6 · Hash partitionerpartition = key.hashCode() % numPartitions17 % 4 = 1
W6 · Shuffle recordsgroupByKey = all pairs; reduceByKey ≤ partitions × distinct keys per partition4,000 vs 200 records
W6 · Efficiency per node(T_old × N_old) / (T_new × N_new)GraySort (72 × 2,100) / (23 × 206) ≈ 32×
W7 · Map-side aggregationreduction = N_rows / G (with M mappers: N_rows / (M × G))50M rows, 200 countries: 250,000×
W7 · Partition pruningfraction read = P_read / P_totalP_total = product of values per partition column
W7 · Bucket numberbucket = hash(column) % Ninteger key: hash = value; 98 % 4 = 2
W7 · Bucket file sizesize = S / (P × N); files = P × Naim near the 128 MB HDFS block
W7 · ALS model size(users + items) × k × bytes(75M + 30M) × 50 × 4 B = 21 GB
W7 · Speed-upT_old / T_new; saved = (T_old − T_new) / T_old8 h to 2 h: 4×, 75% saved
W7 · Pairs in a playlistL(L − 1) / 220 songs: 190 pairs
W8 · Point-to-point ETL jobsN × (N − 1); bus = N50 systems: 2,450 jobs vs 50 connections
W8 · Partition copiesP × Rbkash-txns: 3 × 2 = 6
W8 · Broker failures survivedR − 1default R = 3 survives 2
W8 · Active consumers in a groupmin(P, C); idle = C − P (only if C > P)one consumer per partition per group
W8 · Messages per secondMB/s × 1,000,000 ÷ message bytes50.5 MB/s, 100 B = 505,000 msgs/s
W8 · SpeedupKafka ÷ other50.5 / 4.3 ≈ 11.7×; 50.5 / 14.7 ≈ 3.4×
W8 · Sequential vs random diskseq ÷ random600 / 100 = 6× (paper figures)
W8 · Zero-copy4 copies → 2 copiesabout 2× throughput, CPU near zero
W8 · Events per secondevents per day ÷ 86,400Netflix 100 B/day ≈ 1.16 M/s
W9 · Data-ink ratioink encoding data ÷ total inkMaximise it; 1 is the ceiling
W9 · Lie factoreffect shown in graphic ÷ effect in data1 truthful; above 1 overstates; below 1 understates
W9 · Hexbin reductionpoints ÷ hex cells10 M ÷ 10,000 = 1,000×
W9 · Alpha stackingopacity = 1 − (1 − α)ⁿα = 0.02: n = 100 → 0.867, saturates

W10 · Prophet modely(t) = g(t) + s(t) + h(t) + ε(t)trend + seasonality + holidays + noise
W10 · Prophet trendg(t) = (k + a(t)ᵀδ) t + (m + a(t)ᵀγ)δ slope changes at changepoints, sparse prior
W10 · Fourier seasonalitys(t) = Σ [aₙ cos(2πnt/P) + bₙ sin(2πnt/P)], n = 1..NP = 365.25 yearly, 7 weekly

W10 · Greedy influence boundspread ≥ (1 − 1/e) × optimal ≈ 0.632 × optimalKempe et al. 2003
W10 · Modularity (words)fraction of edges inside communities − fraction expected by chanceLouvain maximises it
W10 · Power lawP(k) ∝ k^(−α), α ≈ 2.276Twitter follower counts (Kwak 2010)
W10 · Spike ratiocurrent rate / baseline rate10 to 10,000 per hour = 1,000×
